Part II
Equivalent definitions of strongly commutative triples

We review some key results in
[
Ch22+
]
and
[
Ch22+d
]
in this part.

8.
Notations on
\eta
-invariants

8.1.
Notations

Most of time, we shall usually give/prove statements involving right derivatives and/or left integrals. The analogous version switching between left and right can be formulated/proved similarly and will be left to the reader.

For lightening notation, set
D_{\Delta}=D^{R}_{\Delta}
and
I_{\Delta}=I^{L}_{\Delta}
for a segment
\Delta
.

8.2.
\eta
-invariants

Definition 8.1
.

Let
\Delta=[a,b]_{\rho}
be a segment. Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
.

•

Define
\varepsilon_{\Delta}(\pi)=\varepsilon_{\Delta}^{R}(\pi)
(resp.
\varepsilon_{\Delta}^{L}(\pi)
) to be the largest non-negative integer
k
such that
D^{k}_{\Delta}(\pi)\neq 0
(resp.
(D^{L}_{\Delta})^{k}(\pi)\neq 0
). (The power
k
means doing compositions
k
-times.)

•

Define

\eta_{\Delta}(\pi)=\eta^{R}_{\Delta}(\pi)=(\varepsilon_{[a,b]_{\rho}}(\pi),\varepsilon_{[a+1,b]_{\rho}}(\pi),\ldots,\varepsilon_{[b,b]_{\rho}}(\pi));

(resp.\quad\eta_{\Delta}^{L}(\pi)=(\varepsilon^{L}_{[a,b]_{\rho}}(\pi),\varepsilon^{L}_{[a,b-1]_{\rho}}(\pi),\ldots,\varepsilon^{L}_{[a,a]_{\rho}}(\pi))).

•

We may simply write
\eta_{\Delta}(\pi)=0
if
\varepsilon_{[c,b]_{\rho}}(\pi)=0
for all
c=a,\ldots,b
. Similarly, we may write
\eta_{\Delta}(\pi)\neq 0
if
\varepsilon_{[c,b]_{\rho}}(\pi)\neq 0
for some
c=a,\ldots,b
.

•

We write
\eta_{\Delta}(\pi)?\ \eta_{\Delta}(\pi^{\prime})
for
?\in\left\{=,\leq,<,\geq,>\right\}
if
\varepsilon_{[c,b]_{\rho}}(\pi)?\varepsilon_{[c,b]_{\rho}}(\pi^{\prime})
for all
c
satisfying
a\leq c\leq b
.

•

We similarly define the left version terminologies for
\varepsilon^{L}_{\Delta}
and
\eta^{L}_{\Delta}
if one uses the left derivatives
D^{L}_{\Delta}
.

8.3.
Multisegment counterpart of the
\eta
-invariant

Definition 8.2
.

For
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
and a segment
\Delta=[a,b]_{\rho}
, define

\mathfrak{mx}(\pi,\Delta):=\mathfrak{mx}^{R}(\pi,\Delta):=\sum_{k=0}^{b-a}\varepsilon_{[a+k,b]_{\rho}}(\pi)\cdot[a+k,b]_{\rho},

\mathfrak{mx}^{L}(\pi,\Delta):=\sum_{k=0}^{b-a}\varepsilon^{L}_{[a,b-k]_{\rho}}(\pi)\cdot[a,b-k]_{\rho}.

Here the numbers
\varepsilon_{[a+k,b]_{\rho}}(\pi)
and
\varepsilon^{L}_{[a,b-k]_{\rho}}(\pi)
are the multiplicities of those segments.

For
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
and
\rho\in\mathrm{Irr}^{c}
, similarly define:

\mathfrak{mxpt}(\pi,\rho):=\mathfrak{mxpt}^{R}(\pi,\rho):=\sum_{\Delta:b(\Delta)\cong\rho}\varepsilon_{\Delta}(\pi)\cdot\Delta,

\mathfrak{mxpt}^{L}(\pi,\rho)=\sum_{\Delta:a(\Delta)\cong\rho}\varepsilon^{L}_{\Delta}(\pi)\cdot\Delta

We have that
D_{\mathfrak{mx}(\pi,\Delta)}(\pi)
is R-
\Delta
-reduced (see Section
4.4
), and similarly
D_{\mathfrak{mx}^{L}(\pi,\Delta)}^{L}(\pi)
is L-
\Delta
-reduced (i.e.
\eta_{\Delta}^{L}(\pi)=0
). For the details, one sees
[
Ch22+
, Corollary 7.23]
(also see
[
Ch22+b
,
LM22
]
). Moreover, if
\mathfrak{m}
is a
\Delta
-saturated multisegment such that
D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi)\neq 0
, then
\mathfrak{m}
is a submultisegment of
\mathfrak{mx}(\pi,\Delta)
.

8.4.
More notations on multisegments

For
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathrm{Mult}
and
\rho\in\mathrm{Irr}^{c}
, let

\mathfrak{m}_{a=\rho}=\left\{\Delta\in\mathfrak{m}:a(\Delta)\cong\rho\right\},\quad\mathfrak{m}_{b=\rho}=\left\{\Delta\in\mathfrak{m}:b(\Delta)\cong\rho\right\}.

We remark that
\varepsilon_{\Delta}(\pi)
is equal to the cardinality of:

\left\{\widetilde{\Delta}\in\mathfrak{hd}(\pi)_{a=a(\Delta)}:\Delta\subset\widetilde{\Delta}\right\}.

8.5.
Highest derivative multisegments
\mathfrak{hd}

Following results in
[
Ch22+
]
, we shall use the following quick definition of the highest derivative multisegment.

Definition 8.3
.

[
Ch22+
]

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Define the
highest right (resp. left) derivative multisegment
, denoted by
\mathfrak{hd}(\pi):=\mathfrak{hd}^{R}(\pi)
(resp.
\mathfrak{hd}^{L}(\pi)
), of
\pi
to be the unique minimal multisegment such that

D_{\mathfrak{hd}(\pi)}(\pi)\cong\pi^{-},\quad(\mbox{resp. $D_{\mathfrak{hd}^{L}(\pi)}^{L}(\pi)\cong{}^{-}\pi$}).

Definition 8.4
.

[
Ch22+
, Section 7]

Let
\mathfrak{h}\in\mathrm{Mult}
. Let
\Delta=[a,b]_{\rho}
. The
removal process
for
(\Delta,\mathfrak{h})
is an algorithm to carry out the following steps:

(1)

Find the shortest segment
[a,b^{\prime}]_{\rho}
in
\mathfrak{h}
with
b^{\prime}\geq b
. Name the segment to be
\Delta_{1}
.

(2)

We inductively define the shortest segment
\Delta_{i}=[a_{i},b_{i}]_{\rho}
(for
i\geq 2
) in
\mathfrak{h}
satisfying
a_{i-1}<a_{i}
and
b_{i}<b_{i-1}
. The process terminates when no more such segment can be found and we denote
\Delta_{2},\ldots,\Delta_{r}
to be all those segments.

(3)

Define new segments as follows:

•

\Delta^{tr}_{i}=[a_{i+1},b_{i}]_{\rho}
for
i<r

•

\Delta^{tr}_{r}=[b_{r-1}+1,b_{r}]_{\rho}
(possibly empty).

(4)

Define

\mathfrak{r}(\Delta,\mathfrak{h})=\mathfrak{h}-\sum_{i=1}^{r}\Delta_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}\Delta_{i}^{tr}.

We shall call
\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{r}
to be the
removal sequence
for
(\Delta,\mathfrak{h})
.

For a multisegment
\mathfrak{m}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{r}\right\}
written in an ascending order, define

\mathfrak{r}(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{h})=\mathfrak{r}(\Delta_{r},\ldots,\mathfrak{r}(\Delta_{1},\mathfrak{h})\ldots).

It is shown in
[
Ch22+
, Section 7]
that
\mathfrak{r}(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{h})
is independent of a choice of such ordering.

For a segment
\Delta
and a multisegment
\mathfrak{h}
, define
\varepsilon_{\Delta}(\mathfrak{h})
to be the number of segments
\widetilde{\Delta}
in
\mathfrak{h}_{a=a(\Delta)}
with
\widetilde{\Delta}\subset\Delta
.

As shown in
[
Ch22+
]
, the removal process is a simpler process in keep-tracking the effect of derivatives. For a multisegment
\mathfrak{h}
, define

\varepsilon_{[a,b]_{\rho}}(\mathfrak{h})=|\left\{[a,c]_{\rho}\in\mathfrak{h}:b\leq c\right\}|.

In particular, we have:

Theorem 8.5
.

[
Ch22+
, Theorem 7.20]

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
and let
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{hd}(\pi)
. Let
\Delta
be a segment such that
D_{\Delta}(\pi)\neq 0
. Then for any segment
\Delta^{\prime}\not<\Delta
,
\varepsilon_{\Delta^{\prime}}(D_{\Delta}(\pi))
is equal to
\varepsilon_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{r}(\Delta,\mathfrak{h}))
.

8.6.
Change of
\eta_{\Delta}
by a left integral

The following lemma will be useful later.

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. For any segments
\Delta
and
\Delta^{\prime}
,
\mathfrak{mx}(\pi,\Delta)
is a submultisegment of
\mathfrak{mx}(I_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi),\Delta)
, equivalently

\eta_{\Delta}(\pi)\leq\eta_{\Delta}(I_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi)).

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{mx}(\pi,\Delta)
and let
\sigma=\mathrm{St}(\Delta^{\prime})
. We have the embedding:

\pi\hookrightarrow D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi)\times\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{m}).

Then

I_{\sigma}(\pi)\hookrightarrow\sigma\times\pi\hookrightarrow\sigma\times D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi)\times\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{m}).

Thus
D_{\mathfrak{m}}(I_{\sigma}(\pi))\neq 0
. Then, for instance, one applies Theorem
8.5
and
[
Ch22+
, Corollary 7.23]
to see that
\mathfrak{m}\subset\mathfrak{mx}(I_{\sigma}(\pi),\Delta)
.

∎

8.7.
Some properties on
|\eta|_{\Delta}

Definition 8.7
.

(c.f.
[
LM22
, Section 7]
)
For
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
and a segment
\Delta=[a,b]_{\rho}
, define

|\eta|_{\Delta}(\pi)=\varepsilon_{[a,b]_{\rho}}(\pi)+\varepsilon_{[a+1,b]_{\rho}}(\pi)+\ldots+\varepsilon_{[b,b]_{\rho}}(\pi).

We shall use the following properties in Section
16.2
.

Proposition 8.8
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
and
\Delta=[a,b]_{\rho}\in\mathrm{Seg}
. Let
\Delta^{\prime}=[c,d]_{\rho}
be a segment with
D_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi)\neq 0
. Then

(1)

For
c<a
and
d=b
,
\eta_{\Delta}(\pi)=\eta_{\Delta}(D_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi))
.

(2)

For
b\geq c\geq a
and
d=b
,
|\eta|_{\Delta}(\pi)=|\eta|_{\Delta}(D_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi))-1
.

(3)

For
b>d\geq c\geq a
,
|\eta|_{\Delta}(\pi)=|\eta|_{\Delta}(D_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi))
.

(4)

For
b>d
and
a>c
,
\eta_{\Delta}(D_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi))\geq\eta_{\Delta}(\pi)
.

Proof.

We shall give a proof using removal sequences. A possible alternative is to do analysis in the geometric lemma.

For (2), let
\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{r}
be the removal sequence for
(\Delta^{\prime},\pi)
, and let
\Delta_{1}^{tr},\ldots,\Delta_{r}^{tr}
be the truncated segments. Note that, by Definition
8.4
, those
\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{r}
contribute to
|\eta|_{\Delta}(\pi)
. Only
\Delta_{1}^{tr},\ldots,\Delta_{r-1}^{tr}
, but not
\Delta_{r}^{tr}
contribute to
|\eta|_{\Delta}(\pi)
. Now Theorem
8.5
concludes that
|\eta|_{\Delta}(\pi)
and
|\eta|_{\Delta}(D_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi))
differs by
1
.

For (1), it is similar to the one for (2). Use the terminologies above. Suppose
i
is the smallest integer such that
\Delta_{i}
contributes
\eta_{\Delta}(\pi)
. We have that
i>1
from the condition of (1). We only have
\Delta_{i},\ldots,\Delta_{r}
(among those
\Delta_{k}
’s) contributing
\eta_{\Delta}(\pi)
and only have
\Delta_{i-1}^{tr},\ldots,\Delta_{r-1}^{tr}
(among those
\Delta_{k}^{tr}
’s) contributing
\eta_{\Delta}(D_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi))
. From the removal process in Definition
8.4
(3), one also has that
\Delta_{k-1}^{tr}
and
\Delta_{k}
contribute to the same
\varepsilon_{\widetilde{\Delta}}
for some
\Delta
-saturated segment
\widetilde{\Delta}
. Now Theorem
8.5
concludes (1).

(3) is similar to (2) and (1). Using notations in (2), suppose
\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{j}
(possibly
i=0
) are those all, among those
\Delta_{k}
’s, contributing to
|\eta|_{\Delta}(\pi)
. Then,
\Delta_{1}^{tr},\ldots,\Delta_{j}^{tr}
are all, among those
\Delta_{k}^{tr}
’s, contributing to
|\eta|_{\Delta}(D_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi))
. Now (3) again follows from Theorem
8.5
.

(4) is also similar. Use the notations in (2). By (3), we may assume that
a>c
. Suppose
\Delta_{i},\ldots,\Delta_{j}
are those all contributing to
\eta_{\Delta}(\pi)
. (If those segments
\Delta_{i},\ldots,\Delta_{j}
do not exist, then the case is slightly simpler as the number may increase by the contribution from a truncated segment.) Then
\Delta_{i-1}^{tr},\ldots,\Delta_{j}^{tr}
are those all contributing to
\eta_{\Delta}(\pi)
. Again, we have that among those,
\Delta_{k-1}^{tr}
and
\Delta_{k}
give contribution to the same
\varepsilon_{\widetilde{\Delta}}
for some
\Delta
-saturated segment
\widetilde{\Delta}
; for
\Delta_{j}^{tr}
, it increases
\varepsilon_{\Delta_{j}^{tr}}
in
\eta_{\Delta}
by
1
. One then uses Theorem
8.5
again.
∎

We give a refinement of Proposition
8.8
(3).

Proposition 8.9
.

We use the notations in the previous lemma. Suppose
b>d\geq c\geq a
. Let
\widetilde{\Delta}=[\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b}]_{\rho}
be the first segment in the removal sequence for
(\Delta^{\prime},\mathfrak{hd}(\pi))
.

(1)

If
\widetilde{b}<b
, then
\eta_{\Delta^{\prime}}(D_{\Delta}(\pi))=\eta_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi)
.

(2)

If
\widetilde{b}\geq b
, then there exists a
\Delta
-saturated segment
\overline{\Delta}=[\overline{a},b]_{\rho}
with
\overline{a}>c
such that
\eta_{\overline{\Delta}}(D_{\Delta}(\pi))\neq\eta_{\overline{\Delta}}(\pi)
and
\varepsilon_{\overline{\Delta}}(D_{\Delta}(\pi))>0
.

Proof.

Let
\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{r}
be the removal sequence for
(\Delta^{\prime},\mathfrak{hd}(\pi))
. Let
\Delta_{1}^{tr},\ldots,\Delta_{r}^{tr}
be the truncated ones. For (1), by Definition
8.4
(2) and (3), none of those segments contributes to
\eta_{\Delta}(D_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi))
and
\eta_{\Delta}(\pi)
. Thus, we have the equality.

We now consider (2). Let
k
be the largest integer such that
a(\Delta_{k})\geq a(\Delta)
. Such integer exists by the assumption. If
k\neq r
, we pick
\overline{\Delta}=[a(\Delta_{k+1}),b(\Delta)]
; and if
k=r
, we pick
\overline{\Delta}=[\nu b(\Delta^{\prime}),b(\Delta)](\neq\emptyset)
. Then
\Delta_{k}^{tr}
will add extra one to the coordinates
\varepsilon_{\overline{\Delta}}(\pi)
to get
\varepsilon_{\overline{\Delta}}(D_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi))
while other coordinates are unchanged. This gives the desired statement.
∎

8.8.
A criteria on minimality

We first have the following minimality. The following is shown in
[
Ch22+
, Proposition 17.2]
, while we demonstrate a different argument (which however depends on results
[
Ch22+
]
as well).

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathrm{Mult}
be minimal to
\pi
. Let
\Delta
be a segment such that

a(\widetilde{\Delta})<a(\Delta),\quad b(\widetilde{\Delta})<b(\Delta)

for any segment
\widetilde{\Delta}\in\mathfrak{m}
. We also assume
D_{\Delta}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi)\neq 0
. Then
\mathfrak{m}+\Delta
is minimal to
\pi
if and only if

\eta_{\Delta}(D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi))=\eta_{\Delta}(\pi).

Proof.

Note that when
\mathfrak{m}
has only one segment. This is proved in
[
Ch22+
, Lemma 14.3 and Proposition 14.4]
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{r}\right\}
in an ascending order. Suppose
\mathfrak{m}+\Delta
is minimal to
\pi
. Then, by the subsequent property
[
Ch22+
]
,
\Delta_{1}+\Delta
is still minimal to
\pi
. Thus, by the two segment case
[
Ch22+
]
,
\eta_{\Delta}(D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi))=\eta_{\Delta}(\pi)
. We have that
\left\{\Delta_{2},\ldots,\Delta_{r}\right\}+\Delta
is minimal to
D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)
by
[
Ch22+
]
(also see Lemma
15.6
below) and so by induction,
\eta_{\Delta}(D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi))=\eta_{\Delta}(D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi))
and so we have the only if direction.

For the if direction, suppose
\mathfrak{m}+\Delta
is not minimal. Then, by
[
Ch22+
, Corollary 1.6]
, we can find a consecutive pair such that the intersection-union still gives the same derivatives. Then, we must have one segment to be
\Delta
. Then, by using consecutive pairs, we may relabel the segments such that another segment is
\Delta_{r}
. Then
[
Ch22+
, Proposition 14.4]
and Proposition
8.8
(4) imply that

\eta_{\Delta}(D_{\Delta_{r}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}-\Delta_{r}}(\pi))>\eta_{\Delta}(D_{\mathfrak{m}-\Delta_{r}}(\pi)).

On the other hand, by Proposition
8.8
(4):

\eta_{\Delta}(D_{\Delta_{r-1}}\circ\ldots\circ D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi))\geq\ldots\geq\eta_{\Delta}(D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)).

Thus, combining, we obtain that
\eta_{\Delta}(D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi))>\eta_{\Delta}(\pi)
.
∎

9.
Strong commutativity

Since most of results in this section have been shown in
[
Ch22+d
]
, some parts will be brief. For generic representations
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}
, we can extend the definition of pre-commutativity and strong commutativity for a triple
(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime},\pi)
in an obvious manner (see
[
Ch22+d
]
for more discussions). In particular, when
\sigma=\mathrm{St}(\Delta)
and
\sigma^{\prime}=\mathrm{St}(\Delta^{\prime})
, their terminologies for
(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime},\pi)
coincide with
(\Delta,\Delta^{\prime},\pi)
.

9.1.
Geometric lemma

For a
G_{k}\times G_{l}
-representation
\omega
and a
G_{n}
-representation
\pi
, inflate the
G_{n}\times G_{k}\times G_{l}
-representation
\pi\boxtimes\omega
to a
P_{n+k}\times G_{l}
-representation. Denote the (normalized) parabolically induced module

\mathrm{Ind}_{P_{n,k}\times G_{l}}^{G_{n+k}\times G_{l}}(\pi\boxtimes\omega)

by
\pi\dot{\times}^{1}\omega
.

Recall that the geometric lemma is shown in
[
BZ77
]
, which is important in formulating the pre-commutativity in Definition
2.2
and
[
Ch22+d
]
. For
\pi\in\mathrm{Alg}(G_{k})
and
\pi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Alg}(G_{l})
and for
r\leq l
,
P_{k+l}P_{k+l-r,r}
is the closed set in
G_{k+1}
. This gives rise the top layer
\pi\dot{\times}^{1}(\pi^{\prime}_{N_{r}})
in the geometric lemma on
(\pi\times\pi^{\prime})_{N_{r}}
. Hence, it gives a natural surjection from
(\pi\times\pi^{\prime})_{N_{r}}
to
\pi\dot{\times}^{1}(\pi^{\prime}_{N_{r}})
.

9.2.
Strong commutativity
\Rightarrow
Commutativity

It is not hard to prove the following proposition from definitions:

Proposition 9.1
.

[
Ch22+d
, Proposition 5.1]

Let
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
be generic representations. Let
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\pi)
be a strongly RdLi-commutative triple. Then
I_{\sigma_{2}}\circ D_{\sigma_{1}}(\pi)\cong D_{\sigma_{1}}\circ I_{\sigma_{2}}(\pi)
.

9.3.
Examples of pre-commutativity

We give simple examples of pre-commutativity, which can be deduced from a simple application of the geometric lemma:

Example 9.2
.

(1)

Suppose
\Delta_{1}\cap\Delta_{2}=\emptyset
. Then
(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\pi)
is a pre-RdLi-commutative triple (for any
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
).

(2)

Let
\Delta_{1}=[a_{1},b_{1}]_{\rho}
and let
\Delta_{2}=[a_{2},b_{2}]_{\rho}
be segments. Suppose
a_{2}<a_{1}
or
b_{2}<b_{1}
. Then
(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\pi)
is a pre-RdLi-commutative triple (for any
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
).

9.4.
Pre-commutativity
\Rightarrow
Strong commutativity

Proposition 9.3
.

[
Ch22+d
, Proposition 6.3]

Let
(\mathrm{St}(\Delta),\mathrm{St}(\Delta^{\prime}),\pi)
be a pre-RdLi-commutative triple. Let
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{mx}(\Delta,\pi)
. Then
(\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}),\mathrm{St}(\Delta^{\prime}),\pi)
is also a pre-RdLi-commutative triple.

Proof.

We only sketch the proof. Now one labels the segments in
\mathfrak{p}
such that

a(\Delta_{r})\leq\ldots\leq a(\Delta_{1}).

Let
\mathfrak{p}_{k}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{k}\right\}
. One proceeds inductively on
k
. When
k=1
, it is automatic from the given hypothesis. We shall assume that we are not in a case of Example
9.2
.

Suppose we have that
(\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}_{k}),\mathrm{St}(\Delta^{\prime}),\pi)
is a strongly RdLi-commutative triple. We now proceed in two cases:

(1)

a(\Delta_{k+1})>a(\Delta^{\prime})
. This follows from a simple application of the geometric lemma (or uses Example
9.2
(2)) and the inductive hypothesis.

(2)

a(\Delta_{k+1})\leq a(\Delta^{\prime})
. Suppose
(\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}),\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{2}),\pi)
is not strongly RdLi-commutative triple. Let
l=l_{a}(\mathfrak{p}_{k})
. Write
\Delta=[a,b]_{\rho}
and
\Delta^{\prime}=[a^{\prime},b^{\prime}]_{\rho}
. Let

\overline{\Delta}^{\prime}=[b+1,b^{\prime}]_{\rho},\quad\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}=[a^{\prime},b]_{\rho}.

Let
p=l_{a}(\mathfrak{p}_{k})
,
n=l_{a}(\Delta_{2})+n(\pi)
,
r=l_{a}(\overline{\Delta}^{\prime})
and
s=l_{a}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})
.
Then, there is only one possible geometric layer can work:

D_{\mathfrak{p}_{k+1}}(\pi)\boxtimes\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}_{k+1})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Ind}_{P_{r,n-p-r}\times P_{s,p-s}}^{G_{n-p}\times G_{p}}(\mathrm{St}(\overline{\Delta}^{\prime})\boxtimes\mathrm{St}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})\boxtimes\pi_{N_{l}})^{\phi},

where
\phi
is a twist bringing to a
G_{r}\times G_{n-p-r}\times G_{s}\times G_{p-s}
-representation. Let
\lambda
be the rightmost representation. Now the pre-commutativity for
(\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}_{k}),\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{2}),\pi)
forces that the submodule

D_{\mathfrak{p}_{k+1}}(\pi)\boxtimes\mathrm{St}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})\boxtimes D^{L}_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}_{k+1}))\hookrightarrow\lambda_{N_{r,q-r}},

where
q=l_{a}(\mathfrak{p}_{k+1})
, lies in the top layer from the geometric lemma on
\lambda_{N_{r,q-r}}
. This contradicts to a structure arising from the socle-irreducible property of a big derivative in
[
Ch22+b
, Section 9]
. See
[
Ch22+d
]
for more details.

∎

Theorem 9.4
.

[
Ch22+d
]

Let
(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\pi)
be a pre-RdLi-commutative triple. Then
(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\pi)
is a strongly RdLi-commutative triple.

Proof.

We sketch main ideas of a proof that relies on more explicit structure. Let
l=l_{a}(\Delta_{2})
. As in
[
Ch22+b
]
, we define the big derivative as:

\mathbb{D}_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi):=\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{l}}(\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{2}),\pi_{N_{l}}),

where the
G_{l}
-action is defined via embedding to the second factor in
G_{n(\pi)-l}\times G_{l}
. The big derivative is equipped with
G_{n(\pi)-l}
-action via embedding to the factor in
G_{n(\pi)-l}\times G_{l}
.

By the condition of pre-RdLi-commutativity, we have that:

D_{\Delta_{1}}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{2})\times\mathbb{D}_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)

To show strong commutativity, it is equivalent to show that the map factors through the embedding
\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{2})\times D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{2})\times\mathbb{D}_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)
.

It is shown in
[
Ch22+b
]
that
\mathbb{D}_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)
satisfies the socle-irreducible property. Thus, it suffices to show that for any composition factor
\tau
in
\mathbb{D}_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)/D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)
, there is no embedding from
D_{\Delta_{1}}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi)
to
\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{2})\times\tau
.

We shall not go through all the details. The main idea is to consider the invariants
\eta_{\Delta_{1}}
and
\eta_{\Delta_{2}}^{L}
. By the arguments in the proof of Theorem
10.3
(which we only need pre-commutativity rather than strong commutativity, and use Proposition
9.3
), we have that

\eta_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)=\eta_{\Delta_{1}}(I_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi)),\quad\eta_{\Delta_{2}}^{L}(D_{\Delta_{1}}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi))=\eta^{L}_{\Delta_{2}}(I_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi)).

On the other hand, some computations on Jacquet functors give that

|\eta|_{\Delta_{1}}(\tau)\leq|\eta|_{\Delta_{1}}(D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi))=|\eta|_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)-1,\quad|\eta|_{\Delta_{2}}^{L}(\tau)\leq|\eta|_{\Delta_{2}}^{L}(\pi)

and
\eta_{\Delta_{1}}(\tau)\neq\eta_{\Delta_{1}}(D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi))
. Then one uses those data to analyse and show that the embeddings
D_{\Delta_{1}}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{2})\times\tau
cannot happen.
∎

10.
Combinatorial commutativity

Definition 10.1
.

Let
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
be segments. Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. We say that
(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\pi)
is a
combinatorially RdLi-commutative triple
if
D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)\neq 0
and

\eta_{\Delta_{1}}(I_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi))=\eta_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi).

There is a dual definition:

Definition 10.2
.

Let
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
be segments. Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. We say that
(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\pi)
is a
dual combinatorially RdLi-commutative triple
if

\eta_{\Delta_{2}}^{L}(D_{\Delta_{1}}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi))=\eta_{\Delta_{2}}^{L}(I_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi)).

Theorem 10.3
.

[
Ch22+d
, Theorem 8.4]

Let
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
be segments. Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Then the followings are equivalent:

(1)

(\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{1}),\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{2}),\pi)
is a strongly RdLi-commutative triple;

(2)

(\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{2}),\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{1}),D_{\Delta_{1}}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi))
is a strongly LdRi-commutative triple;

(3)

(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\pi)
is a combinatorially RdLi-commutative triple;

(4)

(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\pi)
is a dual combinatorially RdLi-commutative triple.

Proof.

We sketch a proof. Let
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{mx}(\Delta_{1},\pi)
. For (1)
\Rightarrow
(3), note that Proposition
9.3
holds for any
\Delta
-saturated
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
such that
D_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}(I_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi))\neq 0
. One can now deduce (3) with Lemma
8.6
. An alternative proof is given in
[
Ch22+d
, Theorem 8.4]
.

For (3)
\Rightarrow
(1). It is simpler to prove that
(\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}),\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{2}),\pi)
is a strongly RdLi-commutative triple
[
Ch22+d
]
by using the property that
D_{\mathfrak{p}}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi)\boxtimes\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})
is a direct summand in
I_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi)_{N_{l}}
, where
l=l_{a}(\mathfrak{p})
. (For instance, one may use
[
Ch22+d
, Proposition 4.4]
and the decomposition
\pi\hookrightarrow D_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi)\times\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})
.) Then one sees that
(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\pi)
is pre-RdLi-commutative and so (1) follows from Proposition
9.3
.

We now prove (1)
\Rightarrow
(2). Let
\sigma_{1}=\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})
and let
\sigma_{2}=\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{2})
. Let
\tau=I_{\Delta_{2}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi)\cong D_{\mathfrak{p}}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi)
(the commutativity follows from Proposition
9.3
and the strong commutativity of
(\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}),\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{2}),\pi)
). Let
m=n(\pi)
,
l_{1}=l_{a}(\sigma_{1})
,
l_{2}=l_{a}(\sigma_{2})
. Let
N=N_{l_{2},m,l_{1}}
. We consider the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\tau_{N_{l_{2},m}}\boxtimes\sigma_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\sigma_{2}\boxtimes D_{\Delta_{2}}^{L}(\tau)\boxtimes\sigma_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi)_{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\tau\times\sigma_{1})_{N}}
\textstyle{\sigma_{2}\boxtimes\pi_{N_{m,l_{1}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

(The commutativity of the diagram follows from some socle irreducible property of parabolic inductions, see
[
Ch22+d
, Section 2]
.) Indeed,
\tau\boxtimes\sigma_{1}
is a direct summand in
I_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi)_{N_{l_{1}}}
(see
[
Ch22+d
, Proposition 7.1]
) and so gives a non-zero composition as follows:

\tau\boxtimes\sigma_{1}\hookrightarrow I_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi)_{N_{l_{1}}}\rightarrow(\tau\times\sigma_{1})_{N_{l_{1}}}\twoheadrightarrow\tau\boxtimes\sigma_{1},

where the last projection comes from the quotient in the geometric lemma. (For more details, see
[
Ch22+d
]
.) This gives the layer that the copy
\sigma_{2}\boxtimes D_{\sigma_{2}}(\tau)\boxtimes\sigma_{1}
lies in
(\tau\times\sigma_{1})_{N}
from geometric lemma. Now from the commutativity diagram, one must have that
(\sigma_{2},\sigma_{1},\tau)
is pre-LdRi-commutative triple. This implies that
(\Delta_{2},\Delta_{1},\tau)
is also pre-LdRi-commutative triple (see e.g.
[
Ch22+d
, Proposition 7.2]
). Then
(\Delta_{2},\Delta_{1},\tau)
is also strongly LdRi-commutative triple by Theorem
9.4
as desired.

Proof for (2)
\Rightarrow
(1) is similar to that of (1)
\Rightarrow
(2). A proof for (2)
\Leftrightarrow
(4) is similar to that of (1)
\Leftrightarrow
(3).
∎

Part III
Generalized GGP relevance from sequences of strongly commutative triples

In this section, we define and study the strong commutation for multisegments, extending the segment case in Part
II
. As shown in
[
Ch22+
]
(see Theorem
15.3
), there is a good theory of minimal multisegments for derivatives and integrals. Sections
11
to
15
show the compatibility of such theory with the strong commutation for multisegments, which will be useful in the later proofs. Another two main results in this part are the uniqueness property (Theorem
16.2
) and the symmetry property (Theorem
18.1
) for relevance.

11.
Unlinked segments for strongly commutative triples

11.1.
Unlinked segments

We first have the following result, see e.g.
[
Ch22+
, Lemma 4.10]
:

Lemma 11.1
.

Let
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
be unlinked segments. Then
D_{\Delta_{1}}\circ D_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi)\cong D_{\Delta_{2}}\circ D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)
and
I_{\Delta_{1}}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi)\cong I_{\Delta_{2}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)
.

Proposition 11.2
.

Let
\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\Delta_{2}^{\prime}
be unlinked segments. Let
\sigma=\mathrm{St}(\left\{\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\right\})
. Let
\Delta
be another segment. Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

(\Delta,\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\Delta,\Delta_{2}^{\prime},I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi))
are strongly RdLi-commutative triples;

(2)

(\Delta,\Delta_{2}^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\Delta,\Delta_{1}^{\prime},I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\pi))
are strongly RdLi-commutative triples.

Proof.

Suppose (1). By Theorem
10.3
,

\eta_{\Delta}(\pi)=\eta_{\Delta}(I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi))=\eta_{\Delta}(I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi))

and, by Lemma
8.6
,

\eta_{\Delta}(\pi)\leq\eta_{\Delta}(I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\pi))\leq\eta_{\Delta}(I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\pi))=\eta_{\Delta}(I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\pi)).

Thus, those inequalities are equalities. This implies (2). The other direction can be proved similarly.
∎

Proposition 11.3
.

Let
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
be unlinked segments. Let
\sigma=\mathrm{St}(\left\{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}\right\})
. Let
\Delta^{\prime}
be another segment. Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

(\Delta_{1},\Delta^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\Delta_{2},\Delta^{\prime},D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi))
are strongly RdLi-commutative triples;

(2)

(\Delta_{2},\Delta^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\Delta_{1},\Delta^{\prime},D_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi))
are strongly RdLi-commutative triples.

Proof.

Let
\tau=I_{\Delta^{\prime}}\circ D_{\Delta_{2}}\circ D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)
. Note that if
(\Delta^{\prime},\Delta_{2},\tau)
is strongly LdRi-commutative, then
D_{\Delta^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}}(\tau)=D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)
by Proposition
9.1
and so
I_{\Delta_{2}}(\tau)=I_{\Delta^{\prime}}\circ D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)
.

By using the duality in Theorem
10.3
, the two conditions can be rephrased as:

(1)

(\Delta^{\prime},\Delta_{1},I_{\Delta_{2}}(\tau))
and
(\Delta^{\prime},\Delta_{2},\tau)
are strongly LdRi-commutative triples;

(2)

(\Delta^{\prime},\Delta_{2},I_{\Delta_{1}}(\tau))
and
(\Delta^{\prime},\Delta_{1},\tau)
are strongly LdRi commutative triples.

Now the proposition follows from the LdRi version of the integral one in Proposition
11.2
.
∎

12.
Intersection-union operations for integrals in commutative triples

12.1.
Intersection-union process

Let
\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{2}\in\mathrm{Mult}
. We say that
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
is obtained from
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
by an
intersection-union process
if there exists a pair of linked segments
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
such that

\mathfrak{m}_{2}=\mathfrak{m}_{1}-\left\{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}\right\}+\Delta_{1}\cup\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{1}\cap\Delta_{2}.

(Drop the last term if
\Delta_{1}\cap\Delta_{2}=\emptyset
.) Recall that
\leq_{Z}
is defined in Section
2.2
.

12.2.
Strong commutativity under intersection-union process for integrals

In this section, we shall prove:

Proposition 12.1
.

Let
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
be linked segments. Let
\widetilde{\Delta}
be another segment. Let
\Delta_{1}^{\prime}=\Delta_{1}\cup\Delta_{2}
and
\Delta_{2}^{\prime}=\Delta_{1}\cap\Delta_{2}
. Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Suppose
I_{\Delta_{2}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)\cong I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\pi)
. Then, the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

(\widetilde{\Delta},\Delta_{1},\pi)
and
(\widetilde{\Delta},\Delta_{2},I_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi))
are strongly RdLi-commutative triples;

(2)

(\widetilde{\Delta},\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\widetilde{\Delta},\Delta_{2}^{\prime},I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi))

are strongly RdLi-commutative triples;

(3)

(\widetilde{\Delta},\Delta_{2}^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\widetilde{\Delta},\Delta_{1}^{\prime},I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\pi))
are strongly RdLi-commutative triples.

Moreover, if any of the equivalent conditions holds, then
I_{\Delta_{2}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}}\circ D_{\widetilde{\Delta}}(\pi)\cong I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\widetilde{\Delta}}(\pi)
.

Proof.

We use the notations in the statement of the proposition. Suppose (1) holds. By Theorem
10.3
,

(*)\quad\eta_{\widetilde{\Delta}}(\pi)=\eta_{\widetilde{\Delta}}(I_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi))=\eta_{\widetilde{\Delta}}(I_{\Delta_{2}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi))=\eta_{\widetilde{\Delta}}(I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi)).

On the other hand, by Lemma
8.6
,

\eta_{\widetilde{\Delta}}(\pi)\leq\eta_{\widetilde{\Delta}}(I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi))\leq\eta_{\widetilde{\Delta}}(I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi)).

Now, (*) forces that the inclusion is actually equations. Hence, we have that
(\widetilde{\Delta},\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\widetilde{\Delta},\Delta_{2}^{\prime},I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\pi))
are combinatorially commutative triples, and so are strongly commutative triples by Theorem
10.3
. This proves (2).

Since
\Delta_{1}^{\prime}
and
\Delta_{2}^{\prime}
are unlinked, we have
I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi)\cong I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\pi)
. Then a similar argument as above will prove (3).

For (3)
\Rightarrow
(2), one can argue similarly by using the followings:

\eta_{\widetilde{\Delta}}(\pi)=\eta_{\widetilde{\Delta}}(I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi))=\eta_{\widetilde{\Delta}}(I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi))=\eta_{\widetilde{\Delta}}(I_{\Delta_{2}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)),

\eta_{\widetilde{\Delta}}(\pi)\leq\eta_{\widetilde{\Delta}}(I_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi))\leq\eta_{\widetilde{\Delta}}(I_{\Delta_{2}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)).

Proving (3)
\Rightarrow
(1) is similar. The last assertion follows by applying
D_{\widetilde{\Delta}}
on
I_{\Delta_{2}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)\cong I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\pi)
and then using Proposition
9.1
.
∎

Remark 12.2
.

An analogous statement does not hold in general (while it is not so far away, c.f. Lemma
12.3
). For example, consider
\Delta_{1}=[0,1]
and
\Delta_{2}=[2,3]
and
\widetilde{\Delta}=[1,2]
. Let
\pi=\mathrm{St}([0,3])
. It is clear that
D_{\Delta_{2}}\circ D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)=D_{[0,3]}(\pi)=\mathbb{C}
as
G_{0}
-representation. We clearly have that
([0,3],\widetilde{\Delta},\pi)
is strongly RdLi-commutative triple, but
([0,1],\widetilde{\Delta},\pi)
is not even pre-RdLi-commutative triple.

12.3.
Commutativity for derivatives and integrals

Lemma 12.3
.

[
Ch22+
, Proposition 15.3]

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Let
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
be linked segments with
\Delta_{1}<\Delta_{2}
. If
D_{\Delta_{2}}\circ D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)\not\cong D_{\Delta_{1}\cup\Delta_{2}}\circ D_{\Delta_{1}\cap\Delta_{2}}(\pi)
, then

D_{\Delta_{2}}\circ D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)\cong D_{\Delta_{1}}\circ D_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi).

Remark 12.4
.

It is not true in general if one switches the condition
\Delta_{1}<\Delta_{2}
to
\Delta_{2}<\Delta_{1}
. For example, let

\pi=\langle\left\{[0,2],[0,1],[1,2]\right\}\rangle.

Let
\Delta_{1}=[1]
and let
\Delta_{2}=[2]
. Note that
D_{[1]}\circ D_{[2]}(\pi)\cong\langle\left\{[0,1],[0],[1,2]\right\}\rangle
, but
D_{[1,2]}(\pi)\cong D_{[2]}\circ D_{[1]}(\pi)=\langle\left\{[0,2],[0],[1]\right\}\rangle
.

Lemma 12.5
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Let
\Delta_{1}^{\prime}
and
\Delta_{2}^{\prime}
be linked segments with
\Delta_{1}^{\prime}<\Delta_{2}^{\prime}
. If
I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi)\not\cong I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\cup\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\pi)
, then

I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi)\cong I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\pi).

Proof.

Let
\tau=I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi)
. Then
D^{L}_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}\circ D^{L}_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\tau)=\pi
and
D^{L}_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\cup\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ D^{L}_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\tau)\not\cong D^{L}_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}\circ D^{L}_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\tau)
. Thus, the left version of Lemma
12.3
implies that

D^{L}_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}\circ D^{L}_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\tau)\cong D^{L}_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ D^{L}_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\tau),

which implies the lemma.
∎

Proposition 12.6
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Let
\Delta_{1}^{\prime}
and
\Delta_{2}^{\prime}
be linked segments with
\Delta_{1}^{\prime}<\Delta_{2}^{\prime}
. Let
\Delta
be another segment. Suppose

I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi)\not\cong I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\cup\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\pi).

Then
(\Delta,\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\Delta,\Delta_{2}^{\prime},I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi))
are strongly RdLi-commutative triples if and only if
(\Delta,\Delta_{2}^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\Delta,\Delta_{1}^{\prime},I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\pi))
are strongly RdLi-commutative triples. Moreover, if one of the equivalent conditions holds, then

I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\Delta}(\pi)\not\cong I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\cup\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\cap\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\Delta}(\pi).

Proof.

The strong commutation implies
\eta_{\Delta}(\pi)=\eta_{\Delta}(I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi))=\eta_{\Delta}(I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi))
. Moreover, Lemma
8.6
implies that
\eta_{\Delta}(\pi)\leq\eta_{\Delta}(I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\pi))\leq\eta_{\Delta}(I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\pi))
. Combining the equation with Lemma
12.5
, the above inequalities have to be equalities. Now the strong commutations of
(\Delta,\Delta_{2}^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\Delta,\Delta_{1}^{\prime},I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\pi))
follow from Theorem
10.3
.

We now prove the last assertion. One approach is to develop those combinatorial invariants for ladder representations, in which one may use the Kret-Lapid description for the Jacquet modules of ladder representations
[
KL12
]
. Here we give a proof using module structures directly. Suppose not to derive a contradiction. Let
\tau=I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\pi)(\cong I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi))
. Let
l=l_{a}(\Delta)
. We consider the following diagram:

D_{\Delta}(\pi)\boxtimes\mathrm{St}(\Delta)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 399.32397pt\raise-96.66675pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 317.1177pt\raise-80.50229pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.63542pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{j_{3}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 330.7341pt\raise-72.39893pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where
s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}
are the surjections to the top layer in the geometric lemma; and
\lambda=(\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{1}^{\prime})\times\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{2}^{\prime})\times D_{\Delta}(\pi)
;

\sigma=\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{1}^{\prime}+\Delta_{2}^{\prime}),\quad\sigma^{\prime}=\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\cup\Delta_{2}^{\prime}+\Delta_{1}\cap\Delta_{2}^{\prime}).

Using the strong commutativity (see
[
Ch22+d
, Proposition 5.5]
), we have a map
p:D_{\Delta}(\tau)\boxtimes\mathrm{St}(\Delta)\rightarrow\lambda\boxtimes\mathrm{St}(\Delta)
such that
j_{2}\circ p=s_{2}\circ i\circ i^{\prime}
. Using
[
Ch22+
, Appendix B]
and our assumption (and so
D_{\Delta}(\tau)\cong D_{\Delta}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\cong D_{\Delta}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi)\cong I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\Delta}(\pi)
), we have that
t\circ p\neq 0
and so
j_{3}\circ t\circ p\neq 0
. This implies that
t^{\prime\prime}\circ i\neq 0
. Hence, this implies that
\tau\hookrightarrow\sigma^{\prime}\times\pi
. Thus,

\tau\cong I_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\pi),

With Lemma
12.5
, it gives a contradiction to the given condition.
∎

13.
Intersection-union operations for derivatives in commutative triples

13.1.
Strong commutativity under intersection-union process for derivatives

Lemma 13.1
.

Let
\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\Delta_{2}^{\prime}
be linked segments. Let
\Delta
be another segment. Let
\widetilde{\Delta}_{1}^{\prime}=\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\cup\Delta_{2}^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}^{\prime}=\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Delta_{2}^{\prime}
. Suppose
D_{\Delta}(\pi)\neq 0
and

I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\Delta}(\pi)\cong I_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{1}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\Delta}(\pi).

If
(\Delta,\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\Delta,\Delta_{2}^{\prime},I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi))
are strongly RdLi-commutative triples, then the following holds:

(1)

(\Delta,\widetilde{\Delta}_{1}^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\Delta,\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}^{\prime},I_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi))
are strongly RdLi-commutative triples;

(2)

(\Delta,\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\Delta,\widetilde{\Delta}_{1}^{\prime},I_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}^{\prime}}(\pi))
are strongly RdLi-commutative triples;

(3)

I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi)\cong I_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi)
.

Proof.

(3) follows from Proposition
12.6
. Then (1) and (2) follow from Proposition
12.1
.
∎

The following is the derivative analog for Proposition
12.1
.

Proposition 13.2
.

Let
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
be linked segments. Let
\Delta^{\prime}
be another segment. Let
\widetilde{\Delta}_{1}=\Delta_{1}\cup\Delta_{2}
and let
\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}=\Delta_{1}\cap\Delta_{2}
. Suppose
D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)\neq 0
and
D_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi)\neq 0
, and
D_{\Delta_{2}}\circ D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)\cong D_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}}\circ D_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{1}}(\pi)
. If
(\Delta_{1},\Delta^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\Delta_{2},\Delta^{\prime},D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi))
are strongly RdLi-commutative triples, then the following holds:

(1)

(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1},\Delta^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\widetilde{\Delta}_{2},\Delta^{\prime},D_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{1}}(\pi))
are strongly RdLi-commutative triple;

(2)

(\widetilde{\Delta}_{2},\Delta^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1},\Delta^{\prime},D_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}}(\pi))
are strongly RdLi-commutative triple;

(3)

D_{\Delta_{2}}\circ D_{\Delta_{1}}\circ I_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi)\cong D_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}}\circ D_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{1}}\circ I_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi)
.

Proof.

Let

\tau=D_{\Delta_{2}}\circ D_{\Delta_{1}}\circ I_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi)\cong I_{\Delta^{\prime}}\circ D_{\Delta_{2}}\circ D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)

Using the given condition and Lemma
11.1
, one also has:

\tau\cong I_{\Delta^{\prime}}\circ D_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{1}}\circ D_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}}(\pi)\cong I_{\Delta^{\prime}}\circ D_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}}\circ D_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{1}}(\pi).

One first reformulates into equivalent conditions and statements involving
\tau
by Theorem
10.3
and Proposition
9.1
: e.g. the original two strong commutations become that
(\Delta^{\prime},\Delta_{1},I_{\Delta_{2}}(\tau))
and
(\Delta^{\prime},\Delta_{2},\tau)
are strongly LdRi-commutative triples; (1) equivalent to
(\Delta^{\prime},\widetilde{\Delta}_{1},I_{\widetilde{\Delta}_{2}}(\tau))
and
(\Delta^{\prime},\widetilde{\Delta}_{2},\tau)
are strongly LdRi-commutative triples, etc. Then the equivalent statements follow from Lemma
13.1
.

∎

13.2.
Commutativity for derivatives

Lemma 13.3
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Let
\Delta_{1}^{\prime}
and
\Delta_{2}^{\prime}
be linked segments with
\Delta_{1}^{\prime}<\Delta_{2}^{\prime}
. Let
\Delta
be another segment. Suppose

I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\Delta}(\pi)\not\cong I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\cup\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\cap\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\Delta}(\pi).

Then,
(\Delta,\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\Delta,\Delta_{2}^{\prime},I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi))
are strongly RdLi-commutative triples if and only if
(\Delta,\Delta_{2}^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\Delta,\Delta_{1}^{\prime},I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\pi))
are strongly RdLi-commutative triples. Moreover, if one of the equivalent conditions holds, then

I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi)\not\cong I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\cup\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\pi).

Proof.

The last assertion follows from Proposition
12.1
and then the equivalent condition follows from Proposition
12.6
.

∎

The following is the derivative analog of Proposition
12.6
. One may also use Definition
10.2
to show the last assertion of Proposition
13.4
and then use a similar proof as in Proposition
12.1
. Once we have Proposition
13.4
, we can also deduce back Lemma
13.3
by the duality.

Proposition 13.4
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Let
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
be linked segments with
\Delta_{1}<\Delta_{2}
. Let
\Delta^{\prime}
be another segment. Suppose

D_{\Delta_{2}}\circ D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)\not\cong D_{\Delta_{1}\cup\Delta_{2}}\circ D_{\Delta_{1}\cap\Delta_{2}}(\pi).

Then
(\Delta_{1},\Delta^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\Delta_{2},\Delta^{\prime},D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi))
are strongly RdLi-commutative triples if and only if
(\Delta_{2},\Delta^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\Delta_{1},\Delta^{\prime},D_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi))
are strongly RdLi-commutative triples. Moreover, if one of the equivalent conditions holds, then

D_{\Delta_{2}}\circ D_{\Delta_{1}}\circ I_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi)\not\cong D_{\Delta_{1}\cap\Delta_{2}}\circ D_{\Delta_{1}\cup\Delta_{2}}\circ I_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi).

Proof.

We only prove the only if direction. Suppose
(\Delta_{1},\Delta^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\Delta_{2},\Delta^{\prime},D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi))
are strongly RdLi-commutative triples.

Let
\omega=I_{\Delta^{\prime}}\circ D_{\Delta_{2}}\circ D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)
. By Theorem
10.3
,
(\Delta^{\prime},\Delta_{1},I_{\Delta_{2}}(\omega))
and
(\Delta^{\prime},\Delta_{2},\omega)
are strongly LdRi-commutative triples. Note that

I_{\Delta_{1}}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}}(D_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\omega))\cong\pi\not\cong I_{\Delta_{1}\cup\Delta_{2}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}\cap\Delta_{2}}(D_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\omega)).

Hence, by Lemma
13.3
,
(\Delta^{\prime},\Delta_{1},\omega)
and
(\Delta^{\prime},\Delta_{2},I_{\Delta_{1}}(\omega))
are strongly LdRi-commutative triples. Now, by Theorem
10.3
,
(\Delta_{1},\Delta^{\prime},I_{\Delta_{1}}\circ D_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\omega))
and
(\Delta_{2},\Delta^{\prime},D_{\Delta^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{2}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}}(\omega))
are strongly RdLi-commutative triples. Now using
D_{\Delta_{2}}\circ D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)\cong D_{\Delta_{1}}\circ D_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi)
in Lemma
12.3
, we have that
(\Delta_{2},\Delta^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\Delta_{1},\Delta^{\prime},D_{\Delta_{2}}(\pi))
are strongly LdRi-commutative triples.

The last assertion follows by reformulating the last assertion in Lemma
13.3
(with Proposition
9.1
for some commutations).
∎

14.
Strong commutation for multisegments

14.1.
Strongly commutative triples for multisegments

Definition 14.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n}\in\mathrm{Mult}
. Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Suppose
D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi)\neq 0
. We write
\mathfrak{m}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{r}\right\}
in an ascending order and
\mathfrak{n}=\left\{\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\Delta_{s}^{\prime}\right\}
in an ascending order. For
1\leq i\leq r
, let
\mathfrak{m}_{i}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{i}\right\}
. For
1\leq j\leq s
, let
\mathfrak{n}_{j}=\left\{\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\Delta_{j}^{\prime}\right\}
, and let
\mathfrak{m}_{0}=\emptyset
and
\mathfrak{n}_{0}=\emptyset
. We say that
(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n},\pi)
is a
strongly RdLi-commutative triple
if for any
1\leq i\leq r
and
1\leq j\leq s
,

(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j},I_{\mathfrak{n}_{j-1}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}_{i-1}}(\pi))

is a strongly RdLi-commutative triple in the sense of Definition
2.2
.

Note that, by Propositions
11.2
and
11.3
, the strong commutation for multisegments is independent for a choice of an ascending order.

We can similarly define the notion of LdRi-commutative triples. We have the following duality in view of Theorem
10.3
.

Proposition 14.2
.

Let
(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n},\pi)
be a strongly RdLi-commutative triple. Then
(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{m},I_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi))
is a strongly LdRi-commutative triple.

Proof.

This follows from repeated uses of Theorem
10.3
(1)
\Leftrightarrow
(2). We omit the details.

∎

In terms of branching law, one may consider this is compatible with the duality in (
4.2
).

14.2.
Combinatorial commutation for a sequence

Lemma 14.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n}\in\mathrm{Mult}
. Let
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}
be segments. Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

(\mathfrak{m},\Delta^{\prime},\pi)
is strongly RdLi-commutative;

(2)

\eta^{L}_{\Delta^{\prime}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ I_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi))=\eta^{L}_{\Delta^{\prime}}(I_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi))
.

The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

(\Delta,\mathfrak{n},\pi)
is strongly RdLi-commutative;

(2)

\eta_{\Delta}(I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi))=\eta_{\Delta}(\pi)
.

Proof.

Write
\mathfrak{m}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{r}\right\}
with the labelling in an ascending order. Let

\mathfrak{m}_{j}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{j}\right\}.

The second equivalence is easier by repeatedly using combinatorial commutativity (and Theorem
10.3
) and we only prove the first one. For the first one, we first have the following inequalities (by the left version of Lemma
8.6
):

\eta^{L}_{\Delta^{\prime}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ I_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi))\leq\ldots\leq\eta^{L}_{\Delta^{\prime}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}_{j}}\circ I_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi))\leq\ldots\leq\eta^{L}_{\Delta^{\prime}}(I_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi)).

We now assume (2). Then all the inequalities above are equalities. Thus, inductively, we have that

\eta^{L}_{\Delta^{\prime}}(D_{\Delta_{j}}\circ I_{\Delta^{\prime}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}_{j-1}}(\pi))=\eta^{L}_{\Delta^{\prime}}(D_{\Delta_{j}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}_{j-1}}\circ I_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi))=\eta^{L}_{\Delta^{\prime}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}_{j}}\circ I_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi)),

where the first equality follows from the strong commutativity in previous
j-1
cases and Proposition
9.1
. Using Theorem
10.3
, we now have
(\Delta_{j},\Delta^{\prime},D_{\mathfrak{m}_{j-1}}(\pi))
is strongly RdLi-commutative.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2) follows from Theorem
10.3
and Proposition
9.1
.
∎

15.
Minimal strongly commutative triples

15.1.
Minimality for pairs

Lemma 15.1
.

Let
(\Delta,\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\Delta,\Delta_{2}^{\prime},I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi))
be strongly RdLi-commutative triples. Then

I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}(\pi)\not\cong I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\cup\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}(\pi)
if and only if

I_{\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\Delta}(\pi)\not\cong I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\cup\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\cap\Delta_{2}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\Delta}(\pi)
.

Proof.

The if direction follows from Proposition
12.1
. For the only if direction, it follows from Lemma
13.1
.
∎

Lemma 15.2
.

Let
(\Delta_{1},\Delta^{\prime},\pi)
and
(\Delta_{2},\Delta^{\prime},D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi))
be strongly RdLi-commutative triples. Then
D_{\Delta_{2}}\circ D_{\Delta_{1}}(I_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi))\not\cong D_{\Delta_{1}\cup\Delta_{2}}\circ D_{\Delta_{1}\cap\Delta_{2}}(I_{\Delta^{\prime}}(\pi))
if and only if
D_{\Delta_{2}}\circ D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)\not\cong D_{\Delta_{1}\cup\Delta_{2}}\circ D_{\Delta_{1}\cap\Delta_{2}}(\pi)
.

Proof.

The if part follows from Proposition
13.4
. For the only if direction, it follows from
13.2
.

∎

15.2.
Minimality for a sequence

We recall some theory of minimal sequences established in
[
Ch22+
]
.

Theorem 15.3
.

[
Ch22+
, Theorem 1.7]
(Uniqueness of minimality)
Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
.

(1)

For
\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n}\in\mathrm{Mult}
with
D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi)\cong D_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi)
(resp.
D^{L}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi)\cong D^{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi)
), if both
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{n}
are Rd-minimal (resp. Ld-minimal) to
\pi
, then
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{n}
.

(2)

For
\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n}\in\mathrm{Mult}
with
I_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi)\cong I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi)
(resp.
I^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi)\cong I^{R}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi)
), if both
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{n}
are Li-minimal (resp. Ri-minimal) to
\pi
, then
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{n}
.

Proof.

(1) is proved in
[
Ch22+
]
. For (2), let
\tau=I_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi)
. Hence
D^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\tau)\cong D^{R}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\tau)
. Li-minimality to
\pi
implies Ld-minimality to
\tau
. Thus (2) follows from (1).
∎

Definition 15.4
.

A strongly RdLi-commutative triple
(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n},\pi)
is said to be
minimal
if
\mathfrak{m}
is Rd-minimal to
\pi
and
\mathfrak{n}
is Li-minimal to
\pi
.

While we only require that the minimality holds for
\pi
in Definition
15.4
, the following proposition shows that the minimality holds for ’intermediate terms’.

Proposition 15.5
.

Let
(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n},\pi)
be a minimal strongly RdLi-commutative triple. Write
\mathfrak{m}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{r}\right\}
in an ascending order and write
\mathfrak{n}=\left\{\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\Delta_{s}^{\prime}\right\}
in an ascending order. Let
\mathfrak{m}_{i}=\left\{\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\Delta_{i}^{\prime}\right\}
and let
\mathfrak{n}_{i}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{i}\right\}
(with
\mathfrak{n}_{0}=\emptyset
and
\mathfrak{m}_{0}=\emptyset
). Let
\bar{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}=\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{m}_{i}
and let
\bar{\mathfrak{n}}_{i}=\mathfrak{n}-\mathfrak{n}_{i}
. Then, for any
i
and
j
,
\bar{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}
(resp.
\bar{\mathfrak{n}}_{j}
) is Rd-minimal (resp. Li-minimal) to
I_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}}(\pi))
.

Proof.

It is shown in
[
Ch22+
]
that checking minimality is reduced to two segment case. Then, for integrals, it follows from Lemma
15.1
; and for derivatives, it follows from Lemma
15.2
.
∎

15.3.
Commutativity under minimality and RdLi-commutativity

We first recall the following commutativity result:

Lemma 15.6
.

[
Ch22+
]

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Let
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathrm{Mult}
be minimal to
\pi
. For any submultisegment
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{m}
,

(1)

D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi)\cong D_{\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}(\pi)
and
\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
is minimal to
D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}(\pi)
; and

(2)

\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
is still minimal to
\pi
.

One can then applies Lemma
15.6
multiple times to obtain that:

Corollary 15.7
.

[
Ch22+
]

Let
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathrm{Mult}
be minimal to
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Write
\mathfrak{m}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{r}\right\}
in any order. Then

D_{\Delta_{r}}\circ\ldots\circ D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)\cong D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi).

We leave the formulations of an integral version of the above two results to the reader (also see the proof of Lemma
12.5
).

We have an analog of above two results for strongly RdLi-commutative triples:

Corollary 15.8
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Let
(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n},\pi)
be a minimal strongly RdLi-commutative triple. Write the segments in
\mathfrak{m}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{r}\right\}
and write the segments in
\mathfrak{n}=\left\{\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\Delta_{s}^{\prime}\right\}
in any order. For any
\delta\in S_{r}
and any
\delta^{\prime}\in S_{s}
, let
\mathfrak{m}_{i}^{\delta}=\left\{\Delta_{\delta(1)},\ldots,\Delta_{\delta(i)}\right\}
and let
\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\delta^{\prime}}=\left\{\Delta_{\delta^{\prime}(1)}^{\prime},\ldots,\Delta_{\delta^{\prime}(j)}^{\prime}\right\}
. Then, the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

there exists
\delta\in S_{r},\delta^{\prime}\in S_{s}
such that, for any
i,j\geq 1
,
(\Delta_{\delta(i)},\Delta_{\delta^{\prime}(j)}^{\prime},I_{\mathfrak{n}^{\delta^{\prime}}_{j-1}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\delta}_{i-1}}(\pi))
is a strongly RdLi-commutative triple;

(2)

for all
\delta\in S_{r},\delta^{\prime}\in S_{s}
, and for any
i,j\geq 1
,
(\Delta_{\delta(i)},\Delta_{\delta^{\prime}(j)}^{\prime},I_{\mathfrak{n}^{\delta^{\prime}}_{j-1}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\delta}_{i-1}}(\pi))
is a strongly RdLi-commutative triple.

Proof.

(2) implying (1) is clear. For (1) implying (2), by relabeling and using transpositions generating
S_{n}
, it suffices to show that if
(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j}^{\prime},I_{\mathfrak{n}_{j-1}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}_{i-1}}(\pi))
is a strongly RdLi-commutative triple for any
i,j
, then
(\Delta_{\delta(i)},\Delta_{j}^{\prime},\pi)
(resp.
(\Delta_{i},\Delta_{\delta^{\prime}(j)}^{\prime},\pi)
) is also a strongly RdLi-commutative triple for a transposition
\delta\in S_{r}
(resp.
\delta^{\prime}\in S_{s}
). We consider the statement for
\delta
and suppose
\delta
switches
x
and
x+1
. Then we only have to prove the strong commutation of the following pairs for any
j
:

(15.8)

\displaystyle(\Delta_{x},\Delta_{j},\omega),\quad(\Delta_{x+1},\Delta_{j},D_{\Delta_{x}}(\omega)),

where
\omega=I_{\mathfrak{n}_{j-1}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}_{x-1}}(\pi)
. But, we have minimality of
\left\{\Delta_{x},\Delta_{x+1}\right\}
to
\omega
by Lemmas
15.6
and
15.2
, and so (
15.8
) follows from Proposition
13.4
(for linked case) as well as Proposition
11.3
(for unlinked case). For the statement for
\delta^{\prime}
, one uses Lemma
15.1
and integral version of Lemma
15.6
.
∎

In particular, Proposition
15.5
, Lemma
15.6
and Corollary
15.8
give the following two special cases:

Corollary 15.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
be a submultisegment of
\mathfrak{m}
and let
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
be a submultisegment of
\mathfrak{n}
. Then
(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime},\pi)
is still a minimal strongly RdLi-commutative triple.

Corollary 15.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
be a submultisegment of
\mathfrak{m}
and let
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
be a submultisegment of
\mathfrak{n}
. Then
(\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},\mathfrak{n}-\mathfrak{n}^{\prime},I_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}(\pi))
is also a minimal strongly RdLi-commutative triple.

16.
Generalized GGP relevant pairs and their uniqueness

16.1.
Generalized relevant pairs

Definition 16.1
.

Let
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\in\mathrm{Irr}
. We say that a pair
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
is
relevant
if there exist multisegments
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{n}
such that
I_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\nu^{1/2}\cdot\pi_{1})\cong\pi_{2}
and
(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n},\nu^{1/2}\cdot\pi_{1})
is a strongly RdLi-commutative triple. We further say that
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
is
i^{*}
-relevant
if such
\mathfrak{m}
satisfies
i^{*}=l_{a}(\mathfrak{m})
.

Our first main result is the following uniqueness statement, which shows that the
i^{*}
-relevance in Definition
16.1
is well-defined. The main idea is to compare the invariant for
|\eta|_{\Delta}
for a suitable choice of
\Delta
.

Theorem 16.2
.

Let
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Suppose
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
is relevant. There exist unique multisegments
\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n}
such that

•

(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n},\nu^{1/2}\cdot\pi_{1})
is a minimal strongly RdLi-commutative triple; and

•

D^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\nu^{1/2}\cdot\pi_{1})\cong D^{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi_{2})
.

Proof.

The existence part follows from the definition of relevance with Propositions
12.1
and
13.2
, and the closedness property shown in
[
Ch22+
]
.

We now prove the uniqueness part in the following steps:

Step 1: Case of both
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
to be non-empty

Let
\pi=\nu^{1/2}\cdot\pi_{1}
. If both
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
are both empty, then the equality
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
follows from the uniqueness of a minimal element.

Step 2: Choose an appropriate
\Delta_{*}

From now on, we assume at least one of
\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
is non-empty. Let

\mathcal{B}=\left\{b(\Delta):\Delta\in\mathfrak{m}+\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}\right\}.

Choose a
\leq
-maximal
\rho
in
\mathcal{B}
.

Set
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{m}_{b=\rho}
and
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}_{b=\rho}
. We shall only consider the case that both
\mathfrak{p}
and
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
are non-empty. When one of
\mathfrak{p}
and
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
is empty, it can be dealt similarly. Let
\Delta_{*}
(resp.
\Delta_{*}^{\prime}
) be the shortest segment in
\mathfrak{p}
(resp.
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
).

Step 3: A reduction using inductive hypothesis.

Suppose
\Delta_{*}=\Delta_{*}^{\prime}
. Then

\pi_{2}\cong I^{R}_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}}^{L}(\pi)\cong I^{R}_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\Delta_{*}}^{L}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}-\Delta_{*}}^{L}(\pi)\cong D_{\Delta_{*}}^{L}\circ I^{R}_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}-\Delta_{*}}^{L}(\pi_{1})

One can similarly obtain the expression by replacing
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{n}
respectively by
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
. Thus, by cancelling the term
D_{\Delta_{*}}^{L}
, we have:

I^{R}_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}-\Delta_{*}}^{L}(\pi_{1})\cong I^{R}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}-\Delta_{*}}^{L}(\pi_{1}).

Since
\mathfrak{m}-\Delta_{*}
and
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}-\Delta_{*}
are still Rd-minimal to
\pi
(see e.g. Corollary
15.9
), the induction gives that
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{m}-\Delta_{*}=\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}-\Delta_{*}
. Thus we also have
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
.

Step 4: Setup notations for computing
\eta_{\Delta_{*}}
in another case

Thus it remains to show that
\Delta^{\prime}_{*}\neq\Delta_{*}
is not possible. By switching the labelling if necessary, we assume that
\Delta_{*}\subsetneq\Delta^{\prime}_{*}
.

•

Let
\mathfrak{q}
(resp.
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
) be the submultisegment of
\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{p}
(resp.
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}-\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
) containing all segments
\widetilde{\Delta}
with
\widetilde{\Delta}\subset\Delta_{*}
.

Step 5: Begin to compare
|\eta|_{\Delta}(\pi)
and
|\eta|_{\Delta}(I_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi))
.

Write segments in
\mathfrak{n}
as
\overline{\Delta}_{1},\ldots,\overline{\Delta}_{s}
in an ascending order. For
1\leq j\leq s
, write
\mathfrak{n}_{j}=\left\{\overline{\Delta}_{1},\ldots,\overline{\Delta}_{j}\right\}
. We first note that the commutativity of linked segments give the following equations: for any
j
,

(16.9)

\displaystyle D_{\mathfrak{m}}(I_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}(\pi))

\displaystyle=D_{\mathfrak{p}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{p}}(I_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}(\pi))

(16.10)

\displaystyle=D_{\mathfrak{p}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{q}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{p}-\mathfrak{q}}(I_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}(\pi))

(16.11)

\displaystyle=D_{\mathfrak{q}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{p}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{p}-\mathfrak{q}}(I_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}(\pi)),

where the first equation follows by arranging the segments in an ascending order of
b(\Delta)
and the second equation follows by arranging the segments in an ascending order of
a(\Delta)
.

Since
(\Delta_{*},\overline{\Delta}_{j},D_{\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{p}-\mathfrak{q}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}_{j-1}}(\pi))
is a strongly RdLi-commutative triple by Corollary
15.9
, we have that, by Theorem
10.3
,

\eta_{\Delta_{*}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{p}-\mathfrak{q}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}_{j-1}}(\pi)))=\eta_{\Delta_{*}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{p}-\mathfrak{q}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}(\pi)).

Thus inductively, we have the first equation: for any
j
,

(\star)\quad\eta_{\Delta_{*}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{p}-\mathfrak{q}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}(\pi)))=\eta_{\Delta_{*}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{p}-\mathfrak{q}}(\pi)).

Step 6: Compute
\eta_{\Delta_{*}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{p}-\mathfrak{q}}(\pi))

It follows from Lemma
8.10
that

(\star\star)\quad\eta_{\Delta_{*}}(\pi)=\eta_{\Delta_{*}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{p}-\mathfrak{q}}(\pi)).

Step 7: Compute
|\eta|_{\Delta_{*}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{q}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi))

We now consider
|\eta|_{\Delta_{*}}(D_{\mathfrak{p}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{p}-\mathfrak{q}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi))
. By using Proposition
8.8
(1) and (2), we have that

|\eta|_{\Delta_{*}}(D_{\mathfrak{p}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{p}-\mathfrak{q}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi))=|\eta|_{\Delta_{*}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{p}-\mathfrak{q}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi))-l,

where
l
is the number of
\Delta_{*}
in
\mathfrak{p}
(which is at least one from our choice). Thus we have the following strict inequality, by Proposition
8.8
(2),

(\star\star\star)\quad|\eta|_{\Delta_{*}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{p}-\mathfrak{q}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi))>|\eta|_{\Delta_{*}}(D_{\mathfrak{p}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{p}-\mathfrak{q}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi)).

Step 8: Compute
|\eta|_{\Delta_{*}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi))
and get the comparison

We finally have that, by Proposition
8.8
(3),

(\star\star\star\star)\quad|\eta|_{\Delta_{*}}(D_{\mathfrak{q}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{p}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{p}-\mathfrak{q}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi))=|\eta|_{\Delta_{*}}(D_{\mathfrak{p}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}-\mathfrak{p}-\mathfrak{q}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi)).

Now combining
(\star)-(\star\star\star\star)
, we have:

(\bullet)\quad|\eta|_{\Delta_{*}}(\pi)>|\eta|_{\Delta_{*}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi)).

Step 9: Compare
|\eta|_{\Delta_{*}}(I_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}(\pi))
and
|\eta|_{\Delta_{*}}(\pi)

On the other hand, by Lemma
8.6
,

(\ast)\quad|\eta|_{\Delta_{*}}(I_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}(\pi))\geq|\eta|_{\Delta_{*}}(\pi).

Now, by Proposition
8.8
(4),

(\ast\ast)\quad|\eta|_{\Delta_{*}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}-\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}(\pi))\geq|\eta|_{\Delta_{*}}(I_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}(\pi))

Now, since we are assuming
\Delta_{*}\subsetneq\Delta_{*}^{\prime}
, Proposition
8.8
(1) gives that:

(\ast\ast\ast)\quad|\eta|_{\Delta_{*}}(D_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}-\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}-}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}(\pi))=|\eta|_{\Delta_{*}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}-\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}(\pi)).

Thus combining
(\ast),(\ast\ast),(\ast\ast\ast)
, we have that

(\bullet\bullet)\quad|\eta|_{\Delta_{*}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}(\pi))\geq|\eta|_{\Delta_{*}}(\pi).

Step 10: Arrive a contradiction

Since
D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}(\pi)\cong D_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi)
, we arrive a contradiction from
(\bullet)
and
(\bullet\bullet)
as desired.

∎

Remark 16.3
.

An alternative way to show the well-definedness of
i^{*}
-relevance is to use Proposition
21.2
below. Granting that, once one proves Theorem
26.4
(whose proof is independent of Theorem
16.2
), and one can use the multiplicity one of branching laws to deduce the uniqueness in Theorem
16.2
i.e. an alternate (indirect) proof.

17.
Double derivatives and double integrals

17.1.
Level preserving integrals for highest derivative multisegments

Lemma 17.1
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Let
\Delta
be a segment. Suppose
\mathrm{lev}(I_{\Delta}(\pi))=\mathrm{lev}(\pi)
. Then

\mathfrak{hd}(\pi)=\mathfrak{hd}(I_{\Delta}(\pi)).

Proof.

Since
\mathrm{lev}(\pi)=\mathrm{lev}(I_{\Delta}(\pi))
,
l_{a}(\mathfrak{hd}(\pi))=l_{a}(\mathfrak{hd}(I_{\Delta}(\pi))
, the inequality in Lemma
8.6
must be an equality. Thus
\mathfrak{hd}(\pi)=\mathfrak{hd}(I_{\Delta}(\pi))
as desired by
[
Ch22+
]
, also see
[
Ch22+d
, Corollary 8.6]
.
∎

17.2.
Level preserving integrals

Proposition 17.2
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Let
\Delta
be a segment. Suppose
\mathrm{lev}(I_{\Delta}(\pi))=\mathrm{lev}(\pi)
. Let
\mathfrak{n}
be a multisegment such that
D_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi)\neq 0
. Write
\mathfrak{n}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{r}\right\}
in an ascending order. Let
\mathfrak{n}_{j}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{j}\right\}
. Then
(\mathfrak{n},\Delta,\pi)
is a strongly RdLi-commutative triple.

Proof.

By Theorem
10.3
, it suffices to prove that those pairs are combinatorially RdLi-commutative triples. By Proposition
11.3
, it suffices to consider that
\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{r}
are arranged such that for any
i<j
,

a(\Delta_{i})\not>a(\Delta_{j}).

Note that the combinatorial commutativity of
(\Delta_{1},\Delta,\pi)
follows from Lemma
17.1
. (The subtly on the general case is that the level of
I_{\Delta}\circ D_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}(\pi)
may not be equal to
D_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}(\pi)
and we cannot prove inductively by using Lemma
17.1
.)

Since
\mathfrak{hd}(\pi)=\mathfrak{hd}(I_{\Delta}(\pi))
, by Theorem
8.5
,

\varepsilon_{\Delta^{\prime}}(D_{\mathfrak{n}_{j-1}}(\pi))=\varepsilon_{\Delta^{\prime}}(D_{\mathfrak{n}_{j-1}}\circ I_{\Delta}(\pi))

for any
\Delta_{j}
-saturated segment
\Delta^{\prime}
. This in turns gives that
\eta_{\Delta_{j}}(D_{\mathfrak{n}_{j-1}}(\pi))=\eta_{\Delta_{j}}(D_{\mathfrak{n}_{j-1}}\circ I_{\Delta}(\pi))
which is also equal to
\eta_{\Delta_{j}}(I_{\Delta}\circ D_{\mathfrak{n}_{j-1}}(\pi))
by inductively using Proposition
9.1
. Then the proposition now follows from Theorem
10.3
.
∎

17.3.
Double derivatives and integrals

One key result of
[
Ch22+
]
is the following double derivative:

Theorem 17.3
.

[
Ch22+
, Theorem 1.4]

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Let
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathrm{Mult}
. Suppose
D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi)\neq 0
. Then there exists a multisegment
\mathfrak{n}
such that

D_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi)\cong\pi^{-}.

Moreover, one can take
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{r}(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{hd}(\pi))
.

We now deduce an integral version from the double derivative one:

Theorem 17.4
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Let
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathrm{Mult}
. There exists a multisegment
\mathfrak{n}
such that

{}^{-}(I_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi))\cong\pi

and
\mathrm{lev}(I_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi))=\mathrm{lev}(I_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi))
.

Proof.

For a segment
\Delta=[a,b]_{\rho}
, define
\Delta^{+}=[a,b+1]_{\rho}
. Let
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathrm{Mult}
be such that
I_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi)\cong\langle\mathfrak{p}\rangle
. Define

\mathfrak{p}^{+}=\sum_{\Delta\in\mathfrak{p}}\Delta^{+}.

Let
\tau=I_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi)
and
\tau^{+}=\langle\mathfrak{p}^{+}\rangle
.

Let
\mathfrak{h}\in\mathrm{Mult}
such that
D_{\mathfrak{h}}(\tau^{+})\cong(\tau^{+})^{-}\cong\tau
(see Definition
8.3
). Thus we can rewrite as

(17.12)

\displaystyle\tau^{+}\cong I^{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}(\tau).

Claim 1:

I^{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}(\pi)\cong D^{L}_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ I^{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}(\tau)
.

Claim 2:
There exists
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathrm{Mult}
such that

D^{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ I^{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}(\pi)\cong\nu\cdot\tau.

Suppose Claim 2 holds in the meanwhile. Then, rewriting Claim 2, we have:

\nu^{-1}\cdot I^{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}(\pi)\cong I_{\nu^{-1}\mathfrak{n}}(\tau).

Now, we have that
\mathrm{lev}(\nu^{-1}\cdot I^{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}(\pi))=\mathrm{lev}(D^{L}_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ I^{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}(\tau))=\mathrm{lev}(\tau^{+})=\mathrm{lev}(\tau)
, which checks the level condition. (Here the first equality follows from Claim 1, and the second equality follows from that all segments for
\tau^{+}
has at least of relative length
2
, also c.f. Lemma
28.2
below.) Since

\mathrm{lev}(\nu^{-1}\cdot I^{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}(\pi))=|\mathfrak{h}|,

(\nu^{-1}\cdot I^{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}(\pi))^{-}=D_{\nu^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{h}}\circ(\nu^{-1}\cdot I^{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}(\pi))=\nu^{-1}\cdot\pi.

Switching to the highest left derivative, we obtain the statement.

We now prove Claim 1. Since
\mathrm{lev}(I^{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}(\tau))=\mathrm{lev}(\tau)
(see (
17.12
)), using Propositions
17.2
and
9.1
multiple times, we have:

D^{L}_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ I^{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}(\tau)\cong I^{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}\circ D^{L}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\tau),

which is equivalent to Claim 1.

We now prove Claim 2. By the left version of Theorem
17.3
, we have a multisegment
\mathfrak{n}
such that

D^{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D^{L}_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ I^{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}(\tau)\cong{}^{-}(I^{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}(\tau))\cong\nu\cdot(I^{R}_{\mathfrak{h}}(\tau))^{-}\cong\nu\cdot(\tau^{+})^{-}\cong\nu\cdot\tau.

Combining with Claim 1, we have Claim 2.
∎

18.
Symmetry property of relevant pairs

We now prove a symmetry property for relevance. Using the symmetry property with Proposition
14.2
, we also see that it is not necessary to introduce some left-right terminologies for relevance.

Theorem 18.1
.

Let
\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Then
(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
is relevant if and only if
(\pi^{\prime},\pi)
is relevant.

Proof.

We only prove one direction and the other direction is similar. Since
(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
is relevant, there exist multisegments
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{n}
such that

D_{\mathfrak{m}}^{R}(\nu^{1/2}\cdot\pi)\cong D_{\mathfrak{n}}^{L}(\pi^{\prime}).

This implies that
I_{\mathfrak{n}}^{L}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}}^{R}(\nu^{1/2}\cdot\pi)\cong\pi^{\prime}
.

Step 1: Construct dual multisegments by double derivatives and double integrals.

Set
\widetilde{\pi}=\nu^{1/2}\cdot\pi
. By Theorem
17.4
, there exists a multisegment
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
such that

(*)\quad{}^{-}(I_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi}))\cong\widetilde{\pi}

and

(**)\quad\mathrm{lev}(I_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi}))=\mathrm{lev}(I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi})).

On the other hand, by Theorem
17.3
, there exists a multisegment
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
such that

(***)\quad D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}}(I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi}))\cong(I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi}))^{-}.

Step 2: Show a commutation using level preserving.

Write the segments in
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
in an ascending order:
\underline{\Delta}_{1},\ldots,\underline{\Delta}_{s}
. Let
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}_{j}=\left\{\underline{\Delta}_{1},\ldots,\underline{\Delta}_{j}\right\}
and let
\pi_{j}=I_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi})
and
\pi_{0}=I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi})
. Thus (**) implies that
\mathrm{lev}(I_{\underline{\Delta}_{j}}(\pi_{j-1}))=\mathrm{lev}(\pi_{j-1})
and so by Proposition
17.2
,
(\mathfrak{m},\underline{\Delta}_{j},\pi_{j-1})
is a strongly RdLi-commutative triple for all
j
. In particular, we have that

(18.13)

\displaystyle D_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}_{j}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi})\cong I_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi}),

where the isomorphism follows from repeatedly using Proposition
9.1
.

(The point of (
18.13
) is to exploit
(\star)
shown in Step 3 below.)

Step 3: Check strong commutation.

Claim:
Let
\omega=D_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi})
. For
i=1,\ldots,r
,
(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime},\omega)
is strongly RdLi-commutative.

Proof of claim:
By using (**) and Lemma
17.1
,

\mathfrak{hd}(\pi_{j})=\mathfrak{hd}(I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi})).

Thus,

\mathfrak{r}(\mathfrak{m},\pi_{j})=\mathfrak{r}(\mathfrak{m},I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi})).

Hence, by (the second assertion of) Theorem
17.3
,

(\star)\quad D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi_{j})\cong(\pi_{j})^{-}.

Now, by the left version of Lemma
8.6
,

\displaystyle\eta^{L}_{\underline{\Delta}_{j}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi_{j}))

\displaystyle\leq\eta^{L}_{\underline{\Delta}_{j}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}_{r-1}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi_{j}))

\displaystyle\leq\ldots

\displaystyle\leq\eta^{L}_{\underline{\Delta}_{j}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi_{j}))

\displaystyle\leq\eta^{L}_{\underline{\Delta}_{j}}(\pi_{j}),

On the other hand, by Proposition
17.2
, we have that

\eta^{L}_{\underline{\Delta}_{j}}((I_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi}))^{-})=\eta^{L}_{\underline{\Delta}_{j}}(I_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi})).

Thus, with
(\star)
, the above inequalities are equalities and so

\eta^{L}_{\underline{\Delta}_{j}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi}))=\eta^{L}_{\underline{\Delta}_{j}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi})).

Thus, we use Proposition
17.2
and Proposition
9.1
again, we have

\eta^{L}_{\underline{\Delta}_{j}}(D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\underline{\Delta}_{j}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}_{j-1}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi}))=\eta^{L}_{\underline{\Delta}_{j}}(I_{\underline{\Delta}_{j}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}_{j-1}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi})).

By Lemma
14.3
, we have that
(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},\underline{\Delta}_{j}^{\prime},D_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}_{j-1}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi})))
is a strongly RdLi-commutative triple. With
D_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}_{j-1}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi})=I_{\mathfrak{n}_{j-1}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi})
(see
18.13
), we have that
(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime},D_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi}))
is a strongly RdLi-commutative triple, proving the claim.

Recall that
\pi^{\prime}\cong D_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi})
by the relevance. Thus, Step 3 implies that
(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
is a strongly RdLi-commutative triple.

Step 4: Check the isomorphism condition.

To check
(\pi^{\prime},\pi)
is relevant, it remains to show that
\nu^{1/2}\cdot D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}(\pi^{\prime})\cong\pi
. To this end, we consider

\displaystyle\nu^{1/2}\cdot D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}(\pi^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cong\nu^{1/2}\cdot D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi})

\displaystyle\cong\nu^{1/2}\cdot D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi})

\displaystyle\cong\nu^{1/2}\cdot(I_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi}))^{-}

\displaystyle\cong\nu^{-1/2}\cdot{}^{-}(I_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\widetilde{\pi}))

\displaystyle\cong\nu^{-1/2}\cdot\widetilde{\pi}

\displaystyle\cong\pi

where the second isomorphism follows from Step 2, the third isomorphism follows from
(\star)
, the forth isomorphism follows from the highest derivatives of Zelevinsky
[
Ze80
]
, the fifth isomorphism follows from (*). This completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 18.2
.

Let
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Then
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
is a relevant pair if and only if
(\pi_{1}^{\vee},\pi_{2}^{\vee})
is a relevant pair.

Proof.

For a segment
\Delta=[a,b]_{\rho}
, let
\Delta^{\vee}=[-b,-a]_{\rho^{\vee}}
. For a multisegment
\mathfrak{m}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{k}\right\}
, let
\mathfrak{m}^{\vee}=\left\{\Delta_{1}^{\vee},\ldots,\Delta_{k}^{\vee}\right\}
. In general, we have

\eta_{\Delta}^{L}(\pi)=\eta_{\Delta^{\vee}}^{R}(\pi^{\vee}),

and
(I_{\Delta}^{R}(\pi))^{\vee}=I_{\Delta^{\vee}}^{L}(\pi^{\vee})
. This implies that
(\mathfrak{m}^{\vee},\mathfrak{n}^{\vee},\nu^{-1/2}\cdot\pi_{1}^{\vee})
is a strongly LdRi-commutative triple. Hence,
(\mathfrak{n}^{\vee},\mathfrak{m}^{\vee},\pi_{2}^{\vee})
is a strongly RdLi-commutative triple by Proposition
14.2
and

\nu^{1/2}\cdot D_{\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{m}^{\vee}}(\pi_{2}^{\vee})\cong\pi_{1}^{\vee}.

Thus
(\pi_{2}^{\vee},\pi_{1}^{\vee})
is relevant and so is
(\pi_{1}^{\vee},\pi_{2}^{\vee})
by Theorem
18.1
.

∎

Corollary 18.3
.

Let
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Then
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
is a relevant pair if and only if
(\theta(\pi_{1}),\theta(\pi_{2}))
is a relevant pair.

Proof.

It follows from a result of Gelfand-Kazhdan that
\theta(\pi_{1})\cong\pi_{1}^{\vee}
and
\theta(\pi_{2})\cong\pi_{2}^{\vee}
.
∎

Part IV
Proof of sufficiency of generalized relevance

For an overview of this part, see Section
5.1
. The first goal of this part is to compute a certain smallest derivative for achieving the relevance in Section
21
, for which we need tools in Sections
19
and
20
. Section
22
explains connections of branching laws with the smallest derivatives. Section
24
studies a construction of branching law from Rankin-Selberg integrals. Section
25
studies a BZ filtration and analyzes which layers could contribute a branching law by using the smallest derivatives. We prove the sufficiency of the relevance in Section
26
.

19.
\Delta
-reduced representations

19.1.
\Delta
-reduced representation

Lemma 19.1
.

(c.f.
[
Ch22+d
, Section 8.3]
)
Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Let
\Delta
be a segment. Let
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{mx}(\pi,\Delta)
. Suppose
