(\Delta,\mathfrak{n},\pi)
is a strongly RdLi-commutative triple. Then

(1)

(\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{n},\pi)
is also a strongly RdLi-commutative triple.

(2)

D_{\mathfrak{p}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi)
is R-
\Delta
-reduced.

Proof.

By the strong commutativity of
(\Delta,\mathfrak{n},\pi)
,

\eta_{\Delta}(\pi)=\eta_{\Delta}(I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi)).

This shows (2).

Let
\Delta^{\prime}
be a
\Delta
-saturated segment. Since both
\eta_{\Delta^{\prime}}(D_{\Delta}(\pi))
and
\eta_{\Delta^{\prime}}(D_{\Delta}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi))
are obtained by reducing the factor
\varepsilon_{\Delta}
by
1
and all other
\varepsilon_{\Delta^{\prime}}
unchanged, we still have that:

\eta_{\Delta^{\prime}}(D_{\Delta}(\pi))=\eta_{\Delta^{\prime}}(D_{\Delta}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi)).

Now by Proposition
9.1
, we have:

\eta_{\Delta^{\prime}}(D_{\Delta}(\pi))=\eta_{\Delta^{\prime}}(I_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\Delta}(\pi)).

and so we have the strong commutativity for
(\Delta^{\prime},\mathfrak{n},D_{\Delta}(\pi))
by Lemma
14.3
. Thus, we inductively have that
(\mathfrak{p}-\Delta,\mathfrak{n},D_{\Delta}(\pi))
is still strongly RdLi-commutative triple. Thus, combining to have
(\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{n},\pi)
to be a strongly RdLi-commutative triple by definitions. This proves (1).
∎

19.2.
Reduced triples

For segments
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
, we write
\Delta_{1}\prec^{R}\Delta_{2}
if either
b(\Delta_{1})<b(\Delta_{2})
; or
b(\Delta_{1})\cong b(\Delta_{2})
and
a(\Delta_{2})<a(\Delta_{1})
. We write
\Delta_{1}\preceq^{R}\Delta_{2}
if
\Delta_{1}\prec^{R}\Delta_{2}
or
\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}
. In particular, if
\Delta
is a
\preceq^{R}
-maximal segment in
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathrm{Mult}
, then
b(\Delta)
is
\leq
-maximal in
\left\{b(\widetilde{\Delta}):\widetilde{\Delta}\in\mathfrak{m}\right\}
and
\Delta
is the longest segment in
\mathfrak{m}_{b=b(\Delta)}
.

Definition 19.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathrm{Mult}
. Let
\Delta
be a segment. Let
\Delta
be a
\preceq^{R}
-maximal segment in
\mathfrak{m}
. We say that
(\mathfrak{m},\Delta,\pi)
is a
R-reduced triple
if
\eta_{\Delta}(D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi))=0
and
\mathfrak{m}
is minimal to
\pi
.

We first prove a slightly technical lemma. For some related representation-theoretic interpretation, see e.g.
[
Ch22+
, Proposition 6.4]
.

Lemma 19.3
.

Suppose
(\mathfrak{m},\Delta,\pi)
is a R-reduced triple. Let
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{mx}(\pi,\Delta)
. Then
\mathfrak{p}\subset\mathfrak{m}
.

Proof.

We write
\mathfrak{m}
in an ascending sequence:
\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{k}
. When there is only one segment, then
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{m}=\left\{\Delta\right\}
. Then we are done. We now proceed on the number of segments in
\mathfrak{m}
.

If
\Delta_{1}
is
\Delta
-saturated, then
\mathfrak{mx}(D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi),\Delta)=\mathfrak{mx}(\pi,\Delta)-\Delta_{1}=\mathfrak{p}-\Delta_{1}
. (This can be proved by either using removal sequence or some direct computations of geometric lemma.) Then, by induction on the number of segments in
\mathfrak{m}
, we have

\mathfrak{p}-\Delta_{1}\subset\mathfrak{m}-\Delta_{1}

as desired.

Suppose
\Delta_{1}
is not
\Delta
-saturated. Thus, we have
\eta_{\Delta}(D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi))=\eta_{\Delta}(\pi)
by considering the following cases:

(1)

If
b(\Delta_{1})
is not a
\nu
-integral shift of
b(\Delta)
, this case is simple.

(2)

If
b(\Delta_{1})<b(\Delta)
and
a(\Delta_{1})<a(\Delta)
, then, by the subsequent property (Lemma
15.6
),
\Delta_{1}+\Delta
is minimal to
\pi
. Then the equality follows by using minimality and Lemma
8.10
.

(3)

If
b(\Delta_{1})\cong b(\Delta)
, then one uses Proposition
8.8
(1).

(4)

If
b(\Delta_{1})<b(\Delta)
and
a(\Delta)\leq a(\Delta_{1})
, then one further considers two cases: let
\widetilde{\Delta}
be the first segment in the removal sequence for
(\Delta_{1},\mathfrak{hd}(\pi))

•

If
b(\widetilde{\Delta})<b(\Delta)
, then the equality follows from Proposition
8.9
(1);

•

If
b(\widetilde{\Delta})\geq b(\Delta)
, then Proposition
8.9
(2) gives a certain segment
\overline{\Delta}
. Then, by induction and using
\overline{\Delta}
-reducedness (from
\Delta
-reducedness) for
D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi)
and minimality of
\mathfrak{m}-\Delta_{1}
to
D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)
, we have that
\mathfrak{mx}(\overline{\Delta},D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi))\subset\mathfrak{m}-\Delta_{1}
. In particular,
\overline{\Delta}\in\mathfrak{m}
(by Proposition
8.9
(2)). Then, we have
\Delta_{1}+\overline{\Delta}
is minimal to
\pi
by the subsequent property (Lemma
15.6
) and the inequality in Proposition
8.9
(2) then contradicts to Lemma
8.10
. (In other words, this case could not happen.)

This implies
\mathfrak{mx}(\Delta,D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi))=\mathfrak{p}
. Then, by induction, we have

\mathfrak{p}\subset\mathfrak{m}-\Delta_{1}\subset\mathfrak{m}.

∎

20.
Completing to
\Delta
-reduced triples

Recall that the notion of reducedness and saturatedness is defined in Section
4.4
. For
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
and a segment
\Delta
, let
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{mx}(\pi,\Delta)
and one has an embedding

\pi\hookrightarrow D_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi)\times\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})

(see e.g.
[
Ch22+
, Section 4]
,
[
LM22
, Section 7]
). As also seen in
[
Ch22+b
]
, such decomposition is particularly useful in some reduction process. We first explain how to do such reduction in our context of strong commutativity.

20.1.
Reduced multisegments

Definition 20.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathrm{Mult}
and let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
with
D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi)\neq 0
. We say that
\mathfrak{n}
is the
minimized multisegment
for
(\mathfrak{m},\pi)
if
\mathfrak{n}
is minimal to
\pi
and
D_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi)\cong D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi)
. (Again, the minimality is shown in
[
Ch22+
]
.)

Definition 20.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathrm{Mult}
. Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
and let
\Delta
be a
\preceq^{R}
-maximal segment in
\mathfrak{m}
. We say that a
\Delta
-saturated multisegment
\mathfrak{r}
is
completing
(\mathfrak{m},\Delta,\pi)
if
D_{\mathfrak{m}+\mathfrak{r}}(\pi)
is
\Delta
-reduced.

We say that
\mathfrak{q}
is the
reduced multisegment for
(\mathfrak{m},\Delta,\pi)
if
\mathfrak{q}+\mathfrak{mx}(\pi,\Delta)
is the minimized multisegment for
(\mathfrak{m}+\mathfrak{r},\pi)
. (Note that the definition is well-defined by Lemma
19.3
.)

Example 20.3
.

Let
\pi=\mathrm{St}([0,4]+[1,3])
. Let
\mathfrak{m}=[0,2]+[1,3]
. The minimized multisegment
\mathfrak{n}
for
(\mathfrak{m},\pi)
is
[0,3]+[1,2]
. Let
\Delta=[0,3]
. Then
[3]
is completing
(\mathfrak{m},\Delta,\pi)
. The reduced multisegment for
(\mathfrak{m},\Delta,\pi)
is
\emptyset
.

Corollary 20.4
.

Let
(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n},\pi)
be a strongly RdLi-commutative triple. Let
\Delta
be a
\preceq^{R}
-maximal element in
\mathfrak{m}
. Let
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{mx}(\pi,\Delta)
and let
\mathfrak{q}
be the reduced multisegment for
(\mathfrak{m},\Delta,\pi)
. Then
(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{n},D_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi))
is a strongly RdLi-commutative triple.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{mx}(D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi),\Delta)
. Then, by Lemma
19.1
,
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{n},D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi))
is a strongly RdLi-commutative triple and so
(\mathfrak{s}+\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n},\pi)
is also a strongly RdLi-commutative triple. Thus, if
\mathfrak{s}+\mathfrak{m}
is not minimal to
\pi
, the closedness property in
[
Ch22+
]
implies that one can find a consecutive pair (see
[
Ch22+
]
) for doing intersection-union operation to get a new sequence, which still gives a strongly RdLi-commutative triple by Proposition
13.2
. Repeatedly using this reasoning, we have that
(\mathfrak{p}+\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{n},\pi)
is also a strongly RdLi-commutative triple. By Corollary
15.10
(or more directly by Corollary
15.8
), we then have that
(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{n},D_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi))
is also a strongly RdLi-commutative triple.
∎

20.2.
Producing a sequence of strong commutations

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Let
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathrm{Mult}
be minimal to
\pi
. Let
\mathfrak{q}_{0}=\mathfrak{m}
and let
\pi_{0}=\pi
. We recursively produce the following data: for
i\geq 1
,

(1)

Let
\Delta_{i}
be a
\preceq^{R}
-maximal segment in
\mathfrak{q}_{i-1}
.

(2)

Let
\mathfrak{p}_{i}=\mathfrak{mx}(\pi_{i-1},\Delta_{i})
and let
\mathfrak{q}_{i}
be a reduced multisegment for
(\mathfrak{q}_{i-1},\Delta_{i},\pi_{i-1})
.

(3)

Set
\pi_{i}=D_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}(\pi_{i-1})
.

The process terminates when
\mathfrak{q}_{k}=\emptyset
. Note that the data generated above depends on the choices of maximal segments in
\mathfrak{q}_{i-1}
. In the above data, we also let
\mathfrak{r}_{i}
be the multisegment for minimizing
(\mathfrak{q}_{i-1},\Delta_{i},\pi_{i-1})
. We shall call

(\mathfrak{r}_{1},\mathfrak{p}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{1},\pi_{1}),\ldots,(\mathfrak{r}_{k},\mathfrak{p}_{k},\mathfrak{q}_{k},\pi_{k})

to be a
sequence of minimized data
for
(\mathfrak{m},\pi)
.

Lemma 20.5
.

Let
(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n},\pi)
be a strongly RdLi-commutative triple. Denote a sequence of minimized data for
(\mathfrak{m},\pi)
by:

(\mathfrak{r}_{1},\mathfrak{p}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{1},\pi_{1}),\ldots,(\mathfrak{r}_{k},\mathfrak{p}_{k},\mathfrak{q}_{k},\pi_{k})

(with
\mathfrak{q}_{k}=\emptyset
). Then

(\mathfrak{q}_{1},\mathfrak{n},\pi_{1}),\ldots,(\mathfrak{q}_{k-1},\mathfrak{n},\pi_{k-1})

are strongly RdLi-commutative triples.

Proof.

This follows multiple uses of Corollary
20.4
.
∎

Lemma 20.6
.

We use the notations in the previous lemma. Then
l_{a}(\mathfrak{m})=\sum_{x}l_{a}(\mathfrak{p}_{x})-\sum_{y}l_{a}(\mathfrak{r}_{y})
.

Proof.

We have that
l_{a}(\mathfrak{m})+l_{a}(\mathfrak{r}_{1})=l_{a}(\mathfrak{p}_{1})+l_{a}(\mathfrak{q}_{1})
and
l_{a}(\mathfrak{q}_{i})+l_{a}(\mathfrak{r}_{i+1})=l_{a}(\mathfrak{p}_{i+1})+l_{a}(\mathfrak{q}_{i+1})
. Note that
l_{a}(\mathfrak{q}_{k})=0
. Combining the equations, we have the lemma.
∎

Lemma 20.7
.

Let
\tau_{0}=I_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi)
. Let
\tau_{i}=D_{\mathfrak{r}_{i}}\circ\ldots\circ D_{\mathfrak{r}_{1}}(\tau_{0})
. Then

\tau_{i}\cong I_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}(\pi_{i})\cong I_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}+\mathfrak{q}_{i}}(\pi_{i-1}).

Moreover,
\mathfrak{mx}(\Delta_{i},\tau_{i})=\emptyset
, where
\Delta_{i}
is the segment involved in the minimizing process.

Proof.

We shall prove inductively on
i
. By definition,
\tau_{i}=D_{\mathfrak{r}_{i}}(\tau_{i-1})
and so we have
\tau_{i}=D_{\mathfrak{r}_{i}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{q}_{i-1}}(\pi_{i-1})
.

We have the strong commutation for
(\mathfrak{r}_{i},\mathfrak{n},\pi_{i-1})
by Lemma
20.5
and so we have the strong commutation for
(\mathfrak{r}_{i},\mathfrak{n},D_{\mathfrak{q}_{i-1}}(\pi_{i-1}))
by Lemma
19.1
. Thus
\tau_{i}=I_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{r}_{i}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{q}_{i-1}}(\pi_{i-1})
and so, by
\mathfrak{r}_{i}+\mathfrak{q}_{i-1}=\mathfrak{p}_{i}+\mathfrak{q}_{i}
, we have:

\tau_{i}=I_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}(\pi_{i-1}).

Now, by Lemma
15.6
,

\tau_{i}=I_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}(\pi_{i-1})=I_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}(\pi_{i}).

It remains to prove the last assertion. Note that
\mathfrak{mx}(\Delta_{i},D_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}(\pi_{i-1}))=\mathfrak{p}_{i}
and so

\mathfrak{mx}(\Delta_{i},I_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}(\pi_{i-1}))=\mathfrak{p}_{i}

by the subsequent property of Corollary
15.9
and Theorem
10.3
. This implies that

\mathfrak{mx}(\Delta_{i},I_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}(\pi_{i-1}))=\mathfrak{mx}(\Delta_{i},D_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}\circ I_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}(\pi_{i-1}))=\emptyset,

where the equality follows from Proposition
9.1
. Now the last assertion follows from the above expression of
\tau_{i}
.
∎

The following lemma follows from Frobenius reciprocity:

Lemma 20.8
.

We use the notations above. Let
\tau_{i}=I_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}(\tau_{i-1})
. Then

\pi_{i-1}\hookrightarrow\pi_{i}\times\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}_{i}),\quad\tau_{i-1}\hookrightarrow\tau_{i}\times\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{r}_{i}).

21.
Smallest derivatives for a strongly commutative triple

We illustrate the idea of computing the smallest derivative in a segment case. Let
\pi^{\prime}=D_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)
. Our goal is to determine the smallest integer
i^{*}
such that
\mathrm{Hom}(\pi^{(i^{*})},{}^{(j)}\pi^{\prime})\neq 0
. Roughly speaking, one compares
\eta_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)
and
\eta_{\Delta_{1}}({}^{(j)}\pi^{\prime})\leq\eta_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi^{\prime})
. The
\eta_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi)
and
\eta_{\Delta_{1}}(\pi^{\prime})
are differed by
1
on the coordinate for
\varepsilon_{\Delta_{1}}
. Thus in order to get from
\pi
to
\pi^{\prime}
, one has to take at least
l_{a}(\Delta_{1})
-derivative. Such idea can be extended to a strongly commutative triple
(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\pi)
due to the combinatorial criteria (Definition
10.1
), and then to strongly commutative triples
(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n},\pi)
, but then one needs tools from Section
20
.

21.1.
Leibniz’s rule

So far, we mainly deal with the derivatives
D_{\Delta}
. We now start to discuss more on BZ derivatives. Again, a standard tool is the geometric lemma, which we shall also refer to Leibniz’s rule.

For a
G_{n_{1}}
-representation
\pi_{1}
and a
G_{n_{2}}
-representation
\pi_{2}
, Leibniz’s rule asserts that
(\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2})^{(i)}
(resp.
{}^{(i)}(\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2})
) admits a filtration with layers isomorphic to

\pi_{1}^{(i_{1})}\times\pi_{2}^{(i_{2})},\quad\mbox{(resp. ${}^{(i_{1})}\pi_{1}\times{}^{(i_{2})}\pi_{2}$)}

where
i_{1},i_{2}
run for all non-negative integers satisfying
i_{1}+i_{2}=i
.

21.2.
A lemma

Lemma 21.1
.

Let
\Delta
be a segment. Let
\omega\in\mathrm{Irr}
be
\Delta
-reduced and let
\tau\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Let
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathrm{Mult}
be
\Delta
-saturated. Let
\lambda\in\mathrm{Alg}(G_{n})
. Suppose
\mathrm{Hom}(\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})\times\lambda,\omega\times\tau)\neq 0
. Then
l_{a}(\mathfrak{p})\leq n(\tau)
. Moreover, the inequality is strict if there exists
\rho
in
\mathrm{csupp}(\tau)
, but not in
\mathrm{csupp}(\mathfrak{p})
.

Proof.

Applying Frobenius reciprocity, we have that:

\mathrm{Hom}(\pi\boxtimes\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}),(\omega\times\tau)_{N_{n(\pi),l_{a}(\mathfrak{p})}})\neq 0.

Since
\omega
is
\Delta
-reduced, the only layer in the geometric lemma that can contribute the Hom is
\omega\dot{\times}^{1}(\tau_{N_{l_{a}(\mathfrak{p})}})
(c.f.
[
Ch22+d
, Lemma 10.2]
). But this then implies the required inequality.

The strict part of the inequality follows from
\mathrm{csupp}(\mathfrak{p})\subset\mathrm{csupp}(\tau)
for getting a contribution of that layer to a non-zero Hom.
∎

Proposition 21.2
.

Let
(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n},\pi)
be a strongly RdLi-commutative triple. Let
s_{1}=l_{a}(\mathfrak{m})
, let
s_{2}=l_{a}(\mathfrak{n})
. Let
\tau=I_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi)
. Let
n=n(\pi)
. Then
n(\tau)=n-s_{1}+s_{2}
. Then the smallest integer
i
such that

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n-i}}(\pi^{(i)},{}^{(i-s_{1}+s_{2})}\tau)\neq 0

is
l_{a}(\mathfrak{m})
.

Proof.

We first remark that
\mathfrak{n}
does not play much role in the proof, thanks to the properties of strongly RdLi-commutative triples.

Let a sequence of minimized data for
(\mathfrak{m},\pi)
be:

(\mathfrak{r}_{1},\mathfrak{p}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{1},\pi_{1}),\ldots,(\mathfrak{r}_{k},\mathfrak{p}_{k},\mathfrak{q}_{k},\pi_{k}).

Let
n^{\prime}=n(\tau)
. Let
\Pi=\Pi_{i-s_{1}+s_{2}}
. We first rewrite the inequality, by using the second adjointness, as:

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n^{\prime}}}(\pi^{(i)}\times\Pi,\tau)\neq 0.

Then, using Lemma
20.8
(the reducedness part of
\tau_{1}
follows from Lemma
20.7
),

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n^{\prime}}}((\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}_{1})\times\pi_{1})^{(i)}\times\Pi,\tau_{1}\times\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{r}_{1}))\neq 0.

Thus, there exists
i_{1}
such that

\mathrm{Hom}(\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}_{1})^{(i_{1})}\times\pi_{1}^{(i-i_{1})}\times\Pi,\tau_{1}\times\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{r}_{1}))\neq 0

Then we have that
i_{1}\geq l_{a}(\mathfrak{p}_{1})-l_{a}(\mathfrak{r}_{1})
by Lemma
21.1
.

Now, by Frobenius reciprocity again, we have:

\mathrm{Hom}(\pi_{1}^{(i-i_{1})}\times\Pi\boxtimes\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}_{1})^{(i_{1})},(\tau_{1}\times\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{r}_{1}))_{N}),

where
N=N_{l_{a}(\mathfrak{p}_{1})-i_{1}}
. Using the reducedness of
\tau_{1}
, we then have that:

\mathrm{Hom}(\pi_{1}^{(i-i_{1})}\times\Pi\boxtimes\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}_{1})^{(i_{1})},\tau_{1}\dot{\times}^{1}(\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{r}_{1})_{N_{l_{a}(\mathfrak{p}_{1})-i_{1}}})\neq 0.

Using adjointness, we then have that:

\mathrm{Hom}(\pi_{1}^{(i-i_{1})}\times\Pi,\tau_{1}\times\kappa_{1})\neq 0,

for some
\kappa_{1}
in
\mathrm{Alg}(G_{l_{a}(\mathfrak{r}_{1})-l_{a}(\mathfrak{p}_{1})+i_{1}})
.

We now use Lemma
20.8
again to obtain:

\mathrm{Hom}((\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}_{2})\times\pi_{2})^{(i-i_{1})}\times\Pi,\tau_{2}\times\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{r}_{2})\times\kappa_{1})\neq 0.

With the same reasoning as above, we have that: there exists

l_{a}(\mathfrak{r}_{2})+l_{a}(\mathfrak{r}_{1})-(l_{a}(\mathfrak{p})-i_{1})\geq l_{a}(\mathfrak{p}_{2})-i_{2},

equivalently

i_{2}\geq l_{a}(\mathfrak{p}_{2})-l_{a}(\mathfrak{r}_{2})+l_{a}(\mathfrak{p}_{1})-l_{a}(\mathfrak{r}_{1})-i_{1}

such that

\mathrm{Hom}(\pi_{2}^{(i-i_{1}-i_{2})}\times\Pi,\tau_{2}\times\kappa_{2})\neq 0

for some
\kappa_{2}\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{l_{a}(\mathfrak{r}_{2})+l_{a}(\mathfrak{r}_{1})-l_{a}(\mathfrak{p}_{1})-l_{a}(\mathfrak{p}_{2})+i_{1}+i_{2}})
.

Now inductively, we have that:

i_{k}\geq\sum_{x}l_{a}(\mathfrak{p}_{x})-\sum_{y}l_{a}(\mathfrak{r}_{y})-i_{1}-\ldots-i_{k-1}.

Thus,
i\geq i_{1}+\ldots+i_{k}\geq l_{a}(\mathfrak{m})
by Lemma
20.6
.

∎

Corollary 21.3
.

Let
(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n},\pi)
be a strongly RdLi-commutative triple. Let
(\mathfrak{r}_{1},\mathfrak{p}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{1},D_{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}(\pi))
be the first term of a sequence of minimized data for
(\mathfrak{m},\pi)
in Section
20.2
. Let
\tau=I_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi)
. Suppose

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n-i^{\prime}-i^{\prime\prime}}}(\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})^{(i^{\prime})}\times(D_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi))^{(i^{\prime\prime})},{}^{(i-s_{1}+s_{2})}\tau)\neq 0

for some
i^{\prime}>l_{a}(\mathfrak{p}_{1})-l_{a}(\mathfrak{r}_{1})
and some
i^{\prime\prime}
with
i^{\prime}+i^{\prime\prime}=i
. Then
i^{\prime}+i^{\prime\prime}>l_{a}(\mathfrak{m})
.

Proof.

It follows from the previous proof that

i_{k}\geq l_{a}(\mathfrak{p}_{k})-\left(\sum l_{a}(\mathfrak{r}_{x})-\sum_{y=1}^{k-1}(l_{a}(\mathfrak{p}_{y})-i_{y})\right)

If
k=1
, there is nothing to prove. Now, for
k\geq 2
, our choice guarantees that
b(\Delta)\not\geq\rho
for any
\Delta\in\mathfrak{q}_{k}
. Thus the inequality is strict by looking at a cuspidal representation in
\mathrm{csupp}(\kappa_{k})
(see the notations in Proposition
21.2
and
\kappa_{k}
is defined analogously) which contains
b(\Delta)
.
∎

22.
Characterizing the supporting layer in BZ filtration

22.1.
Multiplicity-one results

We first recall the following multiplicity one result:

Theorem 22.1
.

[
Ch23
, Theorem 1.1]

Let
\lambda
be a standard module of
G_{n+1}
and let
\lambda^{\prime}
be a standard module of
G_{n}
. Then

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\cong\mathbb{C}.

This immediately gives the following:

Corollary 22.2
.

Let
\omega\in\mathrm{Alg}(G_{n+1})
be a quotient of
\lambda
and let
\omega^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Alg}(G_{n})
be a quotient of
\lambda^{\prime}
. Then

\mathrm{dim}~\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\omega,\omega^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\leq 1.

When
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
are irreducible, this in particular recovers the multiplicity one theorem
[
AGRS10
]
(see
[
Ch23
]
).

Definition 22.3
.

(1)

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Alg}(G_{n+1})
or
\in\mathrm{Alg}(M_{n+1})
and let
\pi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Alg}(G_{n})
. Let
f
be a non-zero element in
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
. We say that the
right layer supporting
f
is
i^{*}
(or
i^{*}
-th) if
i^{*}
is the largest integer such that

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\Lambda_{i^{*}-1}(\pi),\pi^{\prime})\neq 0.

We denote such integer
i^{*}
by
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(f)
.

(2)

Let
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
be standard modules of
G_{n+1}
and
G_{n}
respectively. Let
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
be (not necessarily irreducible) quotients of
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
. Suppose
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\omega,\omega^{\prime\vee})\neq 0
. We define
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\omega,\omega^{\prime}{}^{\vee})=\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(f)
for the unique (up to a scalar) non-zero element
f
in
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\omega,\omega^{\prime}{}^{\vee})
, see Corollary
22.2
.

(3)

For
\pi\in\mathrm{Alg}(G_{n+1})
or
\in\mathrm{Alg}(M_{n+1}^{t})
. One defines the left layer supporting
f
analogously. Then one also defines the
left layer supporting the branching law
and
\mathcal{L}_{lBL}
analogously by using the filtration
{}_{i}\Lambda(\pi)
.

As hinted from
[
CPS17
, Page 137]
, for generic representations, the Whittaker functions from those layers in Definition
22.3
contribute poles of
L
-functions.

For auxiliary results, we shall only prove the right version and we shall sometimes drop the term ’right’ if there is no confusion.

22.2.
Supporting layer in terms of BZ derivatives

Recall that a standard module is discussed in Section
2.1
.

Lemma 22.4
.

Let
\pi
be a standard module of
G_{n}
. Then
\pi^{(i)}
admits a filtration whose successive subquotients are also standard modules.

Proof.

We have that
\pi\cong\lambda(\mathfrak{m})
for some multisegment
\mathfrak{m}
. We write the segments in
\mathfrak{m}
as
\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{r}
such that
b(\Delta_{k})\not\leq b(\Delta_{l})
for any
k<l
. By using the Leibniz’s rule,
\pi^{(i)}
admits a filtration whose successive subquotients are

\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{1})^{(i_{1})}\times\ldots\times\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{r})^{(i_{r})},

with
i_{1}+\ldots+i_{r}=i
. Each
\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{k})^{(i_{k})}
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{St}({}^{(i_{k})}\Delta_{k})
(here
{}^{(i_{k})}\Delta_{k}
is a segment from
\Delta_{k}
by truncating cuspidal representations on the left), and so the subquotient is still a standard module since
b({}^{(i_{k})}\Delta_{k})\not\leq b({}^{(i_{l})}\Delta_{l})
for any
k<l
(drop the term if
{}^{(i_{k})}\Delta
is empty). This proves the lemma.
∎

The following homological property is the key for deducing a characterization of branching laws.

Lemma 22.5
.

Let
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
be standard modules of
G_{n}
and
G_{m}
. Let
k=n-m
. For
i,i+k\geq 0
, label the standard modules in the filtration (see Lemma
22.4
) of
\lambda^{(i+k)}
by
\kappa_{1},\ldots,\kappa_{p}
and label the standard modules in the filtration (see Lemma
22.4
) of
\lambda^{\prime}{}^{(i)}
by
\kappa_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\kappa_{q}^{\prime}
.

(1)

If
\kappa_{s}\cong\theta(\kappa_{t}^{\prime})
for some
s,t
, then
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{m-i}}(\lambda^{(i+k)},{}^{(i)}(\lambda^{\prime}{}^{\vee}))\neq 0
.

(2)

If
\kappa_{s}\not\cong\theta(\kappa_{t}^{\prime})
for any
s,t
, then, for any
j
,

\mathrm{Ext}^{j}_{G_{m-i}}(\lambda^{(i+k)},{}^{(i)}(\lambda^{\prime}{}^{\vee})=0.

Proof.

Recall that for two standard modules
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
of
G_{k}
,
\mathrm{Ext}^{j}_{G_{k}}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}^{\vee})=0
if
\lambda_{1}\not\cong\theta(\lambda_{2})
(see
[
Ch22+b
, Lemma 5.5]
, which is stated and shown for Zelevinsky classification, but one for Langlands classification can be proved similarly or deduced by applying Bernstein’s cohomological duality). Now, the lemma follows from a standard application of long exact sequences.
∎

Corollary 22.6
.

Let
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
be standard modules of
G_{n+1}
and
G_{n}
. Let
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
be (not necessarily irreducible) quotients of
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
respectively such that
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\neq 0
. Let
i^{*}=\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}{}^{\vee})
. Then, for
i<i^{*}
,

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\Sigma_{i}(\pi),\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee})=0,\quad\mbox{equivalently}\quad\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n+1-i}}(\pi^{[i]},{}^{(i-1)}(\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee}))=0,

and

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\Sigma_{i^{*}}(\pi),\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\cong\mathbb{C},\quad\mbox{equivalently}\quad\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n+1-i^{*}}}(\pi^{[i^{*}]},{}^{(i^{*}-1)}(\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee}))\cong\mathbb{C}.

In particular,
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\pi,\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee})=i^{*}
.

Proof.

Set
\pi_{i}=\Lambda_{i}(\pi)
and
\lambda_{i}=\Lambda_{i}(\lambda)
.

Claim:
For
i<i^{*}
,
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi_{i},\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\cong\mathbb{C}
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\Sigma_{i}(\pi),\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\cong 0
; and
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\lambda_{i},\lambda^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\cong\mathbb{C}
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\Sigma_{i}(\lambda),\lambda^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\cong 0
.

Proof of Claim:
We consider the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\Sigma_{i}(\pi),\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi_{i-1},\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{i}^{\prime\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi_{i},\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\Sigma_{i}(\lambda),\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\lambda_{i-1},\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{i}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\lambda_{i},\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\Sigma_{i}(\lambda),\lambda^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\lambda_{i-1},\lambda^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{i}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\lambda_{i},\lambda^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{G_{n}}(\Sigma_{i}(\lambda),\lambda^{\prime}{}^{\vee})}

When
i=0
,
\pi_{0}=\pi
and so it follows from the assumption and Theorem
22.1
. We now consider
i\geq 1
. By considering the middle line, the injectivity and assumptions imply all Hom’s in the middle are one-dimensional. Then from the assumption on
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}{}^{\vee})=i^{*}
,
f_{i}
is non-zero and so must be injective by one-dimensionality (from induction). Then,
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\Sigma_{i}(\lambda),\lambda^{\prime}{}^{\vee})=0
. By Lemma
22.5
, the last
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}
is zero and so the map
f_{i}
is an isomorphism and so

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\lambda_{i},\lambda^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\cong\mathbb{C},

and then by the commutativity diagram, we also have
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\lambda_{i},\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\cong\mathbb{C}
and
f^{\prime}_{i}
is also an isomorphism. Now we repeat the argument and obtain that
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi_{i},\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\cong\mathbb{C}
and
f_{i}^{\prime\prime}
is an isomorphism. This then also implies that
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\Sigma_{i}(\pi),\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee})=0
. This proves the claim.

We now return to the proof. It remains to establish the case for
i=i^{*}
. To this end, we consider the following exact sequence:

0\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\Sigma_{i^{*}}(\pi),\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi_{i^{*}-1},\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi_{i^{*}},\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee}).

The last term is zero by
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\lambda_{i^{*}},\lambda^{\prime}{}^{\vee})=0
. Thus, the first Hom is isomorphic to the second one, and then the corollary follows from the claim.

We finally remark that the last assertion also follows from Lemma
23.2
below.
∎

Corollary 22.7
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n+1})
and let
\pi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n})
. Suppose
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\neq 0
. Let
i^{*}
be the smallest integer such that

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\Sigma_{i^{*}}(\pi),\pi^{\prime})\neq 0,\mbox{ equivalently }\quad\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n+1-i^{*}}}(\pi^{[i^{*}]},{}^{(i^{*}-1)}\pi^{\prime})\neq 0.

Then
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})=i^{*}
.

Proof.

Let
i^{\#}=\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
. The zeroness part for
i<i^{\#}
of Corollary
22.6
implies
i^{*}\geq i^{\#}
. The multiplicity one part of Corollary
22.6
then implies
i^{\#}\geq i^{*}
.
∎

23.
Some results on computing the layer supporting a branching law

23.1.
Submodule and quotient constraints

We first have a submodule constraint:

Lemma 23.1
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Alg}(G_{n+1})
and let
\pi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Alg}(G_{n})
. Let
f
be a non-zero element in
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
. Suppose
\pi
admits a filtration as
M_{n+1}
-module:

0\rightarrow\omega\rightarrow\pi\rightarrow\omega^{\prime}\rightarrow 0.

Suppose
f|_{\omega}\neq 0
. Then
\mathcal{L}_{lBL}(f)\geq\mathcal{L}_{lBL}(f|_{\omega})
.

Proof.

Let
\pi_{i}=\Lambda_{i}(\pi)
, the BZ filtration of
\pi
. Set
\omega_{i}=\omega\cap\pi_{i}
, which gives the BZ filtration of
\omega
. For any
i
, we have the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\omega_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{h_{i}}
\textstyle{\pi_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k_{i}}
\textstyle{\omega\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{\pi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\pi^{\prime}}

Suppose
(f\circ\iota)\circ h_{i}\neq 0
. Then the commutative diagram implies
f\circ k_{i}\neq 0
. This implies the lemma.
∎

We next have a quotient constraint:

Lemma 23.2
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Alg}(G_{n+1})
and let
\widetilde{\pi}
be a quotient of
\pi
with the quotient map
q
. Let
\pi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Alg}(G_{n})
. Suppose there exists a non-zero map
f:\widetilde{\pi}\rightarrow\pi^{\prime}
. Then

\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(f\circ q)=\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(f).

Proof.

Set
\pi_{i}=\Lambda_{i}(\pi)
for simplicity. Let
\widetilde{\pi}_{i}=\mathrm{im}~q
. We consider the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\pi_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{i}}
\scriptstyle{q_{i}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\pi}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\iota}_{i}}
\textstyle{\pi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\pi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\pi^{\prime}}

Hence, if
f\circ q\circ\iota_{i}\neq 0
, the commutative diagram gives that
f\circ\widetilde{\iota}_{i}\neq 0
. Conversely, if
f\circ\widetilde{\iota}_{i}\neq 0
, then
f\circ\widetilde{\iota}_{i}\circ q_{i}\neq 0
and so
f\circ q\circ\iota_{i}\neq 0
. Thus we now have the lemma.

∎

23.2.
Refined standard trick for the supporting layer

Lemma 23.3
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n+1})
and let
\pi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n})
. Suppose there exists a
\rho\in\mathrm{Irr}^{c}
good to
\nu^{1/2}\pi
and
\rho
is
\leq
-minimal in
\mathrm{csupp}(\pi^{\prime})
. Let
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{mxpt}(\pi^{\prime},\rho)
(see Definition
8.2
). Let
\sigma\in\mathrm{Irr}^{c}(G_{l_{a}(\mathfrak{p})})
be good to
\nu^{1/2}\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
. Then

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\sigma\times D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi^{\prime}))\cong\mathbb{C}

and
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\pi,\sigma\times D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi^{\prime}))=i^{*}
if and only if

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})\times D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi^{\prime}))\cong\mathbb{C}

and
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\pi,\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})\times D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi^{\prime}))=i^{*}
.

Proof.

We only prove the only if direction and the if direction is very similar. We first prove the Hom result. The argument is more standard now and so we are slightly sketchy. By a duality (see
[
Ch22
, Proposition 4.1]
), we have that:

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n+1}}(\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})^{\vee}\times\sigma^{\prime}\times D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi^{\prime})^{\vee},\pi^{\vee})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})\times D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi^{\prime}))

for some cuspidal representation
\sigma^{\prime}
in
G_{2}
. Thus one then applies Proposition
3.2
on
(\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})\times\sigma^{\prime})\times D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi^{\prime})^{\vee},
and the only possible layer (by an argument of comparing cuspidal supports) that can contribute the Hom is the bottom layer of certain Rankin-Selberg model involving
D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi^{\prime})^{\vee}
taking the form:

(23.14)

\displaystyle\mathrm{RS}_{l_{a}(\mathfrak{p})}(D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi^{\prime})^{\vee})

(see Section
3.3
).

On the other hand, if one considers
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\sigma\times D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi^{\prime})^{\vee})
, then one applies a similar duality and then the layer that can contribute the Hom is again of such form. Thus, now we have

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\sigma\times D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi^{\prime}))\cong\mathbb{C}\Longrightarrow\ \mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})\times D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi^{\prime}))\cong\mathbb{C}.

For convenience, for representations
\omega_{1}\in\mathrm{Alg}(G_{k})
and
\omega_{2}\in\mathrm{Alg}(G_{l})
of finite lengths, set

(23.15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{smD}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=\mathrm{min}\left\{i:\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{k-i}}(\omega_{1}^{[i]},{}^{(i-k+l)}\omega_{2})\neq 0\right\}.

We now turn to the part of the layer supporting the branching law. By using Corollary
22.6
,
i^{*}=\mathcal{L}_{smD}(\pi,\sigma\times D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi^{\prime}))
, and so a cuspidal consideration also implies that
i^{*}=\mathcal{L}_{smD}(\pi,D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi^{\prime}))
. This then, by a cuspidal consideration, also implies that
i^{*}=\mathcal{L}_{smD}(\pi,\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})\times D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi^{\prime}))
. Thus, now the lemma follows from Corollary
22.6
) (or some variants of Corollary
22.7
).
∎

Note that similar lines of arguments also prove Lemma
4.3
as well as its refinement analogue to the above one.

24.
Constructing branching laws via Ranking-Selberg integrals

In this section, we consider the Rankin-Selberg integrals and the work
[
CPS17
]
.

24.1.
A construction

Lemma 24.1
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n})
. Let
\Delta
be a segment of absolute length
k
. Let
\pi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n+k-1})
. Let
\sigma\in\mathrm{Irr}^{c}(G_{k})
such that
\sigma
is good to
\pi
and
\nu^{-1/2}\pi^{\prime}
. Suppose

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n+k-1}}(\sigma\times\pi,\pi^{\prime})\neq 0

with the
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\sigma\times\pi,\pi^{\prime})=i^{*}
. Then there exists a non-zero
f
in
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n+k-1}}(\mathrm{St}(\Delta)\times\pi,\pi^{\prime})
such that
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(f)\leq i^{*}
.

Proof.

Step 1: Construct a branching law

From the non-vanishing Hom hypothesis in the lemma and a variation of the standard trick (see Lemma
23.3
), we have that

(24.16)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n+k-1}}(\nu^{u_{0}}\mathrm{St}(\Delta)\times\pi,\pi^{\prime})\neq 0

for all except finitely many
u_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
.

Now we find standard representations
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
for
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee}
respectively:

\lambda:=\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{1})\times\ldots\times\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{r})\twoheadrightarrow\pi

and

\lambda^{\prime}:=\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{1}^{\prime})\times\ldots\times\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{s}^{\prime})\twoheadrightarrow\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee}.

Let
n_{k}=l_{a}(\Delta_{k})
for
k=1,\ldots,r
and let
n_{k}^{\prime}=l_{a}(\Delta_{k}^{\prime})
for
k=1,\ldots,s
.

Let
u=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{r})
be a
r
-tuple and let
v=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{s})
be a
s
-tuple. Let
\lambda_{u}
be the space of functions from
G_{n}
to
\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm u},q^{\pm v}]\otimes(\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{1})\boxtimes\ldots\boxtimes\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{r}))
satisfying

f(pg)=\nu(m_{1})^{u_{1}}\ldots\nu(m_{r})^{u_{r}}p.f(g),

where
p=\mathrm{diag}(m_{1},\ldots,m_{r})\cdot n
for
m_{i}\in G_{n_{i}}
and
n\in N_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{r}}
and
p
acts via the action on
\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{1})\boxtimes\ldots\boxtimes\mathrm{St}(\Delta_{r})
. We similarly define for
\lambda^{\prime}_{v}
.

The Rankin-Selberg integral
[
JPSS83
, Theorem 2.7]
, see Cogdell–Piatetski-Shapiro
[
CPS17
, Page 166]
and
[
CPS17
, Theorem 4.1]
, implies that there exists a non-zero
G_{n}
-equivariant linear functional

\mu_{u_{0},u,v}:(\nu^{u_{0}}\mathrm{St}(\Delta)\times\lambda_{u})\otimes\lambda^{\prime}_{v}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm u_{0}},q^{\pm u},q^{\pm v}].

(In
loc. cit.
, one has extra variable
q^{s}
and one specializes
s=1
in the Rankin-Selberg integral to get the
G_{n}
-equivariant.) Here
\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm u_{0}},q^{\pm u},q^{\pm v}]
is the space of polynomial functions over
q^{\pm u_{0}},q^{\pm u},q^{\pm v}
.

We similarly consider
\widetilde{\lambda}_{u_{0}}
to be all the smooth functions from
G_{n}
to
\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm u_{0}}]\otimes(\mathrm{St}(\Delta)\times\pi)
such that for
p=\begin{pmatrix}m_{1}&x\\
&m_{2}\end{pmatrix}\in P
,

f(pg)=\nu^{u_{0}}(m_{1})p.f(g),

where
p
is the action on the part
\mathrm{St}(\Delta)\boxtimes\pi
.

One then specializes
\mu_{u_{0},u,v}
to the parameters
u_{1}=\ldots=u_{r}=v_{1}=\ldots=v_{s}=0
. Then we obtain a non-zero linear functional

\mu_{u_{0}}:\widetilde{\lambda}_{u_{0}}\otimes\lambda^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm u_{0}}].

Now, we have an embedding

\mathrm{Hom}(\nu^{u_{0}}\mathrm{St}(\Delta)\times\pi,\pi^{\prime})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}(\nu^{u_{0}}\mathrm{St}(\Delta)\times\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}{}^{\vee}),

which is an isomorphism for infinitely many
u_{0}
by using the multiplicity one, see Corollary
22.2
, for both spaces (recall that the former space is non-zero by (
24.16
)). This implies that
\mu_{u_{0}}
vanishes on the kernel of the natural map

(\nu^{u_{0}}\mathrm{St}(\Delta)\times\lambda)\otimes\lambda^{\prime}\rightarrow(\nu^{u_{0}}\mathrm{St}(\Delta)\times\pi)\otimes\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee}

induced from (*) and (**), for infinitely many and so for all
u_{0}
. Thus now
\mu_{0}
descends to a linear functional

(\nu^{u_{0}}\mathrm{St}(\Delta)\times\pi)\otimes\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}(u_{0}).

Let
u^{*}\in\mathbb{C}
. Again, by multiplying a normalizing factor if necessary, it descends to a non-zero map from

\mu_{u^{*}}^{\prime}:(\nu^{u^{*}}\mathrm{St}(\Delta)\times\pi)\otimes\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}.

When
u^{*}=0
, we obtain a desired map in
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}((\mathrm{St}(\Delta)\times\pi)\otimes\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee},\mathbb{C})
.

Step 2: Check the layer supporting the branching law

Set the Bernstein-Zelevinsky filtration, as a
M_{n+k}
-representation, on
\widetilde{\lambda}_{u_{0}}
by

\omega_{i}=\Lambda_{i}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{u_{0}}).

It remains to show that the layer supporting the branching law from
\mu^{\prime}_{0}
is at most
i^{*}
i.e.

\mu_{0}^{\prime}(\omega_{i^{*}}\otimes\pi^{\prime\vee})=0

Let
u^{*}\in\mathbb{C}
. We now let
\widetilde{\lambda}_{u^{*}}=\nu^{u^{*}}\cdot\mathrm{St}(\Delta)\times\pi
, that is the specialization of
\widetilde{\lambda}_{u_{0}}
at
u^{*}
. Set

\omega^{*}_{i}=\Lambda_{i}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{u^{*}}).

Now, we define

\mathrm{sp}_{u^{*}}:\widetilde{\lambda}_{u_{0}}\otimes\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee}\rightarrow\widetilde{\lambda}_{u^{*}}\otimes\pi^{\prime\vee}

by specializing
u_{0}=u^{*}
.

Indeed, one has that, as a consequence of Proposition
32.3
(see
32.4
) and using that the exactness in the functoriality of Bernstein-Zelevinsky filtration, the specialization of
\omega_{i}
for
u^{*}=u_{0}
gives
\omega_{i}^{*}
. Thus, by tensoring with
\otimes\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee}
(which is exact),

(*)\quad\mathrm{sp}_{u^{*}}(\omega_{i}\otimes\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee})=\omega_{i}^{*}\otimes\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee}.

Now, by Lemma
23.3
and
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\sigma\times\pi,\pi^{\prime})=i^{*}
,we have that

\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\nu^{u^{*}}\mathrm{St}(\Delta)\times\pi,\pi^{\prime})=i^{*}

for infinitely many
u^{*}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mu_{u_{0}}(\omega_{i^{*}}\otimes\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee})|_{u_{0}=u^{*}}

\displaystyle=\mu_{u^{*}}^{\prime}(\mathrm{sp}_{u^{*}}(\omega_{i^{*}}\otimes\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee}))

\displaystyle=\mu_{u^{*}}^{\prime}(\omega_{i^{*}}^{*}\otimes\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee})

\displaystyle=0

for infinitely many
u^{*}
. But, this then implies that those equations also hold for all
u^{*}
, particularly when
u^{*}=0
. This now proves the lemma by Definition
22.3
.
∎

25.
The smallest derivative controlling some BZ-layers supporting branching laws

25.1.
Computation for a layer

We need the following slightly technical computation later:

Lemma 25.1
.

Let
\Delta
be a segment and let
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathrm{Mult}
be
\Delta
-saturated. Let
\rho^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}^{c}
such that
\rho^{\prime}\not\leq\widetilde{\rho}
for any
\widetilde{\rho}\in\mathrm{csupp}(\mathfrak{p})
or
\widetilde{\rho}\in\mathrm{csupp}(\tau)
. Let
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
be a strongly L-
\rho^{\prime}
-saturated multisegment (see Section
4.4
). Let
\tau\in\mathrm{Alg}(G_{n})
and let
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n^{\prime}})
, where
l_{a}(\mathfrak{p})+n-k=l_{a}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})+n^{\prime}
. Let
\mathfrak{r}=\mathfrak{mxpt}(\tau^{\prime},\Delta)
. Suppose
l_{a}(\mathfrak{p})\geq l_{a}(\mathfrak{r})
. Then

(1)

If
k<l_{a}(\mathfrak{p})-l_{a}(\mathfrak{r})
, then

\mathrm{Hom}(\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})^{(k)}\times\tau,\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})\times\tau^{\prime})=0.

(2)

If
k=l_{a}(\mathfrak{p})-l_{a}(\mathfrak{r})
, then

\mathrm{Hom}(\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})^{(k)}\times\tau,\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})\times\tau^{\prime})\cong\mathrm{Hom}(\tau,\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})\times D_{\mathfrak{r}}(\tau^{\prime}))

Proof.

One applies the second adjointness of Frobenius reciprocity to have that:

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})^{(k)}\times\tau,\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})\times\tau^{\prime})\cong\mathrm{Hom}(\tau\boxtimes\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})^{(k)},(\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})\times\tau^{\prime})_{N}),

where
N
is the corresponding unipotent radical from the parabolic induction. Now one applies the geometric lemma to analyse the layers.

We first consider (1). In such case, one can conclude that those related Hom’s are zero by either

•

using the cuspidal representation
b(\Delta^{\prime})
; or

•

using
D_{\mathfrak{t}}(\pi^{\prime})=0
if
\mathfrak{t}
is not a submultisegment of
\mathfrak{r}
(e.g. by using
[
Ch22+
, Corollary 7.23]
and the removal process in Section
8.4
) and so, in particular, when a multisegment
\mathfrak{t}
is of the form

\sum_{\Delta\in\mathfrak{p}}{}^{(j_{\Delta})}\Delta

with sum of
j_{\Delta}
equal to
l_{a}(\mathfrak{p})-k
.

Now we consider (2) and so
k=l_{a}(\mathfrak{p})-l_{a}(\mathfrak{r})
. Again, analysing the geometric layers, it reduces to compute

\mathrm{dim}~\mathrm{Hom}(\tau\boxtimes\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})^{(k)},\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})\dot{\times}^{1}\tau^{\prime}_{N}),

where
N=N_{l_{a}(\mathfrak{r})}
. By using
\mathfrak{mx}(\tau^{\prime},\Delta)
, the only composition factor of
\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})^{(k)}
that can contribute a non-zero Hom is
\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{r})
. Let
\zeta
be the indecomposable component in
\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})^{(k)}
that has the cuspidal support as
\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{r})
. It is possibly zero, but if it is non-zero,
\zeta
has a unique simple quotient isomorphic to
\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{r})
(see e.g.
[
Ch21
, Proposition 2.5 and Corollary 2.6]
). Thus, it further reduces to compute

\mathrm{dim}~\mathrm{Hom}(\tau\boxtimes\zeta,\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})\dot{\times}^{1}\tau^{\prime}_{N}).

On the other hand, since
\mathfrak{mx}(\tau,\Delta)=\mathfrak{r}
,
D_{\mathfrak{r}}(\tau)\boxtimes\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{r})
is a direct summand in
\tau_{N}
(see
[
Ch22+b
, Proposition 2.1]
) and no other composition factors in
\tau_{N}
takes the form
\omega\boxtimes\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{r})
. Hence, now with Künneth formula, we now reduce to compute:

\mathrm{dim}~\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\tau,\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})\times D_{\mathfrak{r}}(\tau^{\prime})).

This completes the proof.
∎

25.2.
Relation to the layer supporting the branching law

Lemma 25.2
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n+1})
and let
\pi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n})
. Suppose
(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
is relevant with the associated strongly commutative triple
(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n},\pi)
. Let
\Delta
be a
\preceq^{R}
-maximal element in
\mathfrak{m}
and let
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{mx}(\pi,\Delta)
. Suppose there exists a non-zero
f
in

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})\times D_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi),\pi^{\prime})

with
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(f)\leq l_{a}(\mathfrak{m})
. Let
\mathfrak{r}
be the multisegment completing
(\mathfrak{m},\Delta,\pi)
. Let
\omega
be the submodule containing all the layers

\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})^{[k]}\times\mathrm{RS}_{k-2}(D_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi))

for
k>l_{a}(\mathfrak{p})-l_{a}(\mathfrak{r})
. Then
f|_{\omega}=0
. (Note that
\omega
is possibly zero, which happens when
\mathfrak{r}=\emptyset
.)

Proof.

Suppose not. Then there exists some submodule
\omega^{\prime}
of
\omega
(from the filtration in Proposition
3.2
) such that we have the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\omega^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{\omega\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\pi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}^{[k^{*}]}\times\mathrm{RS}_{k^{*}-2}(\pi_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}}

for some
k^{*}>l_{a}(\mathfrak{p})-l_{a}(\mathfrak{r})
and some
\widetilde{f}
; and the composition
\widetilde{f}\circ s\neq 0
. Set
\tau=D_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi)
. Now,
\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})^{[k^{*}]}\times\mathrm{RS}_{k^{*}-2}(\tau)
admits Bernstein-Zelevinsky filtration whose successive subquotients of the form:

\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})^{[k^{*}]}\times\tau^{[l]}\times\Pi_{k^{*}+l-1}

with
l
varying. Thus, in order to get
\mathrm{Hom}(\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})^{[k^{*}]}\times\tau^{[l]}\times\Pi_{k^{*}+l-1},\pi^{\prime})\neq 0
, we must have that
k^{*}+l>l_{a}(\mathfrak{m})
by Corollary
21.3
. Then,
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\widetilde{f})>l_{a}(\mathfrak{m})
. Then, by Lemma
23.2
,
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\widetilde{f}\circ s)>l_{a}(\mathfrak{m})
. By Lemma
23.1
,
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(f)>l_{a}(\mathfrak{m})
, giving a contradiction.
∎

26.
Proof of sufficiency of the generalized relevant pairs

26.1.
Strategy

We shall try to extract the branching law from some reducible modules.

Lemma 26.1
.

Let
\tau
and
\omega
be
G_{n}
-modules with short exact sequences:

0\rightarrow\tau_{2}\rightarrow\tau\rightarrow\tau_{1}\rightarrow 0

and

0\rightarrow\omega_{1}\rightarrow\omega\rightarrow\omega_{2}\rightarrow 0.

Suppose the following conditions hold:

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\tau,\omega)\cong\mathbb{C},

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\tau,\omega_{1})\cong\mathbb{C},\quad\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\tau_{1},\omega)\cong\mathbb{C}.

Then
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\tau_{1},\omega_{1})\cong\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

It is clear that
\mathrm{dim}~\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\tau_{1},\omega_{1})\leq 1
. Suppose the dimension is zero to derive a contradiction. Let
f\in\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\tau_{1},\omega)
be non-zero. Then
\mathrm{im}~f\not\subset\omega_{1}
. Let
f^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\tau,\omega_{1})
be non-zero. Then
\mathrm{im}~f^{\prime}\subset\omega_{1}
. Both
f
and
f^{\prime}
can lift to a map in
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\tau,\omega)
. But
f
and
f^{\prime}
is not a scalar multiple of each other. This implies that
\mathrm{dim}~\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\tau,\omega)\geq 2
.
∎

26.2.
Zero relative rank case

We shall do an induction and need the following definition:

Definition 26.2
.

Let
\pi_{1}\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n+1})
and let
\pi_{2}\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n})
. A cuspidal representation
\rho
in
\mathrm{csupp}(\pi_{1})
is said to be
relevant
to
\pi_{2}
if
\nu^{1/2}\rho\in\mathrm{csupp}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\pi_{2})
. The
relevant relative rank
of
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
, denoted by
\mathcal{RR}(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
, is

\sum_{\sigma\in\mathrm{csupp}(\pi_{1}):\mbox{ relevant to $\pi_{2}$}}n(\sigma)+\sum_{\sigma\in\mathrm{csupp}(\pi_{2}):\mbox{ relevant to $\pi_{1}$}}n(\sigma).

We first prove a basic case:

Lemma 26.3
.

Let
\pi_{1}\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n+1})
and let
\pi_{2}\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n})
. Suppose the relative rank of
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
is
0
. Then the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})\neq 0
;

(2)

both
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are generic;

(3)

(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
is
(n+1)
-relevant;

(4)

(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
is relevant.

Proof.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1) is well-known
[
JPSS83
]
. (1)
\Rightarrow
(2) follows from an application of BZ filtration and comparing cuspidal supports.

(2)
\Leftrightarrow
(3) is straightforward from definitions and (3)
\Leftrightarrow
(4) follows by definitions and a comparison of cuspidal supports.
∎

26.3.
Proof of sufficiency

Theorem 26.4
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n+1})
and let
\pi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n})
. Suppose
(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
is
i^{*}
-relevant. Then

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\neq 0

and
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})=i^{*}
.

Proof.

Step 1 Inductive setup.
We shall prove by an induction on the relative rank. When the relative rank
\mathcal{RR}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
is zero, it is shown in Lemma
26.3
.

We now consider the relative rank is non-zero. By the relevance condition, there exists a minimal strongly RdLi-commutative triple
(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n},\nu^{1/2}\cdot\pi)
such that

D_{\mathfrak{m}}^{R}(\nu^{1/2}\cdot\pi)\cong D^{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi^{\prime}).

By definition, we have
i^{*}=l_{a}(\mathfrak{m})
. Note that the statement
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})=i^{*}
would follow from Proposition
21.2
and Corollary
22.7
once we show that
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\neq 0
.

Now we consider two cases:

Case 1:
There exists a
\leq
-minimal element
\rho^{\prime}\in\mathrm{csupp}(\pi^{\prime})
such that
\rho^{\prime}\not\in\mathrm{csupp}(\nu^{1/2}\cdot\pi)
; and
\rho^{\prime}
is relevant to
\pi^{\prime}
. Let
(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n},\pi)
be the minimal strongly RdLi-commutative triple that determines the relevance of
(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
.

Step 2: Compute
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\omega)
via the standard trick (
\omega
is defined below)

Let
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{mxpt}^{L}(\pi^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
. By using
D^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\nu^{1/2}\cdot\pi)\cong D^{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi^{\prime})
, we must have
D^{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi^{\prime})
to be strongly L-
\rho^{\prime}
-reduced. Then, we have
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{n}
by (the left version of) Lemma
19.3
. Then, by Corollary
15.9
, we still have that
(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n}-\mathfrak{p}^{\prime},\pi)
is still a strongly RdLi-commutative triple and

D^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\nu^{1/2}\cdot\pi)\cong D^{L}_{\mathfrak{n}-\mathfrak{p}}\circ D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi^{\prime}).

Thus
(\pi,D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}(\pi^{\prime}))
is still relevant.

Now we pick a cuspidal representation
\sigma
of
G_{l_{a}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})}
good to
\nu^{1/2}\cdot\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
. Then we have that
(\pi,\sigma\times D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}(\pi^{\prime}))
is still relevant (determined by the triple
(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n}-\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}+[\sigma],\nu^{1/2}\cdot\pi)
as shown above). Now,
\mathcal{RR}(\pi,\sigma\times D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}(\pi^{\prime}))<\mathcal{RR}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
, by induction, we have that:

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\sigma\times D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}(\pi^{\prime}))\neq 0.

Let
\omega=\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})\times D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}(\pi^{\prime})
. The standard trick of Lemma
23.3
now implies that

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\omega)\cong\mathbb{C}

and
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\pi,\omega)=i^{*}
.

Step 3: Define
\tau^{\prime}
and its quotient
\tau
.

On the other hand, let
\Delta
be a
\preceq^{R}
-maximal (see Section
19.2
) segment in
\mathfrak{m}
. Let
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{mx}(\pi,\Delta)
and let
\tau^{\prime}=\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})\times D_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi)
. Let
\mathfrak{r}=\mathfrak{mx}(\tau,\Delta)
. We now consider the filtration of
\pi
as given in Proposition
3.2
. Let
\kappa
be the submodule containing all the layers

\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})^{[k]}\times\mathrm{RS}_{k-2}(D_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi))

for
k>l_{a}(\mathfrak{p})-l_{a}(\mathfrak{r})\geq 1
.

Now let
\tau=\tau^{\prime}/\kappa
. We pick
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}^{c}
good to
\pi
and
\nu^{-1/2}\pi^{\prime}
. By Corollary
15.10
,
(\sigma^{\prime}\times D_{\Delta}(\pi),\pi^{\prime})
is still relevant. Hence, we can apply induction. By using Lemma
24.1
on
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\sigma^{\prime}\times D_{\Delta}(\pi),\pi^{\prime})\neq 0
with
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\sigma^{\prime}\times D_{\Delta}(\pi),\pi^{\prime})=l_{a}(\mathfrak{m})
, we have a non-zero
\widetilde{f}
in

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\mathrm{St}(\Delta)\times D_{\Delta}(\pi),\pi^{\prime})

with
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\widetilde{f})\leq l_{a}(\mathfrak{m})
. Now from there, we obtain a non-zero map
\bar{f}
in
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})\times D_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi),\pi^{\prime})
with
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\bar{f})\leq l_{a}(\mathfrak{m})
. By Lemma
25.2
, we then have

(*)\quad\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\tau,\pi^{\prime})\neq 0

We want to show that the Hom has dimension one (see Claim 2 below).

Step 4.

Claim 1:

\mathrm{dim}~\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\tau,\omega)\leq 1
.

Proof of claim 1:
Note that
(\Delta,\mathfrak{p}^{\prime},I_{\Delta}^{R}\circ D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}(\pi^{\prime}))
is strongly RdLi-commutative (by Example
9.2
(2)), and so, by Lemma
14.3
,

\eta_{\Delta}(I_{\Delta}^{R}\circ D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}(\pi))=\eta_{\Delta}(I_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}\circ I_{\Delta}^{R}\circ D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}(\pi))=\eta_{\Delta}(I_{\Delta}^{R}(\pi))

and so

\eta_{\Delta}(D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}(\pi^{\prime}))=\eta_{\Delta}(\pi^{\prime}).

Thus
\mathfrak{mx}(D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}(\pi^{\prime}))=\mathfrak{r}
. In Proposition
3.2
, the layers for
(\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})\times D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi))|_{G_{n}}
take the form:

\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})^{[k]}\times\lambda

and so, by Lemma
25.1
(1), the only possible layer that can contribute to a non-zero element in
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\tau,\omega)
is

\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})^{[k^{*}]}\times\mathrm{RS}_{k^{*}-2}(D_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi)),

where
k^{*}=l_{a}(\mathfrak{p})-l_{a}(\mathfrak{r})
.

Recall that
\omega=\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})\times D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}(\pi^{\prime})
. Now, combining with Lemma
25.1
(2),

\mathrm{dim}~\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\tau,\omega)\leq\mathrm{dim}~\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n-l_{a}(\mathfrak{p})}}(\mathrm{RS}_{k^{*}-2}(D_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi)),\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})\times D_{\mathfrak{r}}\circ D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}(\pi^{\prime}))

The last term has dimension at most one by Lemma
3.1
(and indeed it is one by using induction) and so this proves the claim.

Step 5.

Claim 2:
Let
\omega_{1}=\pi^{\prime}
. Then

\mathrm{dim}~\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\tau,\omega_{1})=\mathrm{dim}~\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\tau,\omega)=1.

Proof of claim 2:
This immediately follows from Claim 1 that

\mathrm{dim}~\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\tau,\omega_{1})\leq\mathrm{dim}~\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\tau,\omega)\leq 1.

The equality part now follows from (*).

Step 6.

Claim 3:
Let
\tau_{1}
be the quotient of
\tau
coming from the projection of
\tau^{\prime}
to
\pi
i.e.
\tau_{1}
is the pushout out of two surjections
\tau^{\prime}\rightarrow\pi
and
\tau^{\prime}\rightarrow\tau
. Then

\mathrm{dim}~\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\tau_{1},\omega)=1.

Proof of claim 3:
By Claim 1, we only have to show that
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\tau_{1},\omega)\neq 0
. To this end, we appeal to Lemma
23.3
that there exists a non-zero element
f
in
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\omega)
with
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\pi,\omega)=l_{a}(\mathfrak{m})
.

We consider the following commutative diagram:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.88078pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.88078pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\kappa\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.8808pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.88078pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\tau^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 36.46933pt\raise-15.93501pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{q}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 36.46933pt\raise-27.06448pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern
