0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-5.77779pt\raise-31.87003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\widetilde{\kappa}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.77779pt\raise-31.87003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.6192pt\raise-31.87003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.6192pt\raise-31.87003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\pi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 49.47769pt\raise-25.75893pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 67.05788pt\raise-31.87003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 67.05788pt\raise-31.87003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\omega}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where
\widetilde{\kappa}=q(\kappa)
. By Lemma
23.2
,
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(f\circ q)=l_{a}(\mathfrak{m})
. Thus, by Lemma
25.2
,
(f\circ q)|_{\kappa}=0
. This implies that
f|_{\widetilde{\kappa}}=0
. Since
\tau_{1}=\pi/\widetilde{\kappa}
,
f
lifts to a non-zero map in
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\tau_{1},\omega)
as desired.

Step 7: Complete Case 1 by the strategy.
Now, we return to the proof. By using Claims 2 and 3, Lemma
26.1
implies that
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\tau_{1},\omega_{1})\neq 0
. Since
\tau_{1}
is a quotient of
\pi
, we then have that
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\neq 0
(recall
\omega_{1}=\pi^{\prime}
). This proves Case 1.

Step 8: Use the duality to transfer Case 2 to Case 1.

Case 2:
Suppose we are not in Case 1. Recall that we are assuming
\mathcal{RR}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})>0
. Then there exists a
\leq
-minimal
\rho^{\prime}\in\mathrm{csupp}(\pi)
such that
\rho^{\prime}\not\in\mathrm{csupp}(\nu^{1/2}\pi^{\prime})
and
\rho^{\prime}
is relevant to
\pi^{\prime}
. (Otherwise, if we have
\rho^{\prime}\in\mathrm{csupp}(\nu^{1/2}\pi^{\prime})
, then
\nu^{-1/2}\rho^{\prime}\in\mathrm{csupp}(\pi^{\prime})
. But we also have
\nu^{-1/2}\rho^{\prime}\not\in\mathrm{csupp}(\nu^{1/2}\cdot\pi)
by minimality and then it gives a contradiction.)

Now applying
\vee
and
\theta
, we have that there exists a
\leq
-minimal element
\rho^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{csupp}(\theta(\pi)^{\vee})
such that
\rho^{\prime\prime}\not\in\mathrm{csupp}(\nu^{1/2}\theta(\pi^{\prime})^{\vee})
.

On the other hand,

(26.17)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n+1}}(\sigma\times\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee},\pi^{\vee})

(26.18)

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n+1}}(\theta(\sigma)\times\theta(\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee}),\theta(\pi^{\vee}))

(26.19)

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n+1}}(\theta(\sigma)\times\pi^{\prime},\pi)

Here
\sigma
is a certain cuspidal representation of
G_{2}
such that
\sigma\times\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee}
is irreducible and
\nu^{1/2}\cdot\sigma
is good to
\pi^{\vee}
. The first isomorphism follows from a duality
[
Ch22
, Proposition 4.1]
and the second isomorphism follows by applying
\theta
-action, and
\theta(\sigma\times\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\cong\theta(\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\times\theta(\sigma)\cong\theta(\sigma)\times\theta(\pi^{\prime}{}^{\vee})\cong\theta(\sigma)\times\pi^{\prime}
. The last isomorphism follows by the isomorphism for the Gelfand-Kazhdan involution.

We have that
\mathcal{RR}(\theta(\sigma)\times\pi^{\prime}{},\pi)=\mathcal{RR}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
. We also have that
(\theta(\sigma)\times\pi^{\prime},\pi)
is relevant by Theorem
18.1
. Now the above discussions justify that we can use Case 1 to conclude the Hom in (
26.19
) is non-zero. This implies that
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
is non-zero.
∎

Part V
Proof of necessity of generalized relevance

For an overview of this section, see Sections
5.3
and
5.2
. Section
28
studies a special type of branching laws related to derivatives, in which a technique in Section
27
is needed. Section
29
studies a characterization of the module whose highest derivative gives a simple quotient of a BZ derivative (Corollary
29.6
). Sections
30
and
31
prove our main results.

27.
Taking highest derivatives in branching laws

We first illustrate the idea of taking highest derivatives. Let
\pi
be a smooth representation of the mirabolic subgroup
M_{n+1}
. Let
\tau
be a
G_{n}
-quotient of
\pi|_{G_{n}}
. Then, as
G_{n}
-representations, one can take a derivative to obtain a natural projection:

f:{}^{(j)}(\pi|_{G_{n}})\twoheadrightarrow{}^{(j)}\tau.

(For a precise realization on those derivatives to make sense of the projection, see Section
27.1
.) We write
{}^{\langle j\rangle}\pi:={}^{(j)}(\pi|_{G_{n}})
. On the other hand, one can also take ’left
j
-th derivative’, denoted
{}^{(j)}\pi
, on
\pi
as
M_{n+1}
(close to the one for
G_{n+1}
, and for a precise description, see (
27.20
) below), which then gives a projection:

p:{}^{\langle j\rangle}\pi\twoheadrightarrow{}^{(j)}\pi.

The question is when
f
factors through
p
.

We are interested in the branching law case and so we consider
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n+1})
(then regarded as a
M_{n+1}
-representation) which is thickened (see Definition
27.3
below) and
i=\mathrm{lev}(\pi)
. For a
G_{n}
-quotient
\tau
of
\pi
, if the induced map
f
from
f^{\prime}:\pi\rightarrow\tau
factors through such
p
above, we refer this to
deforming the branching law
f
. Such deformation
does not happen in general
. We show that when
\tau
takes the form
\tau^{\prime}\times\sigma
for some simple quotient
\tau^{\prime}
of
\pi^{(i)}
(for some
i
) and some suitable choice of
\sigma\in\mathrm{Irr}^{c}
, such deformation happens (see Lemma
27.4
). This relies on some analysis on the layers arising from the geometric lemma (or Leibniz’s rule). As a result, we constructed a branching law
\widetilde{f}:{}^{-}\pi\rightarrow{}^{-}\tau^{\prime}\times\sigma
.

As shown in Part
IV
, it is useful to determine the layer supporting a branching law. Indeed, in Lemma
28.3
(in next section), one can use some analysis on Bernstein-Zelevinsky layers to show that
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(f)=i
, and
\mathcal{L}_{lBL}(f)=\mathrm{lev}(\pi)
. It turns out that a similar argument can prove that
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\widetilde{f})=i
in the deformed branching law. Determining
\mathcal{L}_{lBL}(\widetilde{f})
needs some other works (this is also the reason we have to consider the deformation). For this, in Section
27.2
, we study the derivative on the left BZ filtration
{}_{k}\Lambda(\pi)
for
\pi
, which will then be used to show that
\mathcal{L}_{lBL}(\widetilde{f})=\mathrm{lev}(\pi)
(see Proposition
27.8
and Section
28.4
).

We finally also mention that Offen
[
Of20
, Section 6]
asks similar questions on the effect of taking highest derivatives in the content of Sp-distinction.

27.1.
Taking highest derivatives for mirabolic subgroup representations

We use the notations in Section
3
. For the purpose of exposition, it is more convenient to use the definition of BZ derivatives in the form of coinvariants, which we are going to formulate. Let

\bar{R}_{i}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}u&x\\
&I_{n+1-i}\end{pmatrix}:u\in U_{i},x\in\mathrm{Mat}_{i,n+1-i}\right\}\subset G_{n+1}.

Fix a non-trivial character
\psi_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
and extend trivially to a character
\psi_{i}^{\prime}
of
\bar{R}_{i}
. For
\pi\in\mathrm{Alg}(M_{n+1})
and some integer
i\leq n
, we define (by abuse of notations)

(27.20)

\displaystyle{}^{(i)}\pi=\pi_{\bar{R}_{j},\psi_{j}}=\delta_{\bar{R}_{i}}^{-1/2}\cdot\frac{\pi}{\langle u.x-\psi_{i}^{\prime}(u)x:u\in\bar{R}_{i},x\in\pi\rangle}

regarded as a
M_{n+1-i}
-representation via the embedding
m\mapsto\mathrm{diag}(I_{i},m)
. This definition is equivalent (up to a natural isomorphism) to the one in Section
3
and we shall use such realization in this section.

We now define a smaller subgroup
\bar{S}_{i}
in
\bar{R}_{i}
:

\bar{S}_{i}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}u&x^{\prime}&0\\
&I_{n-i}&\\
&&1\end{pmatrix}:u\in U_{i},x^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Mat}_{i,n-i}\right\}.

We similarly define:

{}^{\langle i\rangle}\pi=\delta_{\bar{S}_{i}}^{-1/2}\cdot\frac{\pi}{\langle u.x-\psi_{i}^{\prime}(u)x:u\in\bar{S}_{i},x\in\pi\rangle},

regarded as a
G_{n-i}
representation via the embedding
g\mapsto\mathrm{diag}(I_{i},g,1)
. Note that
{}^{\langle i\rangle}\pi={}^{(i)}(\pi|_{G_{n}})
(recall we embed
G_{n}
to
G_{n+1}
by
g\mapsto\mathrm{diag}(g,1)
).

We are going to study the natural projection:
{}^{\langle i\rangle}\pi\rightarrow{}^{(i)}\pi
. In other words, that is to study how certain derivative for
G_{n}
can be extended to a derivative of
G_{n+1}
.

We first give a computation on
(j)
-derivative on
\Gamma(\pi)
, which follows from a direct computation from Mackey theory:

Lemma 27.1
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Alg}(G_{n})
. Then
{}^{(j)}\Gamma^{k+1}(\pi)\cong\Gamma^{k+1}({}^{(j)}\pi)
.

Proof.

One can proceed by taking Jacquet functors in stage. Take
N
to be the unipotent subgroup containing matrices of the form:

\begin{pmatrix}I_{j}&*\\
&I_{k+1+n-j}\end{pmatrix}.

However, it follows from a similar computation as
[
Ch21
, Lemma 4.5]
that

\Gamma^{k+1}(\pi)_{N}\cong\Gamma^{k+1}(\pi_{N_{j,n-j}}),

where
\Gamma^{k+1}(\pi_{N_{j,n-j}})
is considered to take the functor
\Gamma^{k+1}
in the second factor of
G_{j}\times G_{n-j}
. Then one further takes the Whittaker model on the first factor to obtain
{}^{(j)}\Gamma^{k+1}(\pi)\cong\Gamma^{k+1}({}^{(j)}\pi)
.
∎

Lemma 27.2
.

Let
\pi
be a representation of
G_{m}
of finite length. Let
\kappa=\Gamma^{k+1}(\pi)
. Let
\tau
be a simple quotient of
\pi
. Let
\sigma\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{k})
good to
\nu^{1/2}\pi
. Let
j=\mathrm{lev}(\tau)
. Let
f:\Gamma^{k+1}(\pi)\rightarrow(\nu^{1/2}\cdot\tau)\times\sigma
. As
G_{m+k}
-representations, this induces a map
\widetilde{f}:{}^{\langle j\rangle}\kappa\rightarrow\nu^{1/2}({}^{-}\tau)\times\sigma
. Then
\widetilde{f}
factors through the projection map
{}^{\langle j\rangle}\kappa\rightarrow{}^{(j)}\kappa
.

Proof.

Let

Q=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}u&x&v\\
&g&v^{\prime}\\
&&1\end{pmatrix}:u\in U_{j},x\in Mat_{j,m+k-j},v\in F^{j},v^{\prime}\in F^{m+k-j},g\in G_{m+k-j}\right\}

and let

P=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}g&v\\
&u\end{pmatrix}:g\in G_{j},v\in Mat_{j,m+k+1-j},u\in U_{m+k+1-j}\right\}.

Let
\lambda
be the space of smooth compactly-supported functions from
PQ
to
\pi\boxtimes\psi_{m+k+1-j}
.

Let
\widetilde{\tau}=\nu^{1/2}\tau
. Since
{}^{-}\widetilde{\tau}\times\sigma
appears in the top layer (in the filtration from the geometric lemma) of
{}^{(j)}(\widetilde{\tau}\times\sigma)
,
{}^{-}\widetilde{\tau}\times\sigma
also appears in the top layer of
{}^{(j)}(\widetilde{\tau}\times\sigma)
via the functoriality of geometric lemma. Taking the twisted Jacquet functor, we have that
\widetilde{f}
is factored as:

{}^{\langle j\rangle}\kappa\rightarrow{}^{\langle j\rangle}\lambda\rightarrow({}^{-}\widetilde{\tau})\times\sigma.

Now projecting
{}^{\langle j\rangle}\kappa
to
{}^{(j)}\kappa
, we have that

{}^{(j)}\kappa={}^{(j)}\lambda={}^{\langle j\rangle}\lambda

as vector spaces, where the first equality follows from an application of geometric lemma (see e.g.
[
BZ77
, 5.9]
), c.f. Lemma
27.1
and the second follows from definitions. Thus, the map
\widetilde{f}
factors through the projection from
{}^{\langle j\rangle}\kappa
to
{}^{(j)}\kappa
.
∎

Definition 27.3
.

A multisegment
\mathfrak{m}
is said to be
thickened
if any segment has relative length at least
2
. An irreducible representation
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
is said to be
thickened
if
\pi\cong\langle\mathfrak{m}\rangle
for some thickened multisegment
\mathfrak{m}
.

As also seen in
[
Ch21
, Section 7]
, the thickened case has good combinatorics and the general case needs more substantial work.

Lemma 27.4
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n+1})
be thickened. Let
\tau
be a simple quotient of
\pi^{[i]}
. Let
j=\mathrm{lev}(\pi)(=\mathrm{lev}(\tau))
. Let
\sigma\in\mathrm{Irr}^{c}(G_{i-1})
be good to
\nu^{1/2}\cdot\pi
. Then

•

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\tau\times\sigma)\neq 0
.

•

Let
f^{\prime}:{}^{\langle j\rangle}\pi\rightarrow{}^{-}\tau\times\sigma
be the induced
G_{n-i}
-map from a non-zero map
f:\pi\rightarrow\tau\times\sigma
.

Then
f^{\prime}
factors through the projection
{}^{\langle j\rangle}\pi
to
{}^{(j)}\pi
.

Proof.

By a standard argument of the BZ filtration, we have an isomorphism:

\mathbb{C}\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\tau\times\sigma)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\Lambda_{i-1}(\pi),\tau\times\sigma)

Let
f
be a non-zero map in
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\tau\times\sigma)
and let the corresponding lift in
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\Lambda_{i-1}(\pi),\tau\times\sigma)
be
\widetilde{f}
. Taking the twisted Jacquet functor
⟨j⟩
(and composing with the projection to
{}^{-}\tau\times\sigma
, we then obtain a map
f^{\prime}:{}^{\langle j\rangle}\pi\rightarrow{}^{-}\tau\times\sigma
. We then obtain a (non-zero) lift
\widetilde{f}^{\prime}
on
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n-j}}({}^{\langle j\rangle}\Lambda_{i-1}(\pi),{}^{-}\tau\times\sigma)
by the above isomorphism. Since the map
\widetilde{f}
comes from
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\Sigma_{i}(\pi),\tau\times\sigma)
, Lemma
27.2
implies that
\widetilde{f}^{\prime}
factors through the projection
{}^{\langle j\rangle}\Lambda_{i-1}(\pi)
to
{}^{(j)}\Lambda_{i-1}(\pi)
. Thus we also have a corresponding non-zero map, denoted
g^{\prime}
, in
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}({}^{(j)}\Lambda_{i-1}(\pi),{}^{-}\tau\times\sigma)
.

Let
\omega
be the cokernel of the embedding
\Lambda_{i-1}(\pi)
to
\pi
.
We consider the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n-j}}({}^{\langle j\rangle}\pi,{}^{-}\tau\times\sigma)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n-j}}({}^{\langle j\rangle}\Lambda_{i-1}(\pi),{}^{-}\tau\times\sigma)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n-j}}({}^{(j)}\pi,{}^{-}\tau\times\sigma)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n-j}}({}^{(j)}\Lambda_{i-1}(\pi),{}^{-}\tau\times\sigma)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{G_{n-j}}({}^{(j)}\omega,{}^{-}\tau\times\sigma)}

The Ext-group in the diagram is zero by a Ext group computation on the layers
{}^{(j)}\Sigma_{i^{\prime}}(\pi)=\Sigma_{i^{\prime}}({}^{(j)}\pi)
(
i^{\prime}<i
) (here we use Lemma
27.1
) and a cuspidal support comparison (see the proof of Lemma
28.3
for more details). This implies that there is a lift of
g^{\prime}
in the bottom left corner Hom. It follows from the commutative diagram that the lift gives the desired map.

From the commutative diagram above, it remains to show that the image of
g^{\prime}
in the top left corner agrees with
f^{\prime}
. However, this would follow if we can show that
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n-j}}({}^{\langle j\rangle}\omega,{}^{-}\tau\times\sigma)=0
, where
\omega=\pi/\Lambda_{i-1}(\pi)
. To this end, from the BZ filtration, layers in
\omega
takes the form
\pi^{[k]}\times\Pi_{n-k}
for
k<i
. After taking the twisted Jacquet functor, those layers for
{}^{\langle j\rangle}\omega
take the form
({}^{(j_{1})}(\pi^{[k]})\times\Pi_{n-k-j_{2}})\otimes\mathfrak{Z}_{j_{2}}
, where
j_{1},j_{2}
run for all
j_{1}+j_{2}=j
and
\mathfrak{Z}_{j_{2}}
is the Bernstein Center of
G_{j_{2}}
. Applying the second adjointness and comparing cuspidal support, we always have that:

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n-j}}(({}^{(j_{1})}(\pi^{[k]})\times\Pi_{n-k-j_{2}})\otimes\mathfrak{Z}_{j_{2}},{}^{-}\tau\times\sigma)=0

This implies that
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n-j}}({}^{\langle j_{1}\rangle}\omega,{}^{-}\tau\times\sigma)=0
as desired.
∎

27.2.
Derivatives on another mirabolic restriction

We now consider another mirabolic restriction and so we are considering
M_{n+1}^{t}
-modules. (Here
M_{n+1}^{t}
denotes the transpose of
M_{n+1}
.) To avoid confusion and further abuse of notations, we shall simply use
\pi_{\bar{S},\psi}
to consider the co-invariant spaces.

Let
\Pi_{i}=\mathrm{ind}_{U_{i}}^{G_{i}}\psi_{i}
be the Gelfand-Graev representation of
G_{i}
. By the induction in stages, for
\tau\in\mathrm{Alg}(G_{j})
, we have
{}^{i}\Gamma(\tau)|_{G_{i+j-1}}\cong\Pi_{i-1}\times\tau
.

Lemma 27.5
.

We regard
M_{n-j+1}^{t}
as a subgroup
M_{n+1}^{t}
via
m\mapsto\mathrm{diag}(I_{j},m)
. Note that
\bar{S}_{j}
is invariant under conjugation by
M_{n-j+1}^{t}
. Let
\tau\in\mathrm{Alg}(G_{n+1-i})
. Then

({}^{i}\Gamma(\tau))_{\bar{S}_{j},\psi_{j}}

admits a
M_{n-j+1}^{t}
-filtration of the form: with
j_{1}+j_{2}=j
,

{}^{i-j_{1}}\Gamma({}^{(j_{2})}\tau)\otimes\mathfrak{Z}_{j_{1}},

where
\mathfrak{Z}_{j_{1}}
is the Bernstein center
G_{j_{1}}
. Moreover, when restricting to
G_{n-j}
-representations, the filtration agrees with the filtration obtained by applying Leibniz’s rule on
{}^{(j)}(\Pi_{i-1}\times\tau)
.

Proof.

Let
R_{i}
be the subgroup containing matrices of the form
\begin{pmatrix}g&*\\
&u\end{pmatrix}
for
g\in G_{n+1-i},u\in U_{i}
. Let
w=\mathrm{diag}(\begin{pmatrix}&J_{i}\\
I_{n-i}&\end{pmatrix},1)
, where
J_{i}
is the matrix with
1
in the anti-diagonal and
0
elsewhere. In such case,
wR_{i}^{t}w^{-1}
contains all matrices of the form:

\begin{pmatrix}u&*&\\
&g&\\
*&*&1\end{pmatrix},

where
u\in U_{i-1}
and
g\in G_{n+1-i}
.
Then, using the element
w
, as a
G_{n}
-representation, we have that:

\mathrm{ind}_{R_{i}^{t}}^{M^{t}_{n}}(\tau\boxtimes\psi_{i})\cong\Pi_{i-1}\times\tau.

Now one applies the derivative and Leibniz’s rule on
\Pi_{i-1}\times\tau
and uses
{}^{(j_{1})}(\Pi_{i-1})=\Pi_{i-1-j_{1}}\otimes\mathfrak{Z}_{j_{1}}
, and then we obtain a filtration of the form

(\Pi_{i-1-j_{1}}\times{}^{(j_{2})}\tau)\otimes\mathfrak{Z}_{j_{1}}

with
j_{1}+j_{2}=j
. Now one imposes the action of
M_{n-j+1}^{t}
and obtains such form.

∎

Lemma 27.6
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n+1})
be thickened. Let
\tau={}^{-}({}^{-}\pi)
(i.e. taking highest derivative twice). Let
j^{*}=\mathrm{lev}(\pi)
. Let
m=n+1-j^{*}
. Then, as shown in Lemma
27.5
,
({}_{j^{*}}\Sigma(\pi))_{\bar{S}_{j^{*}},\psi_{j^{*}}}
has a submodule
{}^{j^{*}}\Gamma(\tau)
, as
M_{m}
-modules. Denote such submodule map by
\iota
. Let
p:({}_{j^{*}}\Sigma(\pi))_{\bar{S}_{j^{*}},\psi_{j^{*}}}\rightarrow{}^{\langle j^{*}\rangle}\pi
be the embedding induced from the BZ filtration. Denote the projection map from
{}^{\langle j^{*}\rangle}\pi
to
{}^{(j^{*})}\pi={}^{-}\pi
by
q
. Then
q\circ p\circ\iota\neq 0
.

Proof.

Note that we have a surjection from
\pi_{\bar{S}_{j^{*}},\psi_{j^{*}}}
to
{}^{-}\pi
, which is
M_{m}^{t}
-equivariant. Thus we only have to observe that other composition factors of
({}_{j^{*}}\Sigma(\pi))_{\bar{S}_{j^{*}},\psi_{j^{*}}}
as well as any composition factor of
({}_{j}\Sigma(\pi))_{\bar{S}_{j^{*}},\psi_{j^{*}}}
(
j<j^{*}
) do not take the form
{}^{j^{*}}\Gamma(\kappa)
for some
\kappa\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Indeed, the last assertion follows from the description of those composition factors in Lemma
27.5
.
∎

27.3.
Derivative-descentable quotient on another BZ filtrations

Lemma 27.7
.

Let
\omega\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{k})
. Let
\kappa\in\mathrm{Alg}(G_{l})
. Let
\lambda
be an irreducible quotient of
\kappa\times\omega
. Let
j^{*}=\mathrm{lev}(\omega)
. Let
q
be the quotient map and let
q^{\prime}:{}^{(j^{*})}(\kappa\times\omega)\rightarrow{}^{(j^{*})}\lambda
be the induced map from
q
. Let
\iota:\kappa\times{}^{-}\omega\hookrightarrow{}^{(j^{*})}(\kappa\times\omega)
be the embedding from the bottom layer of Leibniz’s rule. Then

(1)

q^{\prime}\circ\iota\neq 0
;

(2)

Suppose
\kappa
is of finite length. Suppose
\lambda
can be written as
\lambda_{1}\times\lambda_{2}
such that
\mathrm{csupp}(\lambda_{1})\cap\mathrm{csupp}(\omega)=\emptyset
and
\mathrm{csupp}(\lambda_{1})\cap\mathrm{csupp}(\lambda_{2})=\emptyset
. Then, we further have a map:

s:{}^{(j^{*})}(\lambda_{1}\times\lambda_{2})\twoheadrightarrow\lambda_{1}\times{}^{(j^{*})}\lambda_{2}.

Then
s\circ q^{\prime}\circ\iota\neq 0

Proof.

Note that
{}^{(j^{*})}\pi\cong\tau
follows from
\mathrm{lev}(\tau)=j^{*}
. Let
P=P_{l,k}
and let
U=U_{k+l}
. Let
N_{P}^{-}
be the opposite unipotent radical of
P
.

We realize
\kappa\times\omega
as smooth compactly-supported
\kappa\boxtimes\omega
-valued function space
C^{\infty}_{c}(P\setminus G_{k+l},\kappa\boxtimes\omega)
. Let
C^{\infty}_{c}(P\setminus PU^{t},\kappa\boxtimes\omega)
as the subspace with functions supported in
P\setminus PU^{t}
. The following composition, after taking the Jacquet functor
N_{P}^{-}
,

C^{\infty}_{c}(P\setminus PU^{t},\kappa\boxtimes\omega)\hookrightarrow\kappa\times\omega\twoheadrightarrow\lambda

is non-zero, see Bezrukavnikov-Kazhdan
[
BK15
, Section 6.2]
(also see
[
Ch22+c
]
).

Now, we multiply with the element
w=\begin{pmatrix}&I_{l}\\
I_{k}&\end{pmatrix}
to above sequences, we obtain a non-zero composition, after taking the Jacquet functor
w^{-1}N_{P}^{-}w=N_{P_{k,l}}
:

C^{\infty}_{c}(P\setminus PU^{t}w,\kappa\boxtimes\omega)\hookrightarrow\kappa\times\omega\twoheadrightarrow\lambda.

The composition takes the form:

\omega\boxtimes\kappa\rightarrow\lambda_{N_{P_{k,l}}}.

Using Jacquet functor in stages, this implies that after applying the twisted Jacquet functor
{}_{\bar{R}_{j^{*}},\psi_{j^{*}}}
, we also have a non-zero composition (see
[
Ch22+c
]
for more discussions): (set
\tau=\kappa\boxtimes\omega
)

\textstyle{C^{\infty}_{c}(P\setminus PU^{t}w,\tau)_{\bar{R}_{j^{*}},\psi_{j^{*}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{\infty}_{c}(P\setminus PU^{t}w\bar{R}_{j^{*}},\tau)_{\bar{R}_{j^{*}},\psi_{j^{*}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\kappa\times\omega)_{\bar{R}_{j^{*}},\psi_{j^{*}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\lambda_{\bar{R}_{j^{*}},\psi_{j^{*}}}}
\textstyle{\kappa\times{}^{-}\omega}
\textstyle{{}^{(j^{*})}(\kappa\times\omega)}

and so we obtain (1). (Note that taking the twisted Jacquet functor
{}_{\bar{R}_{j^{*}},\psi_{j^{*}}}
is the same as taking the BZ derivative here.)

For (2),
\lambda_{1}\times{}^{(j^{*})}\lambda_{2}
is an indecomposable component in
{}^{(j^{*})}(\lambda_{1}\times\lambda_{2})
by the cuspidal support condition and hence gives the map
s
. Then, by a comparison of cuspidal support and (1), the image of
q\circ\iota^{\prime}
must lie in that component and so this gives (2).
∎

Proposition 27.8
.

(c.f. Theorem
28.1
below)
Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n+1})
be thickened. Let
j^{*}=\mathrm{lev}(\pi)
. Let
\tau
be a simple quotient
\pi^{[i]}
for some
i
. Let
\sigma\in\mathrm{Irr}^{c}(G_{n-i})
good to
\nu^{1/2}\cdot\pi
. Suppose the quotient map
s
from
\pi
to
\tau\times\sigma
is non-zero, when restricted to
{}_{j^{*}}\Sigma(\pi)
. Then the unique map from
{}^{-}\pi
to
{}^{-}\tau\times\sigma
is also non-zero, when restricted to
{}_{j^{*}}\Sigma({}^{-}\pi)
.

Proof.

One realizes
{}_{j^{*}}\Sigma(\pi)
as
\Pi_{j^{*}}\times{}^{-}\pi
and for such explicit realization, see the proof of Lemma
27.5
. Then there exists a (generic, not necessarily irreducible) quotient of finite length
\lambda
of
\Pi_{j^{*}}
such that we have an induced surjection

t:\Pi_{j^{*}}\times{}^{-}\pi\rightarrow\lambda\times{}^{-}\pi

and the given non-zero map
\Pi_{j^{*}}\times{}^{-}\pi\rightarrow\tau\times\sigma
factors through
t
. (To construct such quotient, one may apply an element in the Bernstein center
[
BD84
]
that annihilates
\tau\times\sigma
.)

We have the following commutative diagram:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 277.9521pt\raise-33.47308pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\sigma\times{}^{-}\tau}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-65.95963pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 69.28882pt\raise-65.95963pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 124.32207pt\raise-65.95963pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 212.58832pt\raise-43.138pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.21725pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f^{\prime\prime}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 277.9521pt\raise-36.84715pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 225.58722pt\raise-65.95963pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 289.59215pt\raise-65.95963pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where

•

\iota
comes from the bottom layer of Leibniz’s rule in Lemma
27.6
(also see Lemma
27.5
);

•

t^{\prime}
is induced from
t
;

•

t^{\prime\prime}
is the functorial map from
t
and Leibniz’s rule;

•

f^{\prime\prime}
in the last triangle is from Lemma
27.4
;

•

s^{\prime}
is induced from
s
by taking
{}_{\bar{S}_{j^{*}}}
;

•

s^{\prime\prime}
is the projection to the indecomposable component
\sigma\times{}^{-}\tau
in
{}^{(j^{*})}(\pi\times\tau)
;

•

p
is induced from the embedding in the BZ filtration;

•

r
comes from the above discussion;

•

{}^{-}{}^{-}\pi
means the highest left derivative of
{}^{-}\pi
.

Now, we have:

•

r\circ\iota^{\prime}\neq 0
by Lemma
27.7
.

These two imply that
f^{\prime\prime}\circ(q\circ p\circ\iota)\neq 0
as desired. (We remark that
q\circ p\circ\iota\neq 0
in Lemma
27.6
, can also be deduced indirectly from here. This, in particular, gives that
{}^{j^{*}}\Gamma({}^{-}{}^{-}\pi)
coincides with
{}_{j^{*}}\Sigma({}^{-}\pi)
in
{}^{-}\pi
.)
∎

28.
Branching law from BZ derivatives

28.1.
Branching law from simple quotients of BZ derivatives

The goal of this section is to prove the following:

Theorem 28.1
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n+1})
. Let
\tau
be a simple quotient of
\pi^{[i]}
. Let
\sigma\in\mathrm{Irr}^{c}(G_{i-1})
good to
\nu^{1/2}\cdot\pi
. Then

(1)

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\sigma\times\tau)\neq 0
; and

(2)

\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\pi,\sigma\times\tau)=i
; and

(3)

\mathcal{L}_{lBL}(\pi,\sigma\times\tau)=\mathrm{lev}(\pi)
.

28.2.
A basic criteria for simple quotients of BZ derivatives

Before proving Theorem
28.1
, we introduce some basic results and more notations. For a segment
\Delta=[a,b]_{\rho}
, define:

\Delta^{-}=[a,b-1]_{\rho},\quad{}^{-}\Delta=[a+1,b]_{\rho},\quad\Delta^{0}=\Delta,\quad{}^{0}\Delta=\Delta,

\Delta^{[-]}=[a+\frac{1}{2},b-\frac{1}{2}]_{\rho},\quad{}^{[-]}\Delta=[a+\frac{1}{2},b-\frac{1}{2}]_{\rho},

and we also define
\Delta^{[0]}=[a+\frac{1}{2},b+\frac{1}{2}]_{\rho}
and
{}^{[0]}\Delta=[a-\frac{1}{2},b-\frac{1}{2}]_{\rho}
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{r}\right\}\in\mathrm{Mult}
. Write
\Delta_{k}=[a_{k},b_{k}]_{\rho_{k}}
and let
n_{k}=n(\rho_{k})
. Define

\mathfrak{m}^{(i)}=\left\{\left\{\Delta_{1}^{\#_{1}},\ldots,\Delta_{r}^{\#_{r}}\right\}:\ \forall p,\ \#_{p}=-\mbox{ or }0,\mbox{ and}\sum_{p:\ \#_{p}=-}n_{p}=i\right\}

{}^{(i)}\mathfrak{m}=\left\{\left\{{}^{\#_{1}}\Delta_{1},\ldots,{}^{\#_{r}}\Delta_{r}\right\}:\ \forall p,\ \#_{p}=-\mbox{ or }0,\mbox{ and}\sum_{p:\ \#_{p}=-}n_{p}=i\right\}.

We similarly define the shifted version for
\mathfrak{m}^{[i]}
and
{}^{[i]}\mathfrak{m}
by replacing
-
with
[-]
and replacing
0
by
[0]
.

Lemma 28.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{r}\right\}\in\mathrm{Mult}
. Let
\tau
be a simple quotient of
\langle\mathfrak{m}\rangle^{(i)}
. Then
\tau\cong\langle\mathfrak{n}\rangle
for some
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathfrak{m}^{(i)}
.

Proof.

It follows from
[
Ch21
, Lemma 7.3]
and the embedding
\langle\mathfrak{m}\rangle\hookrightarrow\zeta(\mathfrak{m})
(see Section
2.1
).
∎

28.3.
Proof of Theorem
28.1
for thickened case

Lemma 28.3
.

Theorem
28.1
holds if
\pi
is thickened.

Proof.

We first prove (1) and (2). Let
\pi^{\prime}=\sigma\times\tau
. The standard argument in BZ filtration gives that (also see the argument in
[
Ch21
, Proposition 2.5]
): for all
i^{\prime}<i
, and for all
k\geq 0
,

\mathrm{Ext}^{k}_{G_{n+1-i^{\prime}}}(\pi^{[i^{\prime}]},{}^{(i^{\prime}-1)}\pi^{\prime})=0.

Thus, a long exact sequence argument gives that:

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\Lambda_{i-1}(\pi),\pi^{\prime}).

Now the latter Hom is non-zero since

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\Sigma_{i}(\pi),\pi^{\prime})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n-i+1}}(\pi^{[i]},{}^{(i-1)}\pi^{\prime})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n-i+1}}(\pi^{[i]},\tau)\neq 0.

Then a long exact sequence argument gives (1). Then, (2) also follows, for instance, by Corollary
22.7
.

Let
i^{*}=\mathrm{lev}(\pi)
. From (1), we would have that
\mathrm{Hom}_{M^{\prime}}({}_{i^{*}}\Sigma(\pi),\tau\times\sigma)\neq 0
and (3) if we can show the following claim:

Cliam:
For
j<i^{*}
,

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}({}_{j}\Sigma(\pi),\tau\times\sigma)=0.

Proof of claim:
Suppose the Hom is non-zero for some
j<i^{*}
. Then, by the Frobenius reciprocity,

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n-1}}({}^{[j]}\pi,\tau^{(k)})\neq 0.

where
k=j-i
. Now set
\mathfrak{m}=\left\{\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{r}\right\}
for the multisegment associated to
\pi
. Then
{}^{[j]}\pi
admits a filtration with successive quotients which are some quotients of
\theta(\zeta(\mathfrak{n}))^{\vee}
for
\mathfrak{n}\in{}^{[j]}\mathfrak{m}
by using the surjection
\theta(\zeta(\mathfrak{n}))^{\vee}\twoheadrightarrow\pi
and the geometric lemma (see the proof of
[
Ch21
, Lemma 7.3]
again).

Similarly, set

\mathfrak{M}=\left\{\Delta_{1}^{\#_{p}},\ldots,\Delta_{r}^{\#_{p}}:\forall p,\ \#_{p}=[--],[-],\mbox{ or }[0],\sum_{p:\ \#_{p}=[--]}2n_{p}+\sum_{q:\ \#_{q}=[-]}n_{q}=j\right\},

where
[a,b]_{\rho}^{[--]}=[a+\frac{1}{2},b-\frac{3}{2}]_{\rho}
. (Note that the definition is well-defined for our situation by using the thickening condition.) There is a filtration on
\tau^{(k)}
with successive quotients isomorphic to some submodules of
\zeta(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
for some
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{M}
.

Thus, by a standard argument (see e.g.
[
Ch22
, Proposition 2.3]
), the condition that
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n-j}}({}^{[j]}\pi,\tau^{(k)})\neq 0
implies
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
for some
\mathfrak{n}\in{}^{[j]}\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{M}
. Now it suffices to show the last equation is not possible, which will be proceeded combinatorially as follows.

Suppose
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
for some
\mathfrak{n}\in{}^{[j]}\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{M}
. We fix certain
\leq
-minimal
\rho
in
\mathrm{csupp}(\mathfrak{m})
. Let

N_{c}=|\mathfrak{n}_{a=\nu^{-1/2+c}\rho}|,\quad N_{c}^{\prime}=|\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}_{a=\nu^{-1/2+c}\rho}|.

By the thickening condition and the definition of
\mathfrak{M}
, we have that

N_{c}^{\prime}\leq|\mathfrak{m}_{a=\nu^{c-1}\rho}|

and so
N_{c}\leq|\mathfrak{m}_{a=\nu^{c-1}\rho}|
(by
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
).

Then, we have
N_{0}=0
,
N_{1}\leq|\mathfrak{m}_{a=\rho}|
, and more generally,
N_{c}\leq|\mathfrak{m}_{a=\nu^{c-1}\rho}|
. Then
N_{0}=0
implies that the segments in
\mathfrak{m}_{a=\rho}
are shifted and truncated to get segments in
\mathfrak{n}
. Hence, there are at least
|\mathfrak{m}_{a=\rho}|
segments in
\mathfrak{n}_{a=\nu^{1/2}\rho}
(by the thickending condition). Then this forces
N_{1}=|\mathfrak{m}_{a=\rho}|
and then this implies that all segments in
\mathfrak{m}_{a=\nu\rho}
are shifted and truncated to get segments in
\mathfrak{n}
. Inductively, with varying different
\leq
-minimal
\rho
, we deduce that

\mathfrak{n}=\left\{{}^{[-]}\Delta_{1},\ldots,{}^{[-]}\Delta_{r}\right\}.

However, this contradicts that
j<i^{*}
. This proves the claim.
∎

28.4.
Proof of Theorem
28.1
for general case

We now consider
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
to be arbitrary. Let
\tau
be a simple quotient of
\pi^{[i]}
for some
i
. The proof for (1) and (2) is the same as the one in Lemma
28.3
.

We consider (3). Let
\pi^{\prime}
be the irreducible (thickened) representation such that
{}^{-}\pi^{\prime}\cong\pi
and
\mathrm{lev}(\pi^{\prime})=\mathrm{lev}(\pi)
. By Theorem
29.3
below (whose proof is independent of this section), there exists a simple quotient
\tau^{\prime}
of
\pi^{\prime}{}^{[i]}
such that
{}^{-}\tau^{\prime}\cong\tau
and
\mathrm{lev}(\tau^{\prime})=\mathrm{lev}(\pi^{\prime})
.

Now choose a cuspidal representation
\sigma
good to
\pi^{\prime}
. We have that
\tau^{\prime}\times\sigma
is a simple quotient of
\pi^{\prime}
by Lemma
27.4
(or by Lemma
28.3
). Now it follows from Proposition
27.8
that the unique quotient map from
\pi
to
\tau\times\sigma
, restricted to
{}_{j^{*}}\Sigma(\pi)
, is also non-zero. Since
j^{*}
is the largest possible integer, we have that
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\pi,\tau\times\sigma)=j^{*}
.

29.
Deforming and thickening simple quotients of BZ derivatives

Deforming a simple quotient of a BZ derivative is more straightforward by taking the highest BZ derivative. Thickening relies on the Zelevinsky combinatorial realization on the highest derivative
[
Ze80
]
and then one does the integrals of some cuspidal representations steps by steps.

The idea is to establish some kind of commutativity of integrals and BZ derivatives. In priori, we do not have the exhaustion theorem for simple quotients for BZ derivatives at this point.

29.1.
A lemma for thickening

Lemma 29.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathrm{Mult}_{\rho}
and let
\pi=\langle\mathfrak{m}\rangle
. Let
\rho\in\mathrm{Irr}^{c}
such that any segment
\widetilde{\Delta}
in
\mathfrak{m}
satisfies that
a(\widetilde{\Delta})\not\cong\rho
. Let
k
be the number of segments
\widetilde{\Delta}
in
\mathfrak{m}
satisfying
\nu\cdot\rho\cong a(\widetilde{\Delta})
i.e.

k=|\mathfrak{m}_{a=\nu\cdot\rho}|.

Let
\tau
be a simple quotient of
\pi^{(i)}
(for some
i
). Then
(I_{\rho})^{k}(\tau)
is a simple quotient of
(I_{\rho}^{k}(\pi))^{(i)}
.

Proof.

Let
\tau
be an irreducible quotient of
\pi^{(i)}
. We have surjections:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 27.69884pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\cr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-27.69884pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\rho^{\times k}\times\pi)^{(i)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 51.69884pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-1.99997pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 51.69884pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\rho^{\times k}\times\pi^{(i)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 123.31871pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-1.99997pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 123.31871pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\rho^{\times k}\times\tau}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-23.46794pt\raise-33.3789pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(I^{k}_{\rho}(\pi))^{(i)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-8.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 72.50877pt\raise-33.3789pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 127.5496pt\raise-33.3789pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{I_{\rho}^{k}(\tau)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 141.67462pt\raise-24.38889pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 141.67462pt\raise-7.44444pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Here the vertical injections are induced from the embeddings in Definition
2.1
.

For simplicity, set
\widetilde{\pi}=I_{\rho}^{k}(\pi)
and
\widetilde{\tau}=I_{\rho}^{k}(\tau)
. Since
\varepsilon^{L}_{\rho}(\pi)=0
, we have

(29.21)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{L}_{\rho}(\widetilde{\pi})=k

(see e.g.
[
Mi09
, Section 6]
). By using Lemma
28.2
, we have that
\varepsilon^{L}_{\rho}(\tau)=0
, and so we also have:

(29.22)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{L}_{\rho}(\widetilde{\tau})=k.

On the other hand, we have a short exact sequence:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\cr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\widetilde{\pi})^{(i)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 82.41269pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 82.41269pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\rho^{\times k}\times\pi)^{(i)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 161.81036pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 161.81036pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Q^{(i)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 209.29523pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 209.29523pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where
Q
is the quotient of the embedding
\widetilde{\pi}\hookrightarrow\pi
. However, any factor
\omega
in
Q
satisfies
Q_{\bar{N}}
cannot be of the form

(*)\quad\rho^{\times k}\boxtimes\omega^{\prime}

for some
\omega^{\prime}
i.e.

\varepsilon^{L}_{\rho}(\omega)<k.

Here
\bar{N}\subset G_{n}
takes all the matrices of the form
\begin{pmatrix}I_{p_{a}}&*\\
&I_{n-p_{a}}\end{pmatrix}
. This implies
(Q_{\bar{N}})^{(i)}=(Q^{(i)})_{N^{\prime}}
, where the first
i
-derivative is taken on the second factor on a
G_{p_{a}}\times G_{n-p_{a}}
-module. Here
N^{\prime}\subset G_{n-i}
takes all the matrices of the form
\begin{pmatrix}I_{p_{a}}&*\\
&I_{n-i-p_{a}}\end{pmatrix}
. Thus we see that

(1)

Q^{(i)}
cannot contain the factor
\widetilde{\tau}

since
\widetilde{\tau}_{N^{\prime}}
contains a factor of the form (*).

One can similarly conclude the following:

(2)

any simple composition factor
\omega
of the cokernel
\widetilde{\tau}\hookrightarrow\rho^{\times k}\times\tau
has
\varepsilon_{\rho}^{L}(\omega)<k
.

On the other hand, we have:

(3)

any simple quotient
\omega
of
\widetilde{\pi}^{(i)}
has
\varepsilon_{\rho}^{L}(\omega)=k
by Lemmas
29.2
below and
28.2
; and

(4)

similarly,
\varepsilon_{\rho}^{L}(\widetilde{\tau})=k
by (
29.22
).

Let
\mathrm{pr}
be the composition of horizontal maps in the toppest diagram in this proof. By (1) and (4), the image of
\mathrm{pr}
for
\widetilde{\pi}^{(i)}
contains the factor
\widetilde{\kappa}
and hence is non-zero. Now, (2) and (3) imply that some simple quotient of
\widetilde{\pi}^{(i)}
must be mapped to
\widetilde{\kappa}
under
\mathrm{pr}
.

∎

We also later need the following result, which follows e.g. by an application of a result of Mínguez
[
Mi09
, Théoème 7.5]
(also see Jantzen
[
Ja07
]
):

Lemma 29.2
.

We use the notations in Lemma
29.1
. Let
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}
be the multisegment associated to
I_{\rho}^{k}(\pi)
. Then there is no segment
\widetilde{\Delta}
in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}
such that
a(\widetilde{\Delta})\cong\nu^{-1}\rho
i.e.
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{a=\nu^{-1}\rho}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

For a segment
\widetilde{\Delta}=[a,b]_{\rho}
, define
{}^{+}\widetilde{\Delta}=[a-1,b]_{\rho}
. Since
\mathfrak{m}_{a=\rho}=\emptyset
, the multisegment for
I_{\rho}^{k}(\pi)
is obtained by replacing each segment
\widetilde{\Delta}
in
\mathfrak{m}_{a=\nu^{-1}\rho}
with
{}^{+}\widetilde{\Delta}
. As a result, there is no segment
\widetilde{\Delta}
of the form
a(\widetilde{\Delta})\cong\nu^{-1}\rho
.
∎

29.2.
Deformation of derivatives

Theorem 29.3
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n})
be thickened.

(1)

For any irreducible submodule
\tau
of
\pi^{(i)}
,
\tau^{-}
is also an irreducible submodule of
(\pi^{-})^{(i)}
. Moreover,
\mathrm{lev}(\tau)=\mathrm{lev}(\pi)
.

(2)

For any irreducible submodule
\tau^{\prime}
of
(\pi^{-})^{(i)}
, there is an irreducible submodule
\tau
of
\pi^{(i)}
such that
\tau^{-}\cong\tau^{\prime}
.

Remark 29.4
.

(1)

The condition of thickedness guarantees that the map from isomorphism classes of irreducible submodules of
\pi^{(i)}
to isomorphism classes of irreducible submodules of
(\pi^{-})^{(i)}
given by
\tau\mapsto\tau^{-}
is an injection. This is not true if we drop the condition.

(2)

One also compares with Proposition
17.2
, which uses derivatives instead of BZ derivatives.

Proof.

We first prove (1). Let
\tau
be an irreducible submodule of
\pi^{(i)}
. It follows from Lemma
28.2
that

\mathrm{lev}(\tau)=\mathrm{lev}(\pi).

Then

\tau^{[-]}\cong{}^{[i^{*}]}\tau\hookrightarrow{}^{[i^{*}]}(\pi^{(i)})\cong({}^{[i^{*}]}\pi)^{(i)}=(\pi^{[-]})^{(i)},

where the commutativity between left and right derivatives follows from taking Jacquet functors in stages, see e.g.
[
Of20
, Lemma 5.8]
for more details.

We now consider (2). We shall prove a quotient version of the statement. Set
\pi^{\prime}={}^{-}\pi
. Let
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
be the multisegment such that
\pi^{\prime}\cong\langle\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}\rangle
.

Let
\rho_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\rho_{r}^{\prime}
be all the cuspidal representations appearing in
\left\{a(\Delta):\Delta\in\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}\right\}
. We shall arrange the cuspidal representations such that

\rho_{i}^{\prime}\not>\rho_{j}^{\prime}

for any
i<j
. Let
\rho_{j}=\nu^{-1}\cdot\rho_{j}^{\prime}
. Let

k_{j}=|\left\{\Delta\in\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}:a(\Delta)\cong\rho_{j}^{\prime}\right\}|.

Let
\tau^{\prime}
be a simple quotient of
\pi^{(i)}
for some
i
. Then, by Lemma
29.1
,
I_{\rho_{1}}^{k_{1}}(\tau^{\prime})
is a simple quotient of
(I_{\rho_{1}}^{k_{1}}(\pi^{\prime}))^{(i)}
. But now using Lemma
29.2
, one can apply Lemma
29.1
again for
I_{\rho_{2}}^{k_{2}}
. Repeatedly, we have that
(I_{\rho_{r}}^{k_{r}}\circ\ldots\circ I_{\rho_{1}}^{k_{1}})(\pi^{\prime})^{(i)}
has a simple quotient isomorphic to
I_{\rho_{r}}^{k_{r}}\circ\ldots I_{\rho_{1}}^{k_{1}}(\tau^{\prime})
. Note that

(I_{\rho_{r}}^{k_{r}}\circ\ldots\circ I_{\rho_{1}}^{k_{1}})(\pi^{\prime})\cong\pi,\quad{}^{-}(I_{\rho_{r}}^{k_{r}}\circ\ldots I_{\rho_{1}}^{k_{1}}(\tau^{\prime}))\cong\tau^{\prime},

where the first isomorphism follows from again
[
Mi09
, Théorème 7.5]
and
[
Ze80
, Theorem 8.1]
and the second isomorphism follows similarly with the additional Lemma
28.2
. Now shifting by
\nu^{-1}
, we then obtain (2).

∎

Remark 29.5
.

Even if
\pi
is thickened, it is in general not true that a composition factor of
\pi^{(i)}
has the same level as
\pi
(c.f. Remark
29.4
). For example, let
\mathfrak{m}=\left\{[0,1],[0,1],[2,3]\right\}
and let
\pi=\langle\mathfrak{m}\rangle
. In this case,
\pi=\langle[0,1]\rangle\times\langle\left\{[0,1],[2,3]\right\}\rangle
, which is irreducible. Then
\pi^{(1)}
admits a filtration with two successive quotients
\langle[0]\rangle\times\langle\left\{[0,1],[2,3]\right\}\rangle
and
\langle[0,1]\rangle\times\langle[0]\rangle\times\langle[2,3]\rangle
. The second one has a composition factor
\langle[0,3]\rangle\times\langle[0]\rangle
, which has level
2
.

Corollary 29.6
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Let
\tau
be a simple quotient of
\pi^{(i)}
for some
i
. Let
\pi^{\prime}
be the irreducible representation such that
{}^{-}\pi^{\prime}\cong\pi
and
\mathrm{lev}(\pi)=\mathrm{lev}(\pi^{\prime})
. Let
\mathfrak{h}
be the highest left derivative multisegment of
\pi
. Then there is a unique representation
\tau^{\prime}
such that
D^{L}_{\mathfrak{h}}(\tau^{\prime})=\tau
. For such
\tau^{\prime}
, it satisfies
\mathrm{lev}(\tau^{\prime})=\mathrm{lev}(\pi)
.

Proof.

By Theorem
29.3
(2), there exists a simple quotient
\tau^{\prime}
of
\pi^{\prime}{}^{(i)}
such that
{}^{-}\tau^{\prime}\cong\tau
.

We then have that

\pi^{\prime}{}^{(i)}\twoheadrightarrow\tau^{\prime}.

With a similar proof to Lemma
8.6
, we have that for all segments
\Delta
,

\eta^{L}_{\Delta}(\tau^{\prime})\leq\eta^{L}_{\Delta}(\pi^{\prime}).

But the inclusion has to be an equality since
\mathrm{lev}(\tau^{\prime})=\mathrm{lev}(\pi^{\prime})
(by using
\pi^{\prime}
is thickened and Lemma
28.2
). Hence we have
\mathfrak{hd}^{L}(\tau^{\prime})=\mathfrak{hd}^{L}(\pi)
(also see
[
Ch22+d
, Corollary 8.6]
) and so
D_{\mathfrak{h}}^{L}(\tau^{\prime})\cong{}^{-}\tau^{\prime}\cong\tau
. The uniqueness follows from the uniqueness of the operators
I_{\Delta}
, and
\mathrm{lev}(\tau^{\prime})=\mathrm{lev}(\pi)
follows from
\mathrm{lev}(\tau^{\prime})=\mathrm{lev}(\pi^{\prime})
. (The last equality on levels follows from Lemma
28.2
.)
∎

30.
Exhaustion and relevance conditions

30.1.
Exhaustion condition

We first define the exhaustion condition.

(
Exhaustion Condition
)
Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. We say that the
exhaustion condition
holds for
\pi
if for any
i
and for any simple quotient
\tau
of
\pi^{(i)}
, there exists a multisegment
\mathfrak{m}
such that
D_{\mathfrak{m}}(\pi)\cong\tau
.

Lemma 30.1
.

Suppose
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
satisfies the exhaustion condition. For
\sigma\in\mathrm{Irr}^{c}
good to
\pi
, the exhaustion condition also holds for
\sigma\times\pi
.

Proof.

This follows by using Leibniz’s rule.
∎

30.2.
A technical consequence from the exhaustion condition

Lemma 30.2
.

Suppose the exhaustion condition holds for some
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}
. Let
\rho
be a
\leq
-maximal element in
\mathrm{csupp}(\pi)
. Let
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{mxpt}(\pi,\rho)
(see Section
8.3
). Let
\omega=D_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi)
. Let
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathrm{Mult}
be Rd-minimal to
\omega
. Let
i=l_{a}(\mathfrak{n}+\mathfrak{p})
. Then
D_{\mathfrak{n}}(\omega)
is also a simple quotient of
\pi^{(i)}
if and only if
\mathfrak{n}+\mathfrak{p}
is also admissible to
\pi
.

Proof.

For the only if direction, suppose
D_{\mathfrak{n}}(\omega)
is a simple quotient of a BZ derivative of
\pi
. Then the exhaustion condition implies that there exists a multisegment
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
such that
D_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}(\pi)\cong D_{\mathfrak{n}}(\omega)
. We choose
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
to be the minimal one. Then by Lemma
19.3
,
\mathfrak{p}\subset\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
. Now by Lemma
15.6
, we have that
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}-\mathfrak{p}
is minimal to
\omega
and
D_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}-\mathfrak{p}}(\omega)\cong D_{\mathfrak{n}}(\omega)
. By the uniqueness, we have
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}-\mathfrak{p}
and so
\mathfrak{n}+\mathfrak{p}
is admissible to
\pi
.

For the if direction, suppose
\mathfrak{n}+\mathfrak{p}
is admissible. The same argument as above shows that
\mathfrak{n}+\mathfrak{p}
is minimal. Hence, we have that

D_{\mathfrak{n}+\mathfrak{p}}(\pi)\cong D_{\mathfrak{n}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi)=D_{\mathfrak{n}}(\omega)

by Lemma
15.6
. We have shown that
D_{\mathfrak{n}+\mathfrak{p}}(\pi)
is a simple quotient of
\pi^{(i)}
in
[
Ch22+
]
.
∎

30.3.
Relevance condition

We now introduce another condition:

(
Relevance condition
) Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n+1})
and
\pi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n})
with
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\neq 0
. We say that
(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
satisfies the
relevance condition
if
(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
is also a relevant pair.

Proposition 30.3
.

Fix an integer
n
. Suppose the relevance condition holds for all pairs of the form
(\omega\times\sigma,\pi^{\prime})
satisfying

•

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\omega\times\sigma,\pi^{\prime})\neq 0
;

•

\omega\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{m})
for some
m\leq n
;

•

\pi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n})
;

•

\sigma\in\mathrm{Irr}^{c}(G_{n+1-m})
good to
\omega
and
\nu^{-1/2}\pi^{\prime}
.

Then the exhaustion condition holds for all
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n+1})
.

Proof.

Step 1: Reduction to a case in the hypothesis.
Fix an integer
i
. Let
\tau
be a simple quotient of
\pi^{[i]}
. Then we can find
\sigma\in\mathrm{Irr}^{c}
such that

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\tau\times\sigma)\neq 0

and
\tau\times\sigma
is irreducible. Let
j^{*}=\mathrm{lev}(\pi)
. Then
\mathcal{L}_{lBL}(\pi,\tau\times\sigma)=j^{*}
by Theorem
28.1
.

Now let
\rho
be a
\leq
-minimal element in
\mathrm{csupp}(\pi)
. Let
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{mxpt}^{L}(\pi,\rho)
. Let
\omega=D^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\pi)
. Then, we have

\omega\times\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})\twoheadrightarrow\pi.

Thus
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\omega\times\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p}),\tau\times\sigma)\neq 0
. Then one can find
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}^{c}
by a refinement of the left version of Lemma
4.3
(also see Lemma
23.3
) such that

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\omega\times\sigma^{\prime},\tau\times\sigma)\neq 0

and

(30.23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{lBL}(\omega\times\sigma^{\prime},\tau\times\sigma)=j^{*}.

Step 2: Using the relevance condition.
Let
\widetilde{\omega}=\nu^{-1/2}\cdot\omega
. By using the relevance condition and Theorem
18.1
, there exist multisegments
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{n}
such that

D^{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}((\widetilde{\omega}\times\nu^{-1/2}\sigma^{\prime}))\cong D^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\tau\times\sigma)

and they satisfy the strong commutativity condition i.e.
(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{m},\nu^{-1/2}\cdot(\omega\times\sigma^{\prime}))
is a strongly LdRi-commutative triple by Proposition
14.2
.

By the cuspidal condition, we must have that
[\sigma^{\prime}]\in\mathfrak{n}
and
[\sigma]\in\mathfrak{m}
, and so we have:

D^{L}_{\mathfrak{n}-[\nu^{-1/2}\cdot\sigma^{\prime}]}(\widetilde{\omega})\cong D^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}-[\sigma]}(\tau).

Let
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{n}-[\nu^{-1/2}\cdot\sigma^{\prime}]
and let
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{m}-[\sigma]
. By (the left version of) Proposition
21.2
and Corollary
22.7
with (
30.23
), we then have that

(30.24)

\displaystyle l_{a}(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})=j^{*}-l_{a}(\mathfrak{p}).

By Theorem
16.2
, we can assume that
(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime},\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},\omega)
is minimal strongly LdRi-commutative.

Step 3.

Claim:
Let
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{hd}^{L}(\nu^{-1/2}\cdot\pi)
. Then
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{h}-\nu^{-1/2}\cdot\mathfrak{p}
.

Proof of claim:
Suppose not. Then
D^{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\omega})\not\cong{}^{[-]}\pi
(by Lemmas
19.3
,
15.6
and uniquness of minimality). Let
i_{l}^{*}=l_{a}(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
and
i_{r}^{*}=l_{a}(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})
. Then we obtain a map from
{}^{[i_{l}^{*}]}\omega
to
\tau^{(i_{r}^{*})}
factoring through
D^{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}(\omega)
. On the other hand, we have a map from
{}^{[i_{l}^{*}]}\omega
to
\tau^{(i_{r}^{*})}
factoring through
{}^{[-]}\pi
by (
30.24
). This contradicts the multiplicity one of

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n-i^{*}}}({}^{[i_{l}^{*}]}\omega,\tau^{(i_{r}^{*})})

(which comes from the multiplicity one of
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n-j^{*}}}({}^{[j^{*}]}(\omega\times\sigma^{\prime}),(\tau\times\sigma)^{(i_{r}^{*})})
by Corollary
22.6
).

Thus, we must have that
D_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\omega})\cong{}^{[-]}\pi
. By the commutativity result, we have that
\mathfrak{h}-\nu^{-1/2}\cdot\mathfrak{p}
is Ld-minimal to
\widetilde{\omega}
and
D^{L}_{\mathfrak{h}-\nu^{-1/2}\cdot\mathfrak{p}}(\widetilde{\omega})\cong{}^{[-]}\pi
. Hence, uniqueness of minimality implies that
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{h}-\nu^{-1/2}\cdot\mathfrak{p}
. This proves the claim.

Step 4: Show
I^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}
preserves levels.
We now return to the proof. Let
\widetilde{\pi}=\nu^{-1/2}\pi
. The strong LdRi-commutativity of
(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{m},\nu^{-1/2}\cdot(\omega\times\sigma^{\prime}))
now implies the strong LdRi-commutativity of
(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime},\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},\widetilde{\omega})
. Moreover, we also have the strong LdRi-commutativity for
(\nu^{-1/2}\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},\widetilde{\pi})
by Examples
9.2
(1) and (2). Since
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}+\nu^{-1/2}\cdot\mathfrak{p}
is still Rd-minimal to
\pi
(by definition), this implies that
(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},\widetilde{\pi})
is still a strongly LdRi-commutative triple by Corollary
15.8
. Hence, we have

(30.25)

\displaystyle D_{\mathfrak{h}}^{L}\circ I^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\pi})\cong I^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{h}}^{L}(\widetilde{\pi})\cong I^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}^{L}(\widetilde{\omega})\cong\tau,

where the first isomorphism follows from Proposition
9.1
, the second isomorphism follows from Corollary
15.7
, and the third isomorphism follows from cancelling the derivatives from integrals.

Now, the uniqueness in Corollary
29.6
implies that

(30.26)

\displaystyle\mathrm{lev}(I^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}(\nu^{-1/2}\cdot\pi))=\mathrm{lev}(\pi).

Step 5: Complete the proof using double integral.

Now, by Theorem
17.4
with (
30.26
), we have a multisegment
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime}
such that

(I^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime}}\circ I^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\pi}))^{-}\cong\widetilde{\pi}

and
\mathrm{lev}(I^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime}}\circ I^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\pi}))=\mathrm{lev}(\widetilde{\pi})
. Now the level preservation of
I^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}
also implies the strong commutativity of
(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime},I^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\pi}))
. Thus, we have:

\displaystyle I^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime}}(\tau)

\displaystyle\cong I^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{h}}^{L}\circ I^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\pi})

\displaystyle\cong D^{L}_{\mathfrak{h}}(I^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime}}\circ I^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\pi}))

\displaystyle\cong{}^{-}(I^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime}}\circ I^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\pi}))

\displaystyle\cong\nu\cdot(I^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime}}\circ I^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\pi}))^{-}

\displaystyle\cong\nu^{1/2}\cdot\pi,

where the first isomorphism follows from (
30.25
), the second isomorphism follows from Lemma
17.2
(from (
30.26
)) and Proposition
9.1
, and the third isomorphism follows from
\mathrm{lev}(I^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime\prime}}\circ I^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\pi}))=\mathrm{lev}(\widetilde{\pi})=|\mathfrak{h}|
. Taking
D^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}
, we now have
D^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}(\nu^{1/2}\cdot\pi)\cong\tau
, as desired.
∎

Remark 30.4
.

The exhaustion theorem above also has some similarity to the proof of symmetry property in Theorem
18.1
. One may regard that instead of determining the relevance for
(\pi,\tau\times\sigma)
directly, we determine the relevance for
(\tau\times\sigma,\pi)
in the above proof.

31.
Necessity of generalized relevance and exhaustion theorem

31.1.
Exhuastion
\Rightarrow
necessity

Proposition 31.1
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n+1})
and let
\pi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n})
. Suppose the exhaustion condition holds for
\pi
. Moreover, the exhaustion condition holds for any irreducible representations of
G_{k}
with
k\leq n
, particularly
\pi^{\prime}
. If
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\neq 0
and
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})=i^{*}
, then
(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
is
i^{*}
-relevant.

Proof.

Step 1: Induction setup

When the relative rank for
(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
is
0
, it follows from Lemma
26.3
. We now assume that the relative rank for
(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
is non-zero. By Proposition
21.2
and Corollary
22.7
, it suffices to show the relevance (instead of
i^{*}
-relevance).

Let
\rho
be a
\leq
-maximal element in
\mathrm{csupp}(\pi)\cup\mathrm{csupp}(\nu^{-1/2}\cdot\pi^{\prime})
such that
\rho
is relevant to
\pi^{\prime}
if
\rho\in\mathrm{csupp}(\pi)
; or
\rho
is relevant to
\pi
otherwise. (Such
\rho
exists since we are assuming
\mathcal{RR}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})>0
.)

Step 2: First case of induction and reducing to the inductive case by BZ filtration technique

Case 1:
Suppose
\rho\notin\mathrm{csupp}(\nu^{-1/2}\cdot\pi^{\prime})
. Let
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{mxpt}^{R}(\pi,\rho)
. Let
\omega=D_{\mathfrak{p}}^{R}(\pi)
. This gives a surjection:

\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})\times\omega\twoheadrightarrow\pi

with kernel denoted by
\kappa
.

Now, we have

(*)\quad\mathrm{dim}~\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})\times\omega,\pi^{\prime})=1.

Let
i^{*}=\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})\times\omega,\pi^{\prime})
. Thus, we also have:

•

By Corollary
22.6
,

\mathrm{dim}~\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n+1-i^{*}}}((\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})\times\omega)^{[i^{*}]},{}^{(i^{*}-1)}\pi^{\prime})=1.

•

By Lemma
23.3
,

\mathrm{dim}~\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\sigma\times\omega,\pi^{\prime})=1

for some cuspidal representation
\sigma
good to
\nu^{1/2}\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
, and
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\sigma\times\omega,\pi^{\prime})=i^{*}
.

•

Note that
\sigma\times\omega
also satisfies the exhaustion condition by the given hypothesis and Lemma
30.1
.

•

Now, by induction on the relative rank, there exist multisegments
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{n}
such that

(31.27)

\displaystyle D^{R}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\nu^{1/2}\cdot(\sigma\times\omega))\cong D^{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi^{\prime}).

and
l_{a}(\mathfrak{m})=i^{*}
. By a cuspidal condition, we have that
[\sigma]\in\mathfrak{m}
. Let
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{m}-[\sigma]
and
\widetilde{\omega}=\nu^{1/2}\omega
. We shall assume that
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
is minimal to
\widetilde{\omega}
(by Theorem
16.2
).

Step 3: Determining the admissibility of
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}+\nu^{1/2}\mathfrak{p}
by using information from simple quotients of BZ derivatives

Claim:
Let
\widetilde{\pi}=\nu^{1/2}\pi
. We have that
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}+\nu^{1/2}\mathfrak{p}
is admissible to
\widetilde{\pi}
. Moreover,
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}+\nu^{1/2}\mathfrak{p}
is minimal to
\widetilde{\pi}
.

Proof of claim:
For simplicity, let
\lambda=\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{p})\times\omega
. Note
D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\omega})
is a simple quotient of
\lambda^{[i^{*}]}
(by using
\lambda^{[i^{*}]}
has a quotient
\omega^{[i^{*}-n(\sigma)]}
and
l_{a}(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})=i^{*}-n(\sigma)
in the last bullet of Step 2) and we let
q
to be the quotient map. Suppose the first assertion of the claim does not hold. Then, by the exhaustion condition and Lemma
30.2
,
D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\omega})
is not a simple quotient of
\pi^{[i^{*}]}
. Thus,
q
restricted to
\kappa^{[i^{*}]}
is non-zero.

On the other hand, we also have a non-zero map
\pi^{[i^{*}]}
to
{}^{(i^{*}-1)}\pi^{\prime}
by the independence part in Corollary
22.6
. Composing with the quotient map

\lambda^{[i^{*}]}\rightarrow\pi^{[i^{*}]},

we have a map from
\lambda^{[i^{*}]}
to
{}^{(i^{*}-1)}\pi^{\prime}
whose restriction to
\kappa^{[i^{*}]}
is zero. Hence, we arrive that

\mathrm{dim}~\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n+1-i^{*}}}(\lambda^{[i^{*}]},{}^{(i^{*}-1)}\pi^{\prime})\geq 2,

giving a contradiction.

It remains to show that
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}+\nu^{1/2}\mathfrak{p}
is minimal to
\widetilde{\pi}
. This is similar to the argument in Lemma
30.2
by using Lemma
19.3
and the uniqueness of minimality for
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
.

Step 4: Getting back the original case by using commutativity of the minimal sequences

We now return to the proof. The strong commutativity for
(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n},\nu^{1/2}\cdot(\sigma\times\omega))
implies the strong commutativity for
(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime},\mathfrak{n},\nu^{1/2}\cdot\omega)
. Now we also have the strong commutativity for
(\nu^{1/2}\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{n},\widetilde{\pi})
by Examples
9.2
(1) and(2), and Theorem
9.4
. By Step 3, we have
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}+\nu^{1/2}\mathfrak{p}
is also minimal to
\widetilde{\pi}
. Thus, we have that
(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}+\nu^{1/2}\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{n},\widetilde{\pi})
is also a strongly RdLi-commutative triple by Corollary
15.8
. Now,

D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}+\nu^{1/2}\mathfrak{p}}(\widetilde{\pi})\cong D_{\nu^{1/2}\mathfrak{p}}\circ D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\pi})\cong D_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}\circ D_{\nu^{1/2}\mathfrak{p}}(\widetilde{\pi})\cong D^{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\pi^{\prime}),

where the second isomorphism follows from Lemma
15.6
and the last isomorphism follows from (
31.27
). This shows the relevance for
(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
.

Step 5: The second case and using the symmetry of relevance

Case 2:
Suppose
\rho\in\mathrm{csupp}(\pi^{\prime})
. Then
\rho\notin\mathrm{csupp}(\nu^{-1/2}\cdot\pi)
by the maximality. The argument is very similar to Case 1, and so we will sketch some main steps:

•

Let
\mathfrak{q}=\mathfrak{mx}(\pi^{\prime},\rho)
.

\mathrm{dim}~\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,D_{\mathfrak{q}}(\pi^{\prime})\times\mathrm{St}(\mathfrak{q}))=1.

•

By the right version of Lemma
23.3
, there exists a
\sigma\in\mathrm{Irr}^{c}
good to
\nu^{-1/2}\cdot\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
such that

\mathrm{dim}~\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,D_{\mathfrak{q}}(\pi^{\prime})\times\sigma)=1

•

By induction on the relative rank,
(\pi,D_{\mathfrak{q}}(\pi^{\prime})\times\sigma)
is relevant and so is
(\pi,D_{\mathfrak{q}}(\pi^{\prime}))
. Then, with Theorem
18.1
, there exist multisegments
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
such that:

D^{L}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\nu^{-1/2}\cdot\pi)\cong D^{R}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}\circ D^{R}_{\mathfrak{q}}(\pi^{\prime})

and
(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime},\mathfrak{m},D^{R}_{\mathfrak{q}}(\pi^{\prime}))
is a strongly RdLi-commutative triple. We shall choose
\mathfrak{m}
is Li-minimal to
D^{R}_{\mathfrak{q}}(\pi^{\prime})
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
is Rd-minimal to
D^{R}_{\mathfrak{q}}(\pi^{\prime})
.

•

As in the argument of above claim (which uses the exhaustion condition for
\pi^{\prime}
, and Lemma
30.2
, and one considers simple submodules of
\pi^{\prime(i^{*})}
), we have
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}+\mathfrak{q}
is Rd-admissible to
\pi^{\prime}
. By Lemma
19.3
, uniqueness of minimality implies that
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}+\mathfrak{q}
is also Rd-minimal to
\pi^{\prime}
.

•

Now, the relevance of
(\pi^{\prime},\pi)
follows by a commutation using the minimal sequence as in Step 4.

•

This concludes the relevance for
(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
by the symmetry in Theorem
18.1
.

∎

31.2.
Proof of necessity and exhaustion

Theorem 31.2
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n+1})
and let
\pi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n})
. If
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\neq 0
and
\mathcal{L}_{rBL}(\pi,\pi^{\prime})=i^{*}
, then
(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
is
i^{*}
-relevant.

Theorem 31.3
.

The exhaustion condition holds for all irreducible representations of
G_{n}
.

Proof of Theorems
31.2
and
31.3
.
We shall prove by induction on
n
(for
G_{n}
). When
n=0,1
, the statements are clear.

Suppose Theorems
31.2
and
31.3
hold for some
n>0
. Then, for any
\omega\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{m})
(
m\leq n
) and any
\sigma\in\mathrm{Irr}^{c}(G_{n+1-m})
good to
\omega
, Lemma
30.1
implies that the exhaustion condition holds for
\sigma\times\omega
. The assumption of course also implies the exhaustion condition holds for
\pi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n})
. Then, Proposition
31.1
implies that the relevance condition holds for any such pairs
(\sigma\times\omega,\pi^{\prime})
.

The above verifies conditions in Proposition
30.3
and so we have Theorem
31.3
holds for
n+1
. This, with Proposition
31.1
again, in turn gives that Theorem
31.2
holds for
n+1
. ∎

Part VI
Appendices

32.
Appendix A: Specialization map under Bernstein-Zelevinsky filtrations

32.1.
Some adjointness

We use the notations in Section
3
. We have the following adjointness:

\mathrm{Hom}_{M_{n}}(\Phi^{+}(\pi),\tau)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{M_{n-1}}(\pi,\Phi^{-}(\tau)),

Given a map
\Phi^{+}(\pi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\rightarrow}}\tau
, the adjunction map is given by
[
BZ76
, Proposition 5.12]
(and its proof):

\pi\cong\Phi^{-}\circ\Phi^{+}(\pi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi^{-}(f)}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Phi^{-}(\tau)

On the other hand, given a map
\pi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Phi^{-}(\tau)
, the adjunction map is given by:

\Phi^{+}(\pi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi^{+}(h)}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Phi^{+}\circ\Phi^{-}(\tau)\hookrightarrow\tau.

Moreover, the embedding
\Phi^{+}\circ\Phi^{-}(\tau)
to
\tau
is adjoint to the identity morphism from
\Phi^{-}(\tau)
to
\Phi^{-}(\tau)
.

32.2.
Deformation of representations

Let
P
be a standard parabolic subgroup of
G_{n}
with Levi decomposition
LN
. Identify
L
with
G_{n_{1}}\times\ldots\times G_{n_{r}}
via
\mathrm{diag}(g_{1},\ldots,g_{r})\mapsto(g_{1},\ldots,g_{r})
. Let
\sigma\in\mathrm{Alg}(L)
. For indeterminates
u_{1},\ldots,u_{r}
, define
\pi_{u}=\mathrm{Ind}_{P}^{G_{n}}\sigma
to be the space of functions from
G_{n}
to
\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm u_{1}},\ldots,q^{\pm u_{r}}]
satisfying:

f(pg)=\nu(g_{1})^{u_{1}}\ldots\nu(g_{r})^{u_{r}}p.f(g),

where
p=\mathrm{diag}(g_{1},\ldots,g_{r})v
with
g_{i}\in G_{n_{i}}
and
v
in the unipotent radical. Let
u^{*}=(u_{1}^{*},\ldots,u_{r}^{*})\in\mathbb{C}^{r}
. Define
\mathrm{sp}:\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm u_{1}},\ldots,q^{\pm u_{r}}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
by evaluating
u_{i}=u_{i}^{*}
for all
i
. This induces a
G_{n}
-representation map, denoted by
\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}
, from
\pi_{u}
to
\pi_{u^{*}}
given by:

\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}(f)(g)=\mathrm{sp}\circ f(g).

Lemma 32.1
.

We use the notations above. Then
\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}
is surjective.

Proof.

See e.g.
[
CPS17
, Section 3.1]
. Indeed, let
K_{0}=\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{O})
and we have
G_{n}=PK_{0}
. Then, for each
f\in\pi_{u}
,
\widetilde{f}
is determined by its values
\widetilde{f}(k)
for
k\in K_{0}
. This similarly holds for
f\in\pi_{u^{*}}
. Thus, for any
f\in\pi_{u}
, one defines
\widetilde{f}\in\pi_{u}
such that
\widetilde{f}(k)=f(k)
. One checks it is well-defined from definitions and then
\mathrm{sp}(\widetilde{f})=f
. This shows surjectivity.
∎

32.3.
Specialization for Bernstein-Zelevinsky layers

Lemma 32.2
.

Let
\tau_{i}=(\Phi^{-})^{i}(\pi_{u})
. Similarly, let
\tau^{*}_{i}=(\Phi^{-})^{i}(\pi_{u^{*}})
. Let
s_{i}=(\Phi^{-})^{i}(\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}})
and let
s_{i+1}=(\Phi^{-})^{i+1}(\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}})
, where
\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}
is the specialization map defined in the previous section. Then the following diagram commutes:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 22.7324pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-22.7324pt\raise 22.62863pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\iota}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 47.36638pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-16.20335pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.25891pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\Phi^{+}(s_{i+1})}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 54.21678pt\raise-16.20335pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{s_{i}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 54.21678pt\raise-25.07639pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-22.7324pt\raise-32.40671pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Phi^{+}(\tau_{i+1}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 20.53833pt\raise-26.73462pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.67209pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\iota^{*}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.7324pt\raise-32.40671pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 46.7324pt\raise-32.40671pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\tau_{i}^{*}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where
\iota
(resp.
\iota^{*}
) is the adjunction map to the identity morphism from
\tau_{i+1}=\Phi^{-}(\tau_{i})
to
\Phi^{-}(\tau_{i})
(resp. from
\tau_{i+1}^{*}=\Phi^{-}(\tau_{i}^{*})
to
\Phi^{-}(\tau_{i}^{*})
).

Proof.

We first have:

\Phi^{-}(s_{i}\circ\iota)=\Phi^{-}(s_{i})\circ\Phi^{-}(\iota)=\Phi^{-}(s_{i})\circ\mathrm{id}=s_{i+1},

where the first equation follows from the functoriality of
\Phi^{-}
, the second equation follows from
\Phi^{-}(\iota)=\mathrm{id}
and the third one follows from the definitions of
s_{i}
and
s_{i+1}
.

Similarly, we have

\Phi^{-}(\iota^{*}\circ\Phi^{+}(s_{i+1}))=\Phi^{-}(\iota^{*})\circ(\Phi^{-}\circ\Phi^{+})(s_{i+1})=\mathrm{Id}\circ s_{i+1}=s_{i+1}.

Thus, taking back the adjointness, we have the commutative diagram.

∎

Proposition 32.3
.

Denote the Bernstein-Zelevinsky filtration for
\pi_{u}
by

0\subset\lambda_{n}\subset\ldots\subset\lambda_{1}\subset\lambda_{0}=\pi_{u}

and the Bernstein-Zelevinsky filtration for
\pi_{u^{*}}
by

0\subset\lambda_{n}^{*}\subset\ldots\subset\lambda_{1}^{*}\subset\lambda_{0}^{*}=\pi_{u^{*}}.

Here
\lambda_{i}
is the submodule given by the natural embedding from
\Lambda_{i}(\pi_{u})
to
\pi_{u}
, and
\lambda_{i}^{*}
is the submodule given by the natural embedding from
\Lambda_{i}(\pi_{u^{*}})
to
\pi_{u^{*}}
. Then
\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}(\lambda_{i})=\lambda_{i}^{*}
.

Proof.

We fix a
i
. Recall that we have the identification:

(\Phi^{+})^{i}\circ(\Phi^{-})^{i}(\pi_{u})\cong\lambda_{i},\quad(\Phi^{+})^{i}\circ(\Phi^{-})^{i}(\pi_{u^{*}})\cong\lambda_{i}^{*}

Let
t_{i}=(\Phi^{+})^{i}\circ(\Phi^{-})^{i}(\mathrm{sp}_{u^{*}})
. By Lemma
32.2
, we inductively have the following commutative diagram:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 41.30045pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-39.21016pt\raise 0.0pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 65.30045pt\raise-32.76083pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 65.30045pt\raise-32.76083pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\lambda_{i}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 105.73102pt\raise-32.76083pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 105.73102pt\raise-32.76083pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\lambda_{0}^{*}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where the two horizontal maps are the above isomorphisms. Now, note that
t_{i}
is surjective by Lemma
32.1
and the exactness of the functors. Thus
\widetilde{\mathrm{sp}}
on
\lambda_{i}
is also surjective by using the above commutative diagram.
∎

Remark 32.4
.

Instead of specializing all the parameters
u_{1},\ldots,u_{r}
at once, one can also specialize the parameters step-by-step. The above result still holds.

33.
Appendix B: A short proof for the asymmetry property of left-right BZ derivatives

With the development of new tools, we give a shorter and more conceptual proof for a result proved in
[
Ch21
]
. This also illustrates some powerfulness of those new techniques.

Theorem 33.1
.

[
Ch21
]

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}(G_{n+1})
. Let
i^{*}=\mathrm{lev}(\pi)
. For
i<i^{*}
with
\pi^{[i]}\neq 0
and
{}^{[i]}\pi\neq 0
,

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n+1-i}}(\mathrm{cosoc}(\pi^{[i]}),\mathrm{cosoc}({}^{[i]}\pi))=0.

Here
\mathrm{cosoc}(\pi^{[i]})
and
\mathrm{cosoc}({}^{[i]}\pi)
denote the cosocles of
\pi^{[i]}
and
{}^{[i]}\pi
respectively.

Proof.

Let
\tau
be a simple quotient of
\pi^{[i]}
for some
i<i^{*}
. Then, by Theorem
28.1
, we can choose a cuspidal representation
\sigma
of
G_{i-1}
good to
\nu^{1/2}\pi
and
\tau
so that
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n}}(\pi,\tau\times\sigma)\neq 0
and
\mathcal{L}_{lBL}(\pi,\tau\times\sigma)=i^{*}
. By Corollary
22.7
,
\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n+1-i}}({}^{[i]}\pi,(\tau\times\sigma)^{(i-1)})=0
. A cuspidal condition constraint then also gives that

\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{n+1-i}}({}^{[i]}\pi,\tau)=0.

∎
